# Short biographies shown on author pages: name|bio|more (a fuller life, shown under "More about their life") (written for the app)
Lao Tzu|Legendary Chinese sage of the 6th century BC (his dates and even existence are uncertain), traditionally the author of the Tao Te Ching, the founding text of Daoism.|According to the historian Sima Qian, Lao Tzu ("Old Master") was a keeper of archives at the Zhou court. Weary of the kingdom's decline, he rode west on a water buffalo, and at the border pass a guard begged him to write down his wisdom. The result was the Tao Te Ching, about 5,000 characters in 81 short chapters. Scholars now think the book grew over time from several hands. Its teaching of the Tao, the nameless way of things, and of wu wei, acting without forcing, shaped Chinese religion, art, medicine and politics. It is one of the most translated books in the world. Start with chapters 8 (water), 11 (the usefulness of emptiness) and 33.
Ralph Waldo Emerson|American essayist and poet (1803–1882), leader of Transcendentalism. His essays, such as "Self-Reliance" and "Nature", urged trust in one's own intuition and in the divine in nature.|Emerson was born in Boston, the son of a minister, and became a Unitarian minister himself. After his young wife Ellen died of tuberculosis, he resigned his pulpit, travelled to Europe and met Wordsworth, Coleridge and Carlyle. Back in Concord, Massachusetts, he wrote "Nature" (1836) and gave the "American Scholar" address, which Oliver Wendell Holmes called America's intellectual declaration of independence. He lectured across the country for decades, mentored Thoreau and supported Walt Whitman early on. He also lost his beloved son Waldo at five. Read "Self-Reliance", "The Over-Soul" and "Experience", his essay of grief.
Benjamin Franklin|American printer, writer, scientist and statesman (1706–1790). His Poor Richard's Almanack and Autobiography made thrift, industry and self-improvement into American proverbs.|The fifteenth child of a Boston candle-maker, Franklin ran away at 17 to Philadelphia and became a printer. His "Poor Richard's Almanack" made him famous for sayings about thrift and work. He retired at 42 to pursue science, proving that lightning is electricity with his kite experiment and inventing the lightning rod, bifocals and an efficient stove. He founded a library, a fire company and a university, helped draft the Declaration of Independence, won French support during the American Revolution and later worked against slavery. His Autobiography, with its plan for practising thirteen virtues, is still widely read.
Aristotle|Greek philosopher (384–322 BC), pupil of Plato and tutor of Alexander the Great. He wrote on nearly everything, from ethics and politics to biology and poetry, and shaped Western thought for two thousand years.|Born in Stagira in northern Greece, the son of a doctor, Aristotle studied for twenty years at Plato's Academy in Athens. He later tutored the young Alexander the Great, then founded his own school, the Lyceum, where he taught while walking. He wrote on almost everything: logic, physics, biology, ethics, politics, poetry and the soul. He was the first great biologist, dissecting and classifying hundreds of animals. His ethics centres on eudaimonia, flourishing, achieved through virtues formed by habit. Through Islamic and Christian scholars, his work dominated learning for 2,000 years. Start with the Nicomachean Ethics.
Helen Keller|American author and activist (1880–1968). Deaf and blind from infancy, she learned to read, write and speak with her teacher Anne Sullivan and became a world-famous voice for optimism and social justice.|An illness at 19 months left Helen Keller deaf and blind. At almost seven, her teacher Anne Sullivan arrived and, at a water pump, spelled "w-a-t-e-r" into her hand; Keller later wrote that the living word awakened her soul. She learned to read Braille, to speak and to write, and became the first deaf-blind person to earn a bachelor's degree, from Radcliffe College in 1904. She wrote twelve books, travelled to over 35 countries and campaigned for disabled people, women's suffrage, workers' rights and peace. Read "The Story of My Life" (1903).
Eleanor Roosevelt|American diplomat and activist (1884–1962), First Lady from 1933 to 1945, who chaired the drafting of the Universal Declaration of Human Rights.|Orphaned by ten and shy as a girl, Eleanor Roosevelt found her confidence at a girls' school in England. She married her distant cousin Franklin, and after he was struck by polio in 1921 she became his eyes and ears, travelling widely. As First Lady from 1933 to 1945 she held press conferences for women reporters, wrote a daily newspaper column and championed civil rights and the poor. After his death she chaired the UN commission that drafted the Universal Declaration of Human Rights (1948). Her book "You Learn by Living" collects her practical wisdom.
Squire Bill Widener|A Virginia countryman whose saying "Do what you can, with what you've got, where you are" Theodore Roosevelt quoted in his Autobiography.|Bill Widener was a man from Virginia credited with the saying "Do what you can, with what you have, where you are." Theodore Roosevelt quoted it in his autobiography (1913) as the best summing-up of practical duty he knew, attributing it to Widener of Widener's Valley. Little else is known about him. He is a reminder that wisdom often comes from ordinary people whose names survive only because someone famous remembered them.
Theodore Roosevelt|26th President of the United States (1858–1919), naturalist, soldier and prolific writer, who preached "the strenuous life" of effort and courage.|A sickly, asthmatic child in New York, Roosevelt built up his body through boxing, hiking and ranching in the Dakota Badlands, where he went after his mother and wife died on the same day in 1884. He led the "Rough Riders" in the Spanish-American War, became governor of New York and, at 42, the youngest US president after McKinley's assassination. He broke up monopolies, protected about 230 million acres of wild land and won the Nobel Peace Prize for ending the Russo-Japanese War. He wrote over 35 books. His "Man in the Arena" speech (1910) is still widely quoted.
Virgil|Roman poet (70–19 BC), author of the Aeneid, the epic of Rome's founding, and of the pastoral Eclogues and Georgics. Dante chose him as his guide through the underworld.|Publius Vergilius Maro was born near Mantua to a farming family. His "Eclogues", pastoral poems, and "Georgics", a poem about farming, won him the patronage of Maecenas and the emperor Augustus. He spent his last eleven years on the "Aeneid", the epic of Aeneas, a Trojan refugee who founds the Roman people at great personal cost. Virgil died before finishing it and asked that it be burned; Augustus refused. It became the central text of Latin education, and Dante chose Virgil as his guide through Hell. His phrase "lacrimae rerum", the tears of things, still names a sorrow at the heart of life.
Samuel Johnson|English writer and critic (1709–1784), compiler of the great Dictionary of the English Language, essayist of The Rambler, and subject of Boswell's famous Life.|Johnson grew up poor in Lichfield, the son of a bookseller, scarred by scrofula and troubled by tics and depression. He left Oxford without a degree for lack of money and walked to London to write for a living. Over nine years, with only six assistants, he wrote "A Dictionary of the English Language" (1755), the most influential English dictionary before the Oxford. He also wrote the "Rambler" essays, the novel "Rasselas" and "Lives of the Poets". His friend James Boswell's "Life of Samuel Johnson" made his conversation, wit and kindness immortal.
Voltaire|French writer and philosopher (1694–1778) of the Enlightenment, champion of tolerance and free thought, best known for the satire Candide.|François-Marie Arouet took the pen name Voltaire after a spell in the Bastille for mocking the regent. Exiled to England, he admired its tolerance, Newton and Locke, and popularised them in France. He wrote plays, poems, histories and the satirical tale "Candide" (1759), which mocks the idea that this is the best of all possible worlds. From his estate near the Swiss border he campaigned against religious persecution and judicial cruelty, notably clearing the name of Jean Calas, a Protestant wrongly executed. His motto was "Écrasez l'infâme", crush the infamous thing, meaning fanaticism.
Frederick Douglass|American writer, orator and statesman (c. 1818–1895). He escaped slavery and became the most powerful voice of the abolitionist movement.|Born into slavery in Maryland, Douglass secretly learned to read, which he called the pathway from slavery to freedom. At 20 he escaped to the North disguised as a sailor. His "Narrative of the Life of Frederick Douglass" (1845) made him famous; to avoid recapture he lectured in Britain until friends bought his freedom. He published the newspaper "The North Star", supported women's rights at Seneca Falls, advised Lincoln and recruited Black soldiers for the Union. He was the most photographed American of the 19th century, deliberately presenting a dignified image to counter racist caricatures.
Louis Pasteur|French chemist and microbiologist (1822–1895) who showed that germs cause disease and fermentation, and developed vaccines and pasteurisation.|The son of a tanner, Pasteur began as a chemist, discovering that some molecules come in mirror-image forms. Studying wine and beer, he showed that fermentation and spoilage are caused by microbes, and that heating can kill them, the process now called pasteurisation. His experiments with swan-necked flasks helped disprove spontaneous generation. He developed vaccines against chicken cholera, anthrax and rabies, saving a boy bitten by a rabid dog in 1885. Three of his five children died of typhoid, which deepened his commitment to fighting disease. The Pasteur Institute in Paris carries on his work.
Henry Wadsworth Longfellow|American poet (1807–1882), the most popular poet of his age, author of "A Psalm of Life", Evangeline and The Song of Hiawatha.|Longfellow grew up in Portland, Maine, and became a professor of modern languages at Harvard after years of study in Europe. His first wife died after a miscarriage, and his second, Fanny, died after her dress caught fire; he was badly burned trying to save her, and grew his famous beard to hide the scars. He was the most popular American poet of his century, writing "Evangeline", "The Song of Hiawatha" and "Paul Revere's Ride". He also made the first American translation of Dante's "Divine Comedy". His "A Psalm of Life" urges action and courage.
Robert Frost|American poet (1874–1963) of rural New England, whose plain-spoken poems such as "The Road Not Taken" and "Stopping by Woods on a Snowy Evening" hide deep uncertainty.|Born in San Francisco, Frost moved to New England at eleven after his father's death. He farmed in New Hampshire with little success while writing poems, then moved to England in 1912, where his first books were published and he befriended Edward Thomas. Returning to America, he became its most famous poet, winning four Pulitzer Prizes. His life held great sorrow: he outlived four of his six children. Behind the plain New England voice, his poems are often dark and subtle. He read "The Gift Outright" at John F. Kennedy's inauguration. Read "Stopping by Woods", "Birches" and "Home Burial".
Hillel the Elder|Jewish sage and scholar in Jerusalem (c. 110 BC–10 AD), remembered for his gentleness and for summing up the Torah as "What is hateful to you, do not do to your neighbour."|Hillel was born in Babylon and came to Jerusalem as a poor student; legend says he once nearly froze on a rooftop listening through the skylight to a lesson he couldn't afford. He became one of the most important sages of the Second Temple period and founded the House of Hillel, known for its gentle, generous interpretations of law. Asked by a man to teach him the whole Torah while standing on one foot, he replied: "What is hateful to you, do not do to your neighbour. That is the whole Torah; the rest is commentary. Go and learn it." His sayings are collected in Pirkei Avot.
Mark Twain|American writer and humorist (1835–1910), author of Tom Sawyer and Huckleberry Finn, whose wit hid a sharp eye for human folly.|Samuel Clemens grew up in Hannibal, Missouri, on the Mississippi River, which shaped his life and books. He worked as a printer, a steamboat pilot (his pen name is a river call meaning two fathoms deep), a silver miner and a journalist. "The Adventures of Tom Sawyer" and "Adventures of Huckleberry Finn" made him famous; Hemingway said all modern American literature comes from the latter. He lectured worldwide, lost a fortune on bad investments and paid back every debt. Grief over the deaths of his wife and two daughters darkened his later writing. He was born and died with Halley's Comet.
Franklin D. Roosevelt|32nd President of the United States (1882–1945), who led the country through the Great Depression and the Second World War.|Born to wealth in Hyde Park, New York, Franklin Roosevelt was struck by polio at 39 and never walked unaided again. He became governor of New York and then, in 1932, president during the Great Depression, telling Americans that "the only thing we have to fear is fear itself". His New Deal created Social Security, jobs programmes and banking reforms. His radio "fireside chats" spoke to the nation like a neighbour. He led the country through most of the Second World War and was elected four times, more than any other president, dying in office in April 1945.
James Allen|British writer (1864–1912) whose little book As a Man Thinketh (1903) taught that our thoughts shape our character and our lives.|James Allen was born in Leicester, England. His father went to America seeking work and was murdered there, and James left school at 15 to support his family. He worked as a secretary while reading widely in Tolstoy, Buddhism and the Bible. In 1902 he wrote "As a Man Thinketh", a short book arguing that our thoughts shape our character and circumstances. He then retired with his wife Lily to Ilfracombe in Devon to live simply and write. He wrote some twenty books before dying at 47. His work deeply influenced the self-help tradition.
Samuel Smiles|Scottish writer and reformer (1812–1904) whose Self-Help (1859), full of stories of perseverance, became one of the best-selling books of the Victorian age.|Samuel Smiles was one of eleven children of a Scottish shopkeeper. He trained as a doctor, then became a newspaper editor and campaigner for political reform. Giving talks to working men in Leeds, he collected stories of people who had risen through effort, and published them as "Self-Help" (1859), which sold a quarter of a million copies in his lifetime and was translated into many languages, including Japanese, where it was a great success. He also wrote biographies of engineers. His later books "Character", "Thrift" and "Duty" continued the theme that steady work and integrity build a good life.
Booker T. Washington|American educator and writer (1856–1915), born into slavery, who founded the Tuskegee Institute and wrote the autobiography Up from Slavery.|Born into slavery in Virginia, Washington was nine when emancipation came. He worked in salt furnaces and coal mines, then walked hundreds of miles to attend the Hampton Institute, where he worked as a janitor to pay his way. In 1881, at 25, he became the first leader of the Tuskegee Institute in Alabama, which grew into a major Black college; its students built many of its buildings themselves. His autobiography "Up from Slavery" (1901) was a bestseller. His emphasis on education and economic progress, while accepting segregation for a time, was challenged by W. E. B. Du Bois.
William Ernest Henley|English poet and editor (1849–1903). He wrote "Invictus" in hospital after losing a leg to tuberculosis.|Henley contracted tuberculosis of the bone as a boy and had one leg amputated below the knee. Doctors said the other must go too, but he travelled to Edinburgh to be treated by Joseph Lister, who saved it. During nearly two years in hospital he wrote poems, including "Invictus", which ends "I am the master of my fate: I am the captain of my soul." He became an influential editor and a close friend of Robert Louis Stevenson, who based Long John Silver partly on him. His young daughter Margaret, who inspired the name "Wendy" in "Peter Pan", died at five.
Alfred, Lord Tennyson|English poet (1809–1892), Poet Laureate for over forty years, author of In Memoriam, "Ulysses" and "Crossing the Bar".|Tennyson grew up in a Lincolnshire rectory, one of twelve children, in a family troubled by his father's depression and alcoholism. At Cambridge he formed a deep friendship with Arthur Hallam, whose sudden death at 22 devastated him. Over seventeen years he wrote "In Memoriam A.H.H." (1850), a long sequence of grief, doubt and recovering faith, containing "'Tis better to have loved and lost than never to have loved at all." The same year he became Poet Laureate, a post he held for 42 years. He also wrote "Ulysses", "The Lady of Shalott" and "The Charge of the Light Brigade".
Socrates|Athenian philosopher (c. 470–399 BC) who wrote nothing but questioned everyone. Sentenced to death for corrupting the young, he is known to us through Plato's dialogues.|Socrates was a stonemason's son and a soldier who fought bravely in Athens' wars. He wrote nothing; we know him mostly through his students Plato and Xenophon, and through Aristophanes, who mocked him in a comedy. He spent his days questioning people in the marketplace about justice, courage and virtue, exposing false certainty. In 399 BC he was tried for impiety and corrupting the young, and sentenced to death. He refused to flee and drank hemlock calmly among his friends. His method of questioning, and his insistence that the unexamined life is not worth living, founded Western philosophy. Read Plato's "Apology" and "Phaedo".
Heraclitus|Greek philosopher of Ephesus (c. 535–475 BC), known as "the Obscure", who taught that everything flows and that opposites are bound together. Only fragments of his book survive.|Heraclitus came from an aristocratic family in Ephesus, on the coast of modern Turkey, and is said to have given up his inherited role to his brother. He wrote a single book, now lost; about 130 fragments survive in quotations by later writers. His style was so compressed and paradoxical that he was nicknamed "the Obscure". He taught that everything is in flux, that opposites are bound together, and that a hidden rational order, the logos, governs all change. Fire was his symbol of constant transformation. He influenced the Stoics, Hegel and Nietzsche.
Albert Einstein|German-born physicist (1879–1955) whose theories of relativity changed our understanding of space, time and gravity. He also wrote widely on peace, religion and freedom.|Born in Ulm, Germany, Einstein was a slow talker as a child who was fascinated by a compass at five. He failed to get an academic job after university and worked as a patent clerk in Bern, where in 1905 he published four papers that transformed physics, on light quanta, Brownian motion, special relativity and E = mc². His general theory of relativity (1915) explained gravity as curved spacetime. He won the Nobel Prize in 1921. Of Jewish background, he left Germany when the Nazis came to power and settled in Princeton. He was a pacifist, a violinist and a campaigner for civil rights.
Friedrich Nietzsche|German philosopher (1844–1900) who declared the death of God and asked how we might create new values. His books include Thus Spake Zarathustra and Beyond Good and Evil.|The son of a Lutheran pastor who died when he was four, Nietzsche became a professor of classical philology in Basel at just 24. Ill health forced him to retire at 34, and for ten years he wandered between Swiss and Italian boarding houses, writing book after book: "The Gay Science", "Thus Spoke Zarathustra", "Beyond Good and Evil", "On the Genealogy of Morals". In 1889 he collapsed in Turin, reportedly after embracing a flogged horse, and never recovered his mind. His sister later distorted his work to serve nationalism. His ideas of the death of God, the will to power and amor fati shaped modern thought.
Søren Kierkegaard|Danish philosopher and theologian (1813–1855), often called the father of existentialism. He wrote about anxiety, despair, faith and the single individual.|Kierkegaard was the youngest of seven children of a wealthy, deeply religious Copenhagen merchant. Five of his siblings and his mother died before he turned 21. He broke off his engagement to Regine Olsen, whom he loved all his life, believing he could not make her happy. He then wrote a torrent of books, many under pseudonyms, including "Either/Or", "Fear and Trembling" and "The Sickness unto Death", exploring choice, anxiety, despair and faith. He attacked the comfortable Danish church and died at 42. He is called the father of existentialism.
William Shakespeare|English playwright and poet (1564–1616), author of Hamlet, King Lear, The Tempest and the Sonnets, widely held to be the greatest writer in the English language.|Shakespeare was born in Stratford-upon-Avon, the son of a glove-maker. At 18 he married Anne Hathaway; their son Hamnet died at eleven. By the early 1590s he was in London as an actor and playwright, and became a shareholder in the Lord Chamberlain's Men and the Globe Theatre. He wrote about 37 plays, from comedies like "Twelfth Night" to tragedies like "Hamlet", "King Lear" and "Macbeth", plus 154 sonnets. He coined or first recorded hundreds of words and phrases. Seven years after his death, his colleagues collected his plays in the First Folio, without which half of them might be lost.
Oscar Wilde|Irish writer and wit (1854–1900), author of The Picture of Dorian Gray and The Importance of Being Earnest. Imprisoned for homosexuality, he wrote De Profundis in his cell.|Born in Dublin to a surgeon father and a poet mother, Wilde studied classics brilliantly at Trinity College Dublin and Oxford, where he championed "art for art's sake". In London he became famous for his wit, his clothes and his conversation, then for his novel "The Picture of Dorian Gray" and comedies such as "The Importance of Being Earnest". In 1895 he was convicted of "gross indecency" for his relationships with men and sentenced to two years' hard labour. Prison broke his health; he wrote "De Profundis" and "The Ballad of Reading Gaol" from that experience, and died in exile in Paris at 46.
Isaac Newton|English mathematician and physicist (1643–1727) who discovered the laws of motion and universal gravitation and co-invented calculus.|Newton was born prematurely in Lincolnshire after his father's death and was raised by his grandmother. When plague closed Cambridge in 1665–66, he worked at home and developed calculus, theories of light and colour and the beginnings of universal gravitation. His "Principia" (1687) set out the laws of motion and gravity, explaining the planets, tides and comets. He built the first reflecting telescope. Later he became Warden of the Royal Mint, pursuing counterfeiters, and President of the Royal Society. He also spent years on alchemy and biblical prophecy. He said he had seen further by standing on the shoulders of giants.
Henry David Thoreau|American essayist and naturalist (1817–1862) who lived two years in a cabin by Walden Pond and wrote Walden and "Civil Disobedience".|Thoreau was born in Concord, Massachusetts, the son of a pencil-maker, and graduated from Harvard. He became a friend of Emerson, who let him build a cabin on his land beside Walden Pond, where Thoreau lived from 1845 to 1847. His book "Walden" (1854) records that experiment in simple living and attention to nature. He spent a night in jail for refusing to pay a tax that supported slavery and the Mexican War, which led to his essay "Civil Disobedience", later inspiring Gandhi and Martin Luther King Jr. He was also a surveyor and a careful naturalist. He died of tuberculosis at 44.
Plato|Athenian philosopher (c. 428–348 BC), student of Socrates and teacher of Aristotle, whose dialogues, among them the Republic, Symposium and Phaedo, founded Western philosophy.|Plato came from a distinguished Athenian family and might have gone into politics, but the execution of his teacher Socrates turned him to philosophy. He travelled to Italy, Sicily and perhaps Egypt, then founded the Academy in Athens around 387 BC, which lasted in some form for centuries. He wrote about 35 dialogues, most starring Socrates, on justice, love, knowledge, the soul and the ideal state. His theory of Forms holds that the things we see are shadows of eternal ideas. The philosopher Whitehead said all of European philosophy is a series of footnotes to Plato. Start with the "Apology", "Symposium" and "Republic".
René Descartes|French philosopher and mathematician (1596–1650), father of modern philosophy, who started from doubt and arrived at "I think, therefore I am".|Descartes was born in France and educated by Jesuits. He served as a volunteer soldier, and in 1619, in a stove-heated room in Germany, had a series of dreams that set him on his mission to rebuild knowledge on certain foundations. He lived mostly in the Netherlands, where he wrote "Discourse on the Method" (1637) and "Meditations on First Philosophy" (1641). He doubted everything until he reached "I think, therefore I am". He invented coordinate geometry and contributed to optics. In 1649 he went to Stockholm to teach Queen Christina, rising at 5 a.m. in winter, and died of pneumonia within months.
Protagoras|Greek Sophist (c. 490–420 BC) famous for saying "Man is the measure of all things".|Protagoras of Abdera was the most famous of the Sophists, travelling teachers who charged fees to teach rhetoric and success in public life. Plato's dialogue named after him portrays him with respect. He was invited to write laws for the new Greek colony of Thurii. His saying "Man is the measure of all things" suggests that truth is relative to each person or community. He also declared that he could not know whether the gods exist, since the question is obscure and life is short. Later tradition says his books were burned in Athens for that remark.
Inscription at the Temple of Apollo at Delphi|The maxims carved at the oracle of Apollo at Delphi, among them "Know thyself" and "Nothing in excess", which the Greeks treated as the core of wisdom.|The temple of Apollo at Delphi, in the mountains of central Greece, held the most famous oracle of the ancient world. People came from across the Mediterranean to consult the Pythia, the priestess who spoke for the god. Carved at the entrance were maxims attributed to the Seven Sages, including "Know thyself" and "Nothing in excess". Socrates took "Know thyself" as his life's task. The sanctuary flourished for a thousand years until it was closed in the late 4th century AD.
Jean-Jacques Rousseau|Geneva-born philosopher and writer (1712–1778) whose Social Contract, Emile and Confessions shaped modern politics, education and the idea of the self.|Rousseau was born in Geneva; his mother died days after his birth, and his watchmaker father abandoned him at ten. He ran away at 16 and drifted through Savoy and France, educating himself. In 1749 he won a prize essay arguing that the arts and sciences had corrupted morals, and became famous. "The Social Contract" (1762) begins "Man is born free, and everywhere he is in chains", and "Emile" proposed a new natural education. Both books were banned and he fled persecution. Strangely, he gave all five of his own children to a foundling hospital. His "Confessions" pioneered modern autobiography.
Miguel de Cervantes|Spanish novelist (1547–1616), author of Don Quixote, often called the first modern novel.|Cervantes fought at the naval battle of Lepanto in 1571, where a wound left his left hand useless. Returning to Spain, he was captured by pirates and held for five years as a slave in Algiers, where he made four failed escape attempts before being ransomed. He then worked as a tax collector, was jailed for irregularities and, according to legend, conceived "Don Quixote" in prison. Published in two parts (1605 and 1615), the story of a man who reads too many romances and sets out as a knight is often called the first modern novel. He died in 1616, the same year as Shakespeare.
Francis Bacon|English philosopher and statesman (1561–1626), pioneer of the scientific method and author of the Essays and the Novum Organum.|The son of a senior official of Elizabeth I, Bacon trained as a lawyer and rose under James I to become Lord Chancellor, before being convicted of taking bribes in 1621 and removed from office. He then devoted himself to writing. In "Novum Organum" (1620) he called for a new science built on careful observation and experiment rather than ancient authority, and he is regarded as a father of the scientific method. His "Essays" are brief, sharp reflections on life. He is said to have died of a chill caught while stuffing a chicken with snow to test whether cold preserves meat.
Arthur Schopenhauer|German philosopher (1788–1860) who saw the world as driven by a blind, restless will, and found consolation in art, compassion and Eastern thought.|Schopenhauer was the son of a wealthy Danzig merchant, who probably took his own life, and of the novelist Johanna Schopenhauer, with whom he quarrelled bitterly. His great work "The World as Will and Representation" (1818) went largely unnoticed for decades. As a lecturer in Berlin he scheduled his classes at the same hour as Hegel's, and almost no one came. He lived alone in Frankfurt with a series of poodles. Fame arrived late, with his essays "Parerga and Paralipomena" (1851). Influenced by the Upanishads and Buddhism, he found relief from suffering in art, music and compassion.
Marcus Aurelius|Roman emperor (121–180) and Stoic philosopher. His private notebook, the Meditations, written on campaign, is one of the most loved books of practical wisdom.|Marcus Aurelius was adopted into the imperial family as a teenager and studied philosophy, especially the Stoic Epictetus. He became emperor in 161 and ruled for nearly twenty years, much of it spent fighting on the Danube frontier while a plague ravaged the empire. Of his perhaps fourteen children, many died young. At night in his tent he wrote notes to himself in Greek, never meant for publication, now called the "Meditations": reminders to be patient, just and calm, and to remember how brief life is. He is often called the last of the Five Good Emperors.
Seneca|Roman Stoic philosopher, statesman and dramatist (c. 4 BC–65 AD), tutor to Nero. His Letters to Lucilius are a guide to living well and facing death.|Seneca was born in Córdoba, Spain, and came to Rome as a child. He became a famous orator, survived a sentence of death under Caligula, and was exiled to Corsica for eight years under Claudius. Recalled to tutor the young Nero, he became one of the most powerful and wealthy men in Rome, a contradiction critics noted. When Nero turned murderous, Seneca retired to write, producing his "Letters to Lucilius" and essays on anger, tranquillity and the shortness of life. In 65 AD Nero ordered him to kill himself, and he died calmly, consoling his friends.
Epictetus|Greek Stoic philosopher (c. 50–135) who was born a slave. His teachings, recorded by his student Arrian, centre on what is and isn't in our power.|Epictetus was born a slave in Hierapolis, in modern Turkey, and was owned by a freedman of Nero in Rome. He was lame, perhaps from mistreatment. His owner let him study with the Stoic Musonius Rufus, and once freed he taught philosophy in Rome until the emperor Domitian expelled all philosophers around 93 AD. He then founded a school at Nicopolis in Greece. He wrote nothing, but his student Arrian recorded his lessons in the "Discourses" and the short "Enchiridion" or handbook. Its first line divides the world into what is in our power and what is not.
Epicurus|Greek philosopher (341–270 BC) who taught that happiness comes from simple pleasures, friendship and freedom from fear, especially the fear of death.|Epicurus grew up on the island of Samos, the son of an Athenian schoolteacher. In 306 BC he bought a house and garden in Athens and founded a school there, the Garden, which unusually admitted women and slaves. He taught that the universe is made of atoms and void, that the gods do not interfere in human life, and that death is nothing to us. Happiness comes from simple pleasures, friendship and freedom from fear. He suffered from kidney stones, and on his last, painful day wrote to a friend of the joy he felt remembering their conversations. Only three letters and some sayings survive.
Cicero|Roman statesman, orator and philosopher (106–43 BC) whose essays on friendship, old age and duty brought Greek philosophy to Rome.|Marcus Tullius Cicero came from a provincial family outside Rome's aristocracy and rose by his brilliance as a lawyer and orator to become consul in 63 BC, when he exposed the Catiline conspiracy. He defended the Republic against Julius Caesar and later against Mark Antony, whom he attacked in fierce speeches. After the death of his beloved daughter Tullia, he wrote philosophy for consolation, including "On Friendship", "On Old Age" and "On Duties". Antony had him killed in 43 BC, and his head and hands were displayed in the Forum. His Latin prose became the model for centuries.
The Dhammapada|A collection of 423 verses of the Buddha's teaching in Pali, one of the best-loved texts of early Buddhism.|The Dhammapada ("verses on the Dhamma") is a collection of 423 verses in 26 chapters, part of the Pali Canon of Theravada Buddhism. Tradition holds that each verse was spoken by the Buddha in response to a particular situation, and a later commentary tells these stories. It begins with the teaching that we are what we think, and covers anger, craving, mindfulness, happiness and the path to freedom. It has been memorised and chanted across South and Southeast Asia for over two thousand years, and is often recommended as the best first introduction to the Buddha's teaching.
Emily Dickinson|American poet (1830–1886) who lived quietly in Amherst and wrote nearly 1,800 short, startling poems about death, nature and the inner life, almost none published in her lifetime.|Dickinson spent almost her whole life in her family home in Amherst, Massachusetts. She studied for a year at Mount Holyoke Female Seminary but did not profess the Christian faith expected of her. From her late twenties she withdrew increasingly from society, dressing in white and speaking to visitors through doors, while writing intense letters to friends. She wrote nearly 1,800 poems, sewing many into small handmade booklets, and published fewer than a dozen, anonymously, in her lifetime. After her death her sister Lavinia found the poems and arranged for publication. Her dashes and slant rhymes made her one of the most original poets in English.
Jane Austen|English novelist (1775–1817), author of Pride and Prejudice and Emma, whose ironic comedies of manners are also studies of judgement and feeling.|Austen was the seventh of eight children of a Hampshire clergyman, and grew up in a lively household that put on plays and read novels aloud. She began writing as a teenager. She never married, accepting one proposal and withdrawing her acceptance the next morning. Her four novels published in her lifetime, including "Sense and Sensibility", "Pride and Prejudice" and "Emma", appeared anonymously as "By a Lady". She wrote at a small table in the family sitting room in Chawton. She died at 41, probably of Addison's disease or lymphoma. Her irony, moral insight and sharp dialogue made her one of the most loved novelists.
Charles Dickens|English novelist (1812–1870), author of Great Expectations, David Copperfield and A Christmas Carol, champion of the poor and the greatest storyteller of his age.|When Dickens was twelve, his father was imprisoned for debt, and Charles was sent to work pasting labels on pots of boot polish in a rat-infested factory. He never forgot the shame. He became a reporter, then won sudden fame with "The Pickwick Papers" (1836). His novels, published in monthly instalments, from "Oliver Twist" to "Bleak House" and "Great Expectations", exposed poverty, cruel schools and the workhouse, while overflowing with comedy and memorable characters. "A Christmas Carol" helped shape how we celebrate Christmas. His public readings exhausted him and he died at 58.
Aesop|Greek storyteller (c. 620–564 BC), said to have been a slave, to whom hundreds of animal fables with morals are attributed.|Almost nothing certain is known about Aesop. Ancient sources say he was a slave on the Greek island of Samos in the 6th century BC, ugly but brilliantly clever, who won his freedom through his wit and later died at Delphi. Herodotus mentions him. The fables attributed to him, like the tortoise and the hare, the fox and the grapes, and the boy who cried wolf, were passed down orally and collected centuries later, with many added from other sources. They remain among the first stories children hear, teaching lessons about pride, greed and kindness through animals.
George Eliot|Pen name of Mary Ann Evans (1819–1880), English novelist of Middlemarch and The Mill on the Floss, famed for her moral depth and sympathy.|Mary Ann Evans grew up on a farm estate in Warwickshire and was a devout evangelical in her youth before losing her faith through reading. She translated German theology, moved to London and became an editor of the "Westminster Review". She lived openly with the married philosopher George Henry Lewes for 24 years, which shocked society. She adopted a male pen name to be taken seriously. Her novels, including "Adam Bede", "Silas Marner" and "Middlemarch", which Virginia Woolf called one of the few English novels written for grown-up people, explore moral choice and the quiet heroism of ordinary lives.
Walt Whitman|American poet (1819–1892) whose Leaves of Grass, in free verse, celebrated the self, the body, democracy and the common people.|Whitman grew up in Brooklyn and Long Island and left school at eleven, working as a printer, teacher and journalist. In 1855 he self-published "Leaves of Grass", twelve poems in a new kind of long-lined free verse, with his portrait in work clothes instead of his name. Emerson wrote to greet him "at the beginning of a great career". He revised and expanded the book for the rest of his life. During the Civil War he worked as a volunteer nurse in Washington hospitals, sitting with thousands of wounded soldiers. His elegies for Lincoln include "O Captain! My Captain!" and "When Lilacs Last in the Dooryard Bloom'd".
Kahlil Gibran|Lebanese-American poet, writer and painter (1883–1931) whose book The Prophet, a series of poetic sermons on love, work, joy and death, has been read by millions.|Gibran was born in Bsharri, in the mountains of what is now Lebanon. His father was imprisoned for embezzlement and his mother emigrated with the children to Boston's poor South End in 1895. A social worker noticed his drawings and introduced him to the arts. He returned to Beirut to study Arabic, then lost his sister, brother and mother to illness within fifteen months. He studied art in Paris and settled in New York, writing in Arabic and English. "The Prophet" (1923), 26 poetic essays spoken by a departing sage, has sold millions of copies. He died at 48.
Rainer Maria Rilke|Bohemian-Austrian poet (1875–1926) of the Duino Elegies and Sonnets to Orpheus, whose Letters to a Young Poet are a guide to solitude and inner life.|René Maria Rilke was born in Prague; his mother, grieving a lost daughter, dressed him as a girl in early childhood. He was sent to military school, which he hated. He travelled to Russia, where he met Tolstoy, then worked as secretary to the sculptor Auguste Rodin in Paris, learning to look at things patiently. This produced his "New Poems". Between 1903 and 1908 he wrote the letters to a young cadet later published as "Letters to a Young Poet". After a long creative block, in February 1922 at Château de Muzot in Switzerland he finished the "Duino Elegies" and wrote the "Sonnets to Orpheus" in a few weeks.
Fyodor Dostoevsky|Russian novelist (1821–1881) of Crime and Punishment and The Brothers Karamazov, who explored faith, freedom, guilt and suffering.|Dostoevsky was the son of a doctor at a Moscow hospital for the poor. His father was probably murdered by his own serfs. His first novel, "Poor Folk", brought him acclaim, but in 1849 he was arrested for belonging to a radical circle and sentenced to death. On the execution ground, at the last moment, the sentence was commuted to four years of hard labour in Siberia. Epileptic and often in debt from gambling, he wrote "Crime and Punishment", "The Idiot", "Demons" and "The Brothers Karamazov", novels of ideas about faith, freedom and suffering. His second wife Anna saved his finances and his career.
Leo Tolstoy|Russian novelist (1828–1910) of War and Peace and Anna Karenina, who later turned to a radical Christian ethic of nonviolence and simplicity.|Count Lev Tolstoy was born on the family estate of Yasnaya Polyana and orphaned young. He gambled and drifted in youth, then served as an artillery officer in the Crimean War, writing "Sevastopol Sketches". With his wife Sophia, who copied his manuscripts many times over, he had thirteen children. He wrote "War and Peace" and "Anna Karenina", then went through a spiritual crisis and developed a radical Christianity of nonviolence, simplicity and love, giving up meat, alcohol and his copyrights. He corresponded with the young Gandhi. At 82 he left home secretly and died at a remote railway station.
Franz Kafka|Prague-born German-language writer (1883–1924) of The Trial and The Metamorphosis, whose stories of anxiety and absurd authority gave us the word "Kafkaesque".|Kafka was born in Prague into a German-speaking Jewish family; his domineering father, a shopkeeper, overshadowed his life, as his unsent "Letter to His Father" shows. He studied law and worked for fourteen years for a workers' accident insurance institute, writing at night. He was engaged three times but never married. He published only a few stories in his lifetime, including "The Metamorphosis". Dying of tuberculosis at 40, he asked his friend Max Brod to burn his manuscripts. Brod refused and published "The Trial", "The Castle" and "Amerika", making Kafka one of the most influential writers of the 20th century.
Albert Camus|French-Algerian writer and philosopher (1913–1960) of The Stranger, The Plague and The Myth of Sisyphus, who answered the absurd with revolt and solidarity. Nobel Prize 1957.|Camus was born in poverty in French Algeria. His father was killed in the First World War when Albert was an infant, and his mother, partly deaf and illiterate, cleaned houses. A teacher, Louis Germain, recognised his gifts and helped him to a scholarship. Tuberculosis ended his hopes of a sporting career as a football goalkeeper. During the Nazi occupation he edited the Resistance newspaper "Combat". "The Stranger", "The Myth of Sisyphus" and "The Plague" made him famous. He won the Nobel Prize in 1957, at 44, and died in a car accident three years later, an unused train ticket in his pocket.
Hermann Hesse|German-Swiss novelist and poet (1877–1962) of Siddhartha, Steppenwolf and The Glass Bead Game, about the search for the self. Nobel Prize 1946.|Hesse was born in the Black Forest town of Calw to a family of Pietist missionaries who had worked in India. He ran away from a seminary at 14 and struggled through a suicidal adolescence before working as a bookseller and writing. During the First World War he opposed nationalism and was attacked as a traitor. A personal crisis led him to psychoanalysis with a student of Jung, which shaped "Demian", "Siddhartha", about a young man's search for enlightenment in the time of the Buddha, and "Steppenwolf". He settled in Switzerland. "The Glass Bead Game" helped earn him the Nobel Prize in 1946.
Confucius|Chinese teacher and philosopher (551–479 BC) whose sayings, collected in the Analects, made virtue, learning, ritual and family duty the foundation of Chinese culture.|Kong Qiu, later called Kongzi or Master Kong, was born in the state of Lu in eastern China to a family of minor nobility that had fallen on hard times; his father died when he was three. He worked as a granary keeper and minor official, and taught students of any background who were eager to learn. Failing to find a ruler who would put his ideas into practice, he wandered between states for years before returning home to teach. His conversations, collected by students in the "Analects", stress humaneness, ritual propriety, learning and good government by virtue. His teaching shaped East Asian culture for over two thousand years.
Rumi|Persian poet and Sufi mystic (1207–1273). His Masnavi and lyric poems of longing for the divine Beloved are among the most widely read poetry in the world.|Jalal al-Din Rumi was born in Balkh, in today's Afghanistan, or nearby Vakhsh. As a boy he fled the Mongol advance with his family, and they settled in Konya, in Anatolia, where he became a respected religious scholar like his father. In 1244 he met the wandering dervish Shams of Tabriz, and their intense spiritual friendship transformed him into a poet of divine love. When Shams vanished, Rumi poured his longing into thousands of ghazals. His six-book "Masnavi", told in stories and parables, has been called the Qur'an in Persian. His followers founded the Mevlevi order, known for the whirling ceremony.
Omar Khayyam|Persian mathematician, astronomer and poet (1048–1131). The quatrains attributed to him, made famous in English by Edward FitzGerald, celebrate the fleeting moment.|Omar Khayyam was born in Nishapur, in Persia. In his own time he was famous as a mathematician and astronomer: he wrote a treatise solving cubic equations with geometry and helped reform the Persian calendar into one more accurate than the Gregorian. The quatrains (rubaiyat) attributed to him, on wine, love, the brevity of life and doubt about the hereafter, are of uncertain authorship, and many were added later. In 1859 Edward FitzGerald's free English version, "The Rubáiyát of Omar Khayyám", became one of the most popular poems of the Victorian age.
Sun Tzu|Chinese general and strategist (traditionally 6th century BC), author of The Art of War.|According to the historian Sima Qian, Sun Wu was a general from the state of Qi who served King Helü of Wu around 500 BC. To prove his methods, he is said to have drilled the king's palace women and executed two royal favourites who would not obey. Many scholars think "The Art of War" took shape later, during the Warring States period. Its thirteen short chapters stress deception, preparation, knowing the terrain and winning without fighting. It influenced Chinese and Japanese strategy for centuries and is now read in business schools and sports teams.
Rabindranath Tagore|Bengali poet, philosopher, composer and painter (1861–1941), author of Gitanjali, and the first non-European to win the Nobel Prize in Literature (1913).|Tagore was the youngest of thirteen surviving children in a wealthy, cultured Bengali family in Calcutta. He disliked school and was educated largely at home. He wrote thousands of songs, poems, stories, novels and plays, and took up painting at 60. His English "Gitanjali" (Song Offerings) won him the Nobel Prize in 1913, the first for a non-European. He founded a school and university at Santiniketan, where classes were held under trees. He renounced his knighthood after the Amritsar massacre of 1919. He wrote the national anthems of both India and Bangladesh. He lost his wife and three of his children in his lifetime.
Haruki Murakami|Japanese novelist (born 1949) of Norwegian Wood, Kafka on the Shore and 1Q84, whose dreamlike stories mix loneliness, music and the uncanny.|Murakami was born in Kyoto and grew up in Kobe, the son of teachers of Japanese literature, though he read mostly Western novels. After university he and his wife ran a jazz bar in Tokyo. At 29, watching a baseball game, he suddenly felt he could write a novel. "Norwegian Wood" (1987) made him a celebrity in Japan; "The Wind-Up Bird Chronicle" and "Kafka on the Shore" made him internationally famous. He is a dedicated marathon runner and wrote about it in "What I Talk About When I Talk About Running". He also translates American fiction into Japanese.
William Blake|English poet, painter and visionary (1757–1827) of the Songs of Innocence and of Experience, who saw "a world in a grain of sand".|Blake was the son of a London hosier and saw visions from childhood, including a tree full of angels. He trained as an engraver and developed his own method of "illuminated printing", combining poems and images on copper plates, coloured by hand with his wife Catherine. "Songs of Innocence and of Experience" contains "The Tyger" and "London". His prophetic books create a whole mythology. He was poor, largely unrecognised and briefly tried for sedition. He died singing, it is said, and was buried in an unmarked grave. He is now seen as one of the great English poets and artists.
Giacomo Leopardi|Italian poet and philosopher (1798–1837) of the Canti and the Zibaldone, whose lucid pessimism is matched by the beauty of his verse.|Leopardi was born in the small town of Recanati to a noble, impoverished family. He taught himself Greek, Hebrew and other languages in his father's library, studying so intensely that his health and eyesight were ruined and he developed a curved spine. He longed to escape the town, which he felt as a prison. His "Canti", including "The Infinite" and "To Silvia", are among the most beautiful poems in Italian. His prose "Operette morali" and notebook "Zibaldone" set out a deep pessimism, softened by compassion for all who suffer. He died in Naples at 38, during a cholera epidemic.
Charles Baudelaire|French poet (1821–1867) of Les Fleurs du mal, who found beauty in the city, decay and melancholy and opened the way to modern poetry.|Baudelaire's father died when he was six, and he resented his stepfather, a general. Sent on a voyage to India to steady him, he jumped ship in Mauritius and returned to Paris, where he spent his inheritance on fine clothes and art and was placed under legal guardianship. His poetry collection "Les Fleurs du mal" (1857) was prosecuted for offending public morals, and six poems were banned. He translated Poe into French, wrote pioneering art criticism and prose poems about Paris, and coined the term "modernity". He died at 46 after a stroke, in his mother's arms.
The Bhagavad Gita|A dialogue in 700 verses from the Mahabharata (c. 2nd century BC) in which the god Krishna teaches the warrior Arjuna about duty, action and the eternal self.|The Bhagavad Gita, "Song of the Lord", is a 700-verse dialogue within the Indian epic the Mahabharata, probably composed between the 2nd century BC and the 2nd century AD. On the battlefield of Kurukshetra, the warrior Arjuna despairs at having to fight his own kin. His charioteer, Krishna, revealed as God, teaches him about duty, the immortal self, the paths of action, knowledge and devotion, and acting without attachment to results. It is one of Hinduism's most important scriptures and was a lifelong companion for Gandhi, Thoreau and others.
The Upanishads|Ancient Indian philosophical texts (c. 800–200 BC) that explore the nature of the self (Atman) and ultimate reality (Brahman), the foundation of Vedanta.|The Upanishads are over a hundred philosophical texts, the oldest composed in India between about 800 and 300 BC, that close the Vedas. The name suggests "sitting down near" a teacher. In dialogues and parables they explore the nature of reality and the self, teaching that Atman, the innermost self, is one with Brahman, the ground of all being: "Thou art that". They are the foundation of Vedanta philosophy and influenced the Buddha, Schopenhauer, Emerson, Yeats and T. S. Eliot. The Isha, Katha and Chandogya Upanishads are good places to begin.
Viktor Frankl|Austrian psychiatrist (1905–1997) who survived the Nazi camps and wrote Man's Search for Meaning, arguing that the will to meaning sustains us even in suffering.|Frankl was a Viennese psychiatrist who, as a young doctor, ran programmes that greatly reduced suicides among students. In 1942 he, his wife and his parents were deported by the Nazis. He survived Theresienstadt, Auschwitz and Dachau subcamps; his wife, mother, father and brother did not. In 1946 he wrote "Man's Search for Meaning" in nine days, describing camp life and his conclusion that meaning can be found even in suffering. It has sold over ten million copies. He founded logotherapy, took up mountain climbing, and earned a pilot's licence in his late sixties.
Fernando Pessoa|Portuguese poet (1888–1935) who wrote under dozens of invented personas, or heteronyms. His Book of Disquiet was published after his death.|Pessoa was born in Lisbon; after his father's death his mother remarried and he grew up in Durban, South Africa, writing his first poems in English. He returned to Lisbon at 17 and lived there quietly, working as a freelance commercial translator. He created dozens of "heteronyms", imagined poets with their own biographies and styles, such as Alberto Caeiro, Ricardo Reis and Álvaro de Campos. When he died at 47, a trunk was found with over 25,000 manuscript pages, including "The Book of Disquiet", the fragmentary diary of an assistant bookkeeper, Bernardo Soares.
Jorge Luis Borges|Argentine writer (1899–1986) of labyrinths, mirrors and infinite libraries, whose short stories in Ficciones and The Aleph are puzzles about time and reality.|Borges was born in Buenos Aires and grew up in a house with a large library of English books. He spent his teens in Geneva and Spain before returning to Argentina. After his father's death he worked in a municipal library; when Perón came to power he was "promoted" to poultry inspector, a humiliation he resigned from. His short stories, collected in "Ficciones" and "The Aleph", explore infinite libraries, labyrinths, mirrors and time. A hereditary illness left him blind by his late fifties, just as he was appointed director of the National Library. He called it God's irony to give him books and the night together.
E. M. Cioran|Romanian-born French philosopher and aphorist (1911–1995) whose brilliant, despairing fragments are written with dark humour and lyricism.|Emil Cioran was born in a Romanian village in the Carpathians, the son of an Orthodox priest. He suffered lifelong insomnia from his youth, which he said shaped his philosophy. As a young man he was briefly drawn to the far-right Iron Guard, which he later deeply regretted. In 1937 he moved to Paris on a scholarship and stayed, living modestly in an attic and writing in French. His books of brilliant, bitter aphorisms, such as "A Short History of Decay" and "The Trouble with Being Born", explore despair with a strange vitality and humour.
Simone Weil|French philosopher, mystic and activist (1909–1943) who wrote on attention, affliction and grace in Gravity and Grace.|Weil was born in Paris to a secular Jewish family; her brother André became a famous mathematician. She studied philosophy at the École Normale Supérieure, then taught, while giving her salary to the unemployed. She took a year off to work on factory assembly lines to understand workers' lives, fought briefly with anarchists in the Spanish Civil War, and had profound mystical experiences of Christ. Fleeing the Nazis, she reached London to work for the Free French. She refused to eat more than people in occupied France and died at 34. Her notebooks became "Gravity and Grace".
Pablo Neruda|Chilean poet and diplomat (1904–1973), author of Twenty Love Poems and a Song of Despair and the Odes to common things. Nobel Prize 1971.|Ricardo Eliécer Neftalí Reyes Basoalto was born in Parral, Chile; his mother died weeks after his birth. He took the pen name Pablo Neruda to hide his poetry from his disapproving father. "Twenty Love Poems and a Song of Despair" (1924), published at 19, made him famous. He served as a diplomat in Asia, Spain and Mexico, and the Spanish Civil War turned him to politics. His "Canto General" is an epic of Latin America; his odes celebrate ordinary things like tomatoes and socks. He won the Nobel Prize in 1971 and died days after the 1973 military coup.
Mary Oliver|American poet (1935–2019) of close attention to the natural world, who asked "what is it you plan to do with your one wild and precious life?"|Mary Oliver grew up in Maple Heights, Ohio, and escaped an unhappy childhood into the woods with books. At 17 she visited the home of the poet Edna St. Vincent Millay and stayed for years helping organise Millay's papers. There she met the photographer Molly Malone Cook, her partner for over forty years, and they settled in Provincetown, Massachusetts. Oliver walked the woods and shore daily with a notebook. "American Primitive" won the Pulitzer Prize in 1984. Her poems, such as "Wild Geese" and "The Summer Day", are among the best-loved in America.
Wisława Szymborska|Polish poet (1923–2012) whose witty, wondering poems look at ordinary things with fresh eyes. Nobel Prize 1996.|Szymborska was born near Poznań and lived most of her life in Kraków, where she studied Polish literature and sociology without finishing a degree. For decades she worked as a poetry editor and columnist, writing witty reviews of books no one else would review. She was a member of the Communist Party in her youth but broke with it. She published only about 350 poems, saying she had a wastebasket in her room. She won the Nobel Prize in 1996, calling the fame "the Stockholm tragedy". Her poems combine humour, irony and wonder at ordinary things.
Milan Kundera|Czech-born French novelist (1929–2023) of The Unbearable Lightness of Being, who mixed love stories with reflections on history and fate.|Kundera was born in Brno, Czechoslovakia, the son of a pianist, and studied music before turning to literature and film. He joined the Communist Party, was expelled, rejoined, and was expelled again after the Soviet invasion of 1968 crushed the Prague Spring. His books were banned. In 1975 he emigrated to France, where his Czech citizenship was taken away; it was restored in 2019. His novels, including "The Book of Laughter and Forgetting" and "The Unbearable Lightness of Being", mix storytelling with philosophical essay. Later he wrote in French.
Jean-Paul Sartre|French philosopher, novelist and playwright (1905–1980), leading voice of existentialism, who held that we are condemned to be free.|Sartre was born in Paris; his father died when he was a baby and he was raised in his grandfather's library. At the École Normale Supérieure he met Simone de Beauvoir, his lifelong companion. He was a prisoner of war in 1940. His novel "Nausea" (1938), his treatise "Being and Nothingness" (1943) and plays like "No Exit" made him the leading voice of existentialism. He founded the journal "Les Temps modernes", took up many political causes and refused the Nobel Prize in 1964. Fifty thousand people followed his funeral through Paris.
Kabir|Indian mystic poet and weaver (15th century) whose songs, loved by Hindus and Muslims alike, mock empty ritual and seek God within.|Kabir was a weaver in Benares (Varanasi), born, according to legend, to a Muslim family, and possibly a disciple of the Hindu teacher Ramananda. He criticised the rituals and hypocrisy of both Hindus and Muslims, teaching devotion to a God beyond names, found within. His sayings and songs, in simple, vivid Hindi, were passed down orally and collected in Sikh scripture and other traditions. A famous story says that at his death, Hindus and Muslims argued over his body; when they lifted the shroud, they found only flowers, which they divided. Tagore translated one hundred of his poems.
Ecclesiastes|A book of the Hebrew Bible in which "the Preacher" reflects on the vanity of all things under the sun and the gift of simple joys.|Ecclesiastes is a book of the Hebrew Bible, attributed in its opening to "the Preacher" (Qoheleth), son of David, king in Jerusalem, traditionally identified with Solomon. Scholars generally date it to the 5th to 3rd century BC. Its speaker searches for meaning in pleasure, wealth, work and wisdom and finds all of it "hevel", vanity or vapour. Yet he also urges us to eat our bread with joy, to love, and to enjoy our work. Its passage "To every thing there is a season" is one of the most quoted in literature.
Book of Job|A book of the Hebrew Bible, a poetic drama about a good man's suffering and his argument with God.|The Book of Job, in the Hebrew Bible, is a poetic drama probably written between the 6th and 4th centuries BC. Job, a righteous man, loses his wealth, his children and his health as a test of his faith. His friends insist he must have sinned; Job insists he hasn't, and demands an answer from God. God answers from a whirlwind, not with explanations but with a vision of the vastness and wildness of creation. Job's honest lament has made the book central to thinking about undeserved suffering, admired by writers from Tennyson to Jung.
Zhuangzi|Chinese Daoist philosopher (c. 369–286 BC) whose playful, paradoxical stories, like the butterfly dream, question every fixed point of view.|Zhuang Zhou lived in the state of Song during China's Warring States period, and is said to have worked as a minor official in a lacquer garden. When a king offered him the post of chief minister, he answered that he would rather drag his tail in the mud like a living turtle than be a sacred dead one in a temple. The book named after him, partly by later followers, is full of humour, paradox and talking animals, teaching spontaneity, the relativity of viewpoints and freedom from conventional values. With Lao Tzu he is a founder of Daoism.
Herman Melville|American novelist and poet (1819–1891), author of Moby-Dick, whose voyages became meditations on obsession, evil and the unknowable.|Melville's father, a merchant, went bankrupt and died when Herman was twelve. At 21 he sailed on a whaling ship, deserted in the Marquesas, lived among the Typee people, and drifted through the Pacific. His adventure books "Typee" and "Omoo" were popular, but "Moby-Dick" (1851), dedicated to his friend Hawthorne, was a commercial failure. He spent nineteen years as a customs inspector in New York, writing poetry, while his eldest son took his own life. When he died he was almost forgotten; "Billy Budd" was found in manuscript. A revival in the 1920s made him a giant of American literature.
Ivan Turgenev|Russian novelist and storyteller (1818–1883) of Fathers and Sons and A Sportsman's Sketches, known for his gentle realism and melancholy.|Turgenev grew up on his mother's estate, where she ruled her serfs and family cruelly. He studied in Moscow, St Petersburg and Berlin. "A Sportsman's Sketches" (1852), showing serfs as fully human, is said to have influenced Tsar Alexander II's decision to free them. He was arrested for an obituary of Gogol and confined to his estate. His novel "Fathers and Sons" (1862) introduced the "nihilist" Bazarov and angered both radicals and conservatives. He spent much of his life abroad, following the singer Pauline Viardot, whom he loved for forty years, and was friends with Flaubert.
Anton Chekhov|Russian playwright, short-story writer and doctor (1860–1904) of The Seagull and The Cherry Orchard, master of quiet tragedy and compassion.|Chekhov was the grandson of a serf and the son of a grocer in Taganrog who went bankrupt. He supported his family by writing comic sketches while studying medicine in Moscow, and practised as a doctor, often treating peasants for free. "Medicine is my lawful wife and literature my mistress," he said. He travelled across Siberia to the penal island of Sakhalin to report on its prisoners. His short stories and plays, "The Seagull", "Uncle Vanya", "Three Sisters" and "The Cherry Orchard", changed modern fiction and drama. He died of tuberculosis at 44.
Dante Alighieri|Italian poet (1265–1321), author of the Divine Comedy, a journey through Hell, Purgatory and Paradise guided by Virgil and Beatrice.|Dante was born in Florence to a family of minor nobility. At nine he first saw Beatrice Portinari, whom he loved idealistically for life; her death at 24 inspired "La Vita Nuova". He was active in Florentine politics, but in 1302 he was exiled under threat of death and never returned. In exile he wrote the "Divine Comedy", a journey through Hell, Purgatory and Paradise, guided by Virgil and then Beatrice. Writing it in Italian rather than Latin helped make Tuscan the basis of the Italian language. He died in Ravenna in 1321, soon after finishing it.
Sigmund Freud|Austrian neurologist (1856–1939), founder of psychoanalysis, who explored the unconscious, dreams and desire.|Freud was born in Moravia and grew up in Vienna, the eldest son of his mother's eight children. Barred by antisemitism from some academic paths, he trained as a neurologist, studied hysteria with Charcot in Paris, and developed psychoanalysis, the "talking cure". "The Interpretation of Dreams" (1899) argued that dreams express hidden wishes. He introduced ideas of the unconscious, repression, the ego and the id that changed how we speak about ourselves. In 1938, after the Nazis took Austria, he escaped to London with help from Marie Bonaparte. He died there in 1939 of cancer of the jaw.
Carl Jung|Swiss psychiatrist (1875–1961), founder of analytical psychology, who wrote about archetypes, the collective unconscious and the journey to wholeness.|Jung was born in Switzerland, the son of a pastor, and had vivid dreams and an inner life from childhood. A psychiatrist in Zurich, he became Freud's closest collaborator, but they split in 1913 over Freud's emphasis on sexuality. A period of crisis followed in which he recorded fantasies and visions in "The Red Book". He developed ideas of the collective unconscious, archetypes, introversion and extraversion, and individuation, the lifelong process of becoming whole. He studied alchemy, mythology and Eastern religion, and built a stone tower at Bollingen as a retreat. "Memories, Dreams, Reflections" is his autobiography.
Stephen King|American novelist (born 1947) of horror and suspense, whose memoir On Writing is a classic guide to the craft.|King was born in Portland, Maine; his father left the family when he was two and his mother raised him and his brother on low-wage jobs. He sold short stories while working in a laundry and teaching English. His wife Tabitha rescued the draft of "Carrie" from the bin, and it became his first published novel in 1974. He has since written over sixty novels, including "The Shining", "It" and "The Stand". In 1999 he was struck by a van and badly injured. His memoir "On Writing" is one of the most loved guides to the craft.
Ray Bradbury|American writer (1920–2012) of Fahrenheit 451 and The Martian Chronicles, a lifelong champion of imagination and libraries.|Bradbury grew up in Waukegan, Illinois, and Los Angeles, and never went to college, educating himself in public libraries three days a week for ten years. He sold newspapers while writing a story every week. "The Martian Chronicles" (1950) made his name. He wrote "Fahrenheit 451", a novel about book-burning, on a rented typewriter in a library basement at ten cents per half hour. He also wrote "Dandelion Wine" and "Something Wicked This Way Comes" and the screenplay for John Huston's "Moby Dick". He never learned to drive. He urged writers to write a story a week and to love what they do.
Anne Lamott|American writer (born 1954) whose Bird by Bird is a warm, funny guide to writing and to life.|Lamott grew up in Marin County, California, the daughter of the writer Kenneth Lamott. She overcame alcoholism and became a Christian in her thirties, writing about faith with candour and humour. "Operating Instructions" chronicles her first year as a single mother. "Bird by Bird" (1994), named after her father's advice to her brother about a school report on birds, is one of the most popular books on writing, urging "shitty first drafts" and taking things one step at a time. She has also written novels and short books on prayer, hope and grace.
Annie Dillard|American writer (born 1945) of Pilgrim at Tinker Creek and The Writing Life, whose prose looks at nature with fierce attention.|Dillard grew up in Pittsburgh, described in her memoir "An American Childhood". After studying at Hollins College in Virginia, she spent a year walking and observing the creek near her home, filling notebooks. The result, "Pilgrim at Tinker Creek" (1974), won the Pulitzer Prize for nonfiction when she was 29. Her essays, like "Total Eclipse" and "Living Like Weasels", combine close observation of nature with spiritual intensity. "The Writing Life" contains the line "How we spend our days is, of course, how we spend our lives." She taught for years at Wesleyan University.
Steven Pressfield|American novelist (born 1943) whose The War of Art is about overcoming resistance to creative work.|Pressfield worked as an advertising copywriter, schoolteacher, truck driver, oil-field worker and screenwriter before success came in his fifties. His first novel, "The Legend of Bagger Vance" (1995), became a film. "Gates of Fire", about the Spartans at Thermopylae, is used in military academies. His short book "The War of Art" (2002) names "Resistance" as the inner force that keeps us from doing creative work, and argues that we beat it by turning professional: showing up every day. It is widely read by writers, artists and entrepreneurs.
Madeleine L'Engle|American writer (1918–2007) of A Wrinkle in Time, who also wrote on faith and art in Walking on Water.|L'Engle grew up in New York and at boarding schools in Europe, a lonely child who found refuge in writing. She worked in the theatre, where she met her husband, the actor Hugh Franklin. "A Wrinkle in Time" was rejected by more than two dozen publishers before it was published in 1962; it won the Newbery Medal and has sold millions of copies. She wrote over sixty books, including journals and reflections on faith and creativity such as "Walking on Water". She was for many years librarian and writer-in-residence at the Cathedral of St John the Divine.
Austin Kleon|American writer and artist (born 1983), author of Steal Like an Artist.|Kleon is an American writer and artist from Ohio, living in Texas. He first became known for "Newspaper Blackout", poems made by blacking out words in newspaper articles. A talk he gave to students grew into "Steal Like an Artist" (2012), a short illustrated book arguing that all creative work builds on what came before, which became an international bestseller. He followed it with "Show Your Work!" and "Keep Going". His work encourages daily practice, curiosity and generosity in sharing what you make.
Julia Cameron|American teacher and writer (born 1948) whose The Artist's Way introduced "morning pages" to millions.|Cameron was born in Illinois and worked as a journalist for the Washington Post and Rolling Stone. She married the film director Martin Scorsese and co-wrote films. After struggling with alcoholism and getting sober, she began teaching creativity workshops. Her book "The Artist's Way" (1992) introduced two practices: "morning pages", three pages of longhand writing each morning, and the "artist date", a weekly solo outing to refill the creative well. It has sold millions of copies and helped many people recover their creativity.
Elizabeth Gilbert|American writer (born 1969) of Eat, Pray, Love and Big Magic, on creativity and courage.|Gilbert grew up on a Christmas tree farm in Connecticut and worked as a cook, bartender and magazine journalist. Her early stories won praise, but "Eat, Pray, Love" (2006), a memoir of a year in Italy, India and Indonesia after a painful divorce, became a worldwide phenomenon. Her TED talk on creative genius and her book "Big Magic" (2015) encourage people to live creatively without fear, treating ideas as visitors who seek collaborators. Her novel "The Signature of All Things" follows a 19th-century botanist.
Ursula K. Le Guin|American writer (1929–2018) of the Earthsea books and The Left Hand of Darkness, whose fantasy and science fiction carry Daoist wisdom.|Le Guin was born in Berkeley, California, the daughter of the anthropologist Alfred Kroeber and the writer Theodora Kroeber. Her father's studies of Native American cultures shaped her imagined worlds. She wrote the "Earthsea" books, about a school for wizards, and science-fiction novels such as "The Left Hand of Darkness" and "The Dispossessed", exploring gender, anarchism and ecology. She also translated the Tao Te Ching. She championed genre fiction as literature and, at 85, gave a celebrated speech defending the freedom of writers against commerce. She lived most of her life in Portland, Oregon.
Neil Gaiman|English writer (born 1960) of The Sandman, American Gods and Coraline.|Gaiman grew up in Sussex, England, a voracious reader. He started as a journalist, writing a biography of the band Duran Duran. His comic series "The Sandman" (1989–1996), about the lord of dreams, transformed what comics could be. He went on to write novels for adults and children, including "American Gods", "Coraline", "The Graveyard Book" and "The Ocean at the End of the Lane", drawing on myth and folklore. His 2012 commencement speech "Make Good Art" became a popular little book.
Maya Angelou|American poet and memoirist (1928–2014), author of I Know Why the Caged Bird Sings.|Marguerite Johnson grew up in Stamps, Arkansas, and St Louis. After being raped at eight by her mother's boyfriend, who was then killed, she stopped speaking for almost five years, believing her words had caused his death. A neighbour, Mrs Flowers, brought her back to language through poetry. She became San Francisco's first Black female streetcar conductor, a dancer and singer, a journalist in Egypt and Ghana, and worked with Malcolm X and Martin Luther King Jr. "I Know Why the Caged Bird Sings" (1969) began seven memoirs. She read "On the Pulse of Morning" at Bill Clinton's inauguration.
Toni Morrison|American novelist (1931–2019) of Beloved and Song of Solomon, who wrote the inner lives of Black Americans with mythic power. Nobel Prize 1993.|Chloe Wofford grew up in Lorain, Ohio, in a working-class family that loved stories and music. She studied at Howard and Cornell, and as an editor at Random House she championed Black writers. She wrote her first novel, "The Bluest Eye", early in the mornings as a single mother of two sons. "Song of Solomon" and "Beloved", based on the story of Margaret Garner, who killed her daughter rather than see her returned to slavery, made her one of America's greatest novelists. She won the Pulitzer Prize in 1988 and the Nobel Prize in 1993.
Kurt Vonnegut|American novelist (1922–2007) of Slaughterhouse-Five, whose dark comedy is full of tenderness.|Vonnegut grew up in Indianapolis. His mother took her own life on Mother's Day 1944, while he was on leave from the army. As a prisoner of war in Dresden, he survived the Allied firebombing of February 1945 in a slaughterhouse meat locker, then helped dig bodies from the ruins. That experience became "Slaughterhouse-Five" (1969), with its refrain "So it goes". He worked in public relations for General Electric before writing full-time. His darkly comic novels, including "Cat's Cradle" and "Breakfast of Champions", combine satire with deep kindness: "God damn it, you've got to be kind."
James Baldwin|American novelist and essayist (1924–1987) of Go Tell It on the Mountain and The Fire Next Time, who wrote about race, love and identity with prophetic force.|Baldwin grew up in Harlem, the eldest of nine children; his stepfather was a strict preacher, and James was a boy preacher himself at 14. Facing racism and homophobia in America, he moved to Paris in 1948 with forty dollars. There he wrote "Go Tell It on the Mountain", based on his youth, and the essays "Notes of a Native Son". "Giovanni's Room" depicted love between men. Returning to the US, he became a powerful voice in the civil rights movement; "The Fire Next Time" (1963) was a bestseller. He spent his last years in Saint-Paul-de-Vence, France.
Joan Didion|American essayist and novelist (1934–2021) of Slouching Towards Bethlehem and The Year of Magical Thinking.|Didion was born in Sacramento, California, to a family that had settled there in pioneer days. She won Vogue's essay prize in her senior year at Berkeley and worked at the magazine in New York for seven years. Her essay collections "Slouching Towards Bethlehem" and "The White Album" defined New Journalism with their cool precision and personal anxiety. With her husband John Gregory Dunne she wrote screenplays. After his sudden death in 2003 she wrote "The Year of Magical Thinking", and after their daughter Quintana's death, "Blue Nights".
George Orwell|English writer (1903–1950) of Animal Farm and Nineteen Eighty-Four, and essays defending plain truth against propaganda.|Eric Blair was born in India and educated at Eton. He served five years in the Imperial Police in Burma, which left him ashamed of empire. He chose voluntary poverty to write "Down and Out in Paris and London" and documented working-class life in "The Road to Wigan Pier". In the Spanish Civil War he was shot through the throat and fled when the communist-backed government hunted his militia. His fables "Animal Farm" and "Nineteen Eighty-Four" warned against totalitarianism. He wrote the latter on the remote island of Jura while dying of tuberculosis.
E. B. White|American writer (1899–1985) of Charlotte's Web and co-author of The Elements of Style.|Elwyn Brooks White grew up in Mount Vernon, New York, and became a writer for The New Yorker from its early years, shaping its witty, clear style. He moved to a farm in Maine, writing essays collected in "One Man's Meat". He wrote three children's classics: "Stuart Little", "Charlotte's Web", inspired by a spider in his barn, and "The Trumpet of the Swan". He revised his old professor William Strunk's "The Elements of Style", which became the most famous guide to clear English: "Omit needless words."
Pablo Picasso|Spanish painter and sculptor (1881–1973), co-founder of Cubism and one of the most influential artists of the 20th century.|Picasso was born in Málaga, the son of an art teacher, and could draw like an academic master as a teenager. In Paris his Blue Period, after a friend's suicide, showed poverty and sorrow; then came his Rose Period. "Les Demoiselles d'Avignon" (1907) shocked even his friends, and with Georges Braque he invented Cubism. He worked in every medium, from sculpture to ceramics. "Guernica" (1937) protested the bombing of a Basque town in the Spanish Civil War. He produced tens of thousands of works and remained restlessly inventive until his death at 91.
Henri Matisse|French painter (1869–1954), master of colour and of the paper cut-outs of his last years.|Matisse was studying law when, at 20, recovering from appendicitis, his mother gave him a box of paints, and he felt he had found paradise.
