In 1905 his wild colours earned him and his friends the name "Fauves", wild beasts. He sought "an art of balance, of purity and serenity". He and Picasso were friendly rivals. After cancer surgery in 1941 left him mostly in a wheelchair, he invented a new art of painted paper cut-outs, which he called "drawing with scissors", and designed the Chapel of the Rosary in Vence. He called this his second life.
Georgia O'Keeffe|American painter (1887–1986) of enlarged flowers, bones and New Mexico landscapes.|O'Keeffe grew up on a Wisconsin dairy farm and decided by twelve to be an artist. She taught art in Texas, where the open plains moved her. Her abstract charcoal drawings reached the photographer and gallerist Alfred Stieglitz, who exhibited them and later married her. Her enormous close-ups of flowers made her famous. From 1929 she spent summers in New Mexico, moving there permanently after Stieglitz's death, painting bones, cliffs and the desert sky at Ghost Ranch. When her eyesight failed in old age, she turned to pottery with help. She died at 98.
Frida Kahlo|Mexican painter (1907–1954) whose vivid self-portraits turned pain and identity into art.|Kahlo grew up in the Blue House in Coyoacán, Mexico City. Polio at six left one leg thinner. At 18 a bus accident broke her spine, pelvis and leg, and an iron handrail pierced her body. During months in bed, she began painting with a mirror above her. She married the muralist Diego Rivera twice. About a third of her paintings are self-portraits, exploring pain, identity, Mexican culture and her body with startling honesty. She endured some thirty operations. Her last painting, a still life of watermelons, is inscribed "Viva la vida", long live life.
Leonard Cohen|Canadian poet, novelist and songwriter (1934–2016) of "Suzanne" and "Hallelujah", whose songs mix the sacred and the sensual.|Cohen grew up in a Jewish family in Westmount, Montreal; his father died when he was nine. He published poetry and two novels, "The Favourite Game" and "Beautiful Losers", while living on the Greek island of Hydra. In his thirties he turned to songwriting, with "Suzanne" and "So Long, Marianne". "Hallelujah" took years to write. He spent five years at a Zen monastery on Mount Baldy, California, being ordained as a monk. After discovering his manager had taken most of his savings, he toured again in his seventies to great acclaim. His last album appeared weeks before his death.
John Cage|American composer (1912–1992) who brought chance, silence and Zen into music, most famously in 4′33″.|Cage, the son of an inventor in Los Angeles, studied with Arnold Schoenberg, who said he had no feeling for harmony; Cage said he would beat his head against that wall. He invented the "prepared piano", with screws and rubber between its strings. Influenced by Zen Buddhism through D. T. Suzuki and by the I Ching, he composed using chance and made music from everyday sounds. His "4′33″" asks the audience to listen to the sounds around them. He was the lifelong partner and collaborator of the choreographer Merce Cunningham, and an expert on wild mushrooms.
Twyla Tharp|American choreographer (born 1941), author of The Creative Habit.|Tharp grew up in Indiana and California, where her mother had her take lessons in everything from violin to baton twirling. She danced with Paul Taylor's company, then founded her own in 1965. Her choreography mixed ballet, modern dance and popular music, from Frank Sinatra to the Beach Boys, in works like "Push Comes to Shove" for Mikhail Baryshnikov. She choreographed films such as "Amadeus" and the Broadway show "Movin' Out". Her book "The Creative Habit" argues that creativity comes from daily ritual and hard work, not waiting for inspiration.
Raymond Carver|American short-story writer and poet (1938–1988), master of spare, quiet fiction about ordinary lives.|Carver grew up in Oregon and Washington, the son of a sawmill worker. He married at 19 and had two children by 20, working as a janitor, sawmill hand and delivery man while writing in the car or at the kitchen table. Alcoholism nearly destroyed him; he stopped drinking in 1977 and called the years after his "second life". With the poet Tess Gallagher he found happiness. His stories, collected in "What We Talk About When We Talk About Love" and "Cathedral", portray working-class lives with spare precision. He died of lung cancer at 50.
Virginia Woolf|English novelist and essayist (1882–1941) of Mrs Dalloway, To the Lighthouse and A Room of One's Own, who caught the flow of consciousness in prose.|Adeline Virginia Stephen grew up in London, daughter of the critic Leslie Stephen. Her mother's death when she was thirteen led to the first of several breakdowns. With her siblings she formed the Bloomsbury Group of artists and thinkers. She married Leonard Woolf and together they founded the Hogarth Press. Her novels "Mrs Dalloway", "To the Lighthouse" and "The Waves" capture the flow of consciousness. "A Room of One's Own" argued that a woman needs money and a room of her own to write. During the Second World War, fearing another illness, she took her own life.
Italo Calvino|Italian writer (1923–1985) of Invisible Cities and If on a Winter's Night a Traveller, whose playful fables are full of lightness and wonder.|Calvino was born in Cuba to Italian botanist parents and grew up in San Remo. During the Second World War he joined the partisan Resistance, the subject of his first novel. He worked for the publisher Einaudi in Turin and collected Italian folktales. His fiction became increasingly playful and inventive: "The Baron in the Trees", about a boy who climbs into the trees and never comes down, "Invisible Cities", in which Marco Polo describes impossible cities to Kublai Khan, and "If on a winter's night a traveller", a novel about reading novels. He died of a stroke at 61.
Igor Stravinsky|Russian-born composer (1882–1971) of The Rite of Spring, who also wrote on music and discipline.|Stravinsky was born near St Petersburg, the son of a famous opera singer, and studied law before taking lessons with Rimsky-Korsakov. The impresario Sergei Diaghilev commissioned ballets from him for the Ballets Russes: "The Firebird", "Petrushka" and "The Rite of Spring", whose 1913 premiere in Paris caused a near-riot. He lived in Switzerland and France, then moved to the United States in 1939. His style changed several times, from Russian folk energy to neoclassicism to twelve-tone music. His "Poetics of Music" lectures say that constraint frees the artist.
John Keats|English Romantic poet (1795–1821) of the great odes, who died of tuberculosis at 25. His letters are as loved as his poems.|Keats was the son of a London livery-stable keeper; his father died in a fall when he was eight and his mother of tuberculosis when he was fourteen. He trained as a surgeon before giving his life to poetry. He nursed his brother Tom through tuberculosis, fell in love with Fanny Brawne, and in 1819 wrote, within months, his great odes, "To a Nightingale", "On a Grecian Urn" and "To Autumn". Coughing blood in 1820, he knew he was dying. He travelled to Rome for the climate and died there at 25. He asked for his gravestone to read "Here lies One Whose Name was writ in Water."
Percy Bysshe Shelley|English Romantic poet (1792–1822) of "Ozymandias" and Prometheus Unbound, radical and idealist, who drowned at 29.|Shelley, heir to a baronetcy, was expelled from Oxford for a pamphlet called "The Necessity of Atheism". He eloped at 19, and later left his first wife for Mary Godwin, the future Mary Shelley. A radical who believed in free love, vegetarianism and nonviolent protest, he wrote "The Masque of Anarchy" after the Peterloo Massacre, which later inspired Gandhi. His poems include "Ozymandias", "Ode to the West Wind" and "To a Skylark", and his essay "A Defence of Poetry" calls poets "the unacknowledged legislators of the world". He drowned in a storm off Italy, aged 29.
Samuel Taylor Coleridge|English poet and critic (1772–1834) of "The Rime of the Ancient Mariner" and "Kubla Khan", co-founder of English Romanticism.|Coleridge, the youngest of a Devon vicar's ten children, was sent to a London boarding school after his father's death. A brilliant talker, he left Cambridge, enlisted as a dragoon under a false name and was bought out by his brothers. With Wordsworth he published "Lyrical Ballads" (1798), which opened with his "The Rime of the Ancient Mariner". He also wrote "Kubla Khan", interrupted, he said, by a person from Porlock. Opium addiction, an unhappy marriage and illness troubled him. His "Biographia Literaria" and lectures on Shakespeare made him a great critic.
William Wordsworth|English Romantic poet (1770–1850) of The Prelude and "Tintern Abbey", who found the divine in nature and memory.|Wordsworth was born in Cockermouth in the Lake District. His mother died when he was eight and his father when he was thirteen. As a young man he walked across France and was swept up in the Revolution, and had a daughter with a Frenchwoman, Annette Vallon. Back in England, he lived with his devoted sister Dorothy, whose journals inspired his poems, and collaborated with Coleridge on "Lyrical Ballads". His autobiographical poem "The Prelude" traces the growth of a poet's mind through nature. He settled at Dove Cottage in Grasmere and became Poet Laureate in 1843.
Edgar Allan Poe|American poet and writer (1809–1849) of "The Raven" and macabre tales, inventor of the detective story.|Poe's actor parents left him an orphan before he was three, and he was taken in by John Allan, a Richmond merchant, with whom he later quarrelled. He was expelled from West Point. He married his young cousin Virginia Clemm, whose death from tuberculosis haunted him. Poor despite his fame for "The Raven", he worked as an editor and critic and invented the detective story with "The Murders in the Rue Morgue". His tales of horror, such as "The Fall of the House of Usher", explore guilt and madness. He was found delirious in Baltimore and died days later at 40.
James McNeill Whistler|American painter (1834–1903) in London, champion of "art for art's sake".|Whistler was born in Massachusetts and spent part of his childhood in Russia, where his father built railways. He was dismissed from West Point for failing chemistry. He moved to Paris and then London, where he became famous for his wit and quarrels. He named his paintings like music, "Nocturnes", "Symphonies" and "Arrangements", believing art should be about harmony of colour, not story. His "Arrangement in Grey and Black No. 1" is known as "Whistler's Mother". He sued the critic John Ruskin for libel in 1878, won a farthing in damages, and was bankrupted by the costs.
Wassily Kandinsky|Russian painter and theorist (1866–1944), a pioneer of abstract art, author of Concerning the Spiritual in Art.|Kandinsky was born in Moscow and became a lawyer and economics lecturer. At 30 he left to study painting in Munich, after being overwhelmed by Monet's haystacks and Wagner's "Lohengrin". He may have experienced synaesthesia, seeing colours when hearing music. He co-founded the "Blue Rider" group and painted some of the first purely abstract pictures. His book "Concerning the Spiritual in Art" (1911) argued that colour and form speak directly to the soul. He taught at the Bauhaus until the Nazis closed it, then lived in France until his death.
Walter Pater|English critic and essayist (1839–1894) whose Studies in the History of the Renaissance urged us "to burn always with this hard, gem-like flame".|Pater was born in London, the son of a doctor who died when he was a baby. He became a fellow of Brasenose College, Oxford, and lived quietly with his sisters. His book "Studies in the History of the Renaissance" (1873) ended with a famous conclusion urging readers to "burn always with this hard, gem-like flame", to seek the most intense moments of experience. It caused scandal and he removed it from the second edition, later restoring it. Oscar Wilde called the book "the golden book of spirit and sense". His novel "Marius the Epicurean" explores the same ideals.
Auguste Rodin|French sculptor (1840–1917) of The Thinker and The Kiss.|Rodin was born in Paris to a working-class family and was rejected three times by the École des Beaux-Arts. He worked for years as a decorative craftsman. When his sister died, he briefly entered a religious order. A trip to Italy, where he saw Michelangelo's work, transformed him. "The Age of Bronze" was so lifelike that critics accused him of casting a living model. His "Gates of Hell", inspired by Dante, gave rise to "The Thinker" and "The Kiss". He had a long, stormy relationship with the sculptor Camille Claudel. Rilke worked as his secretary.
Leonardo da Vinci|Italian painter, inventor and scientist (1452–1519) of the Mona Lisa and The Last Supper, whose notebooks are full of observation and wonder.|Leonardo was born in Vinci, near Florence, the illegitimate son of a notary and a peasant woman. He trained in the workshop of Verrocchio and later worked for the Duke of Milan, as painter, military engineer and designer of festivals, before ending his life in France as a guest of King Francis I. His surviving paintings are few, among them "The Last Supper" and the "Mona Lisa". His notebooks, over 7,000 surviving pages written in mirror script, explore anatomy, flight, water, optics and machines with insatiable curiosity. He is the very image of the universal mind.
Ludwig van Beethoven|German composer (1770–1827) who kept writing music after going deaf, from the Moonlight Sonata to the Ninth Symphony.|Beethoven was born in Bonn; his father, a singer and an alcoholic, drilled him harshly at the keyboard. At 21 he moved to Vienna to study with Haydn and became famous as a pianist. In his late twenties he began to lose his hearing. In 1802, in the Heiligenstadt Testament, he wrote that only his art held him back from ending his life. He went on to compose the "Eroica" Symphony, the Fifth, the opera "Fidelio", and, when totally deaf, the Ninth Symphony with its "Ode to Joy" and the late string quartets. Some 10,000 people attended his funeral.
Johann Wolfgang von Goethe|German poet, novelist, scientist and statesman (1749–1832), author of Faust and The Sorrows of Young Werther, the giant of German literature.|Goethe was born into a wealthy family in Frankfurt and studied law. At 24 he became famous across Europe with "The Sorrows of Young Werther", a novel of unhappy love. In 1775 he moved to the small court of Weimar, where he served as minister for decades, directing mines, roads and the theatre, and formed a deep friendship with Schiller. A journey to Italy renewed him. He also did serious science, discovering a bone in the human jaw and writing a theory of colours. He worked on "Faust", the story of a scholar who bargains with the devil, for some sixty years, finishing it shortly before his death.
Goethe|Johann Wolfgang von Goethe (1749–1832), German poet, novelist, scientist and statesman, author of Faust and The Sorrows of Young Werther.|Goethe was born into a wealthy family in Frankfurt and studied law. At 24 he became famous across Europe with "The Sorrows of Young Werther", a novel of unhappy love. In 1775 he moved to the small court of Weimar, where he served as minister for decades, directing mines, roads and the theatre, and formed a deep friendship with Schiller. A journey to Italy renewed him. He also did serious science, discovering a bone in the human jaw and writing a theory of colours. He worked on "Faust", the story of a scholar who bargains with the devil, for some sixty years, finishing it shortly before his death.
Joseph Conrad|Polish-born British novelist (1857–1924) of Heart of Darkness and Lord Jim, who wrote in his third language about the sea and moral darkness.|Józef Teodor Konrad Korzeniowski was born in Russian-ruled Ukraine to Polish parents; his father, a poet and patriot, was exiled to northern Russia, and both parents died by the time Józef was eleven. At 16 he went to sea, first with French ships, then British, rising to captain. A journey up the Congo River in 1890 horrified him and became "Heart of Darkness". He settled in England and, writing in his third language, produced "Lord Jim", "Nostromo" and "The Secret Agent", exploring isolation, moral failure and fidelity.
Henry James|American-British novelist (1843–1916) of The Portrait of a Lady and The Ambassadors, master of psychological subtlety.|Henry James was born in New York into a remarkable family: his father was a theologian and his brother William became a famous psychologist. He grew up between America and Europe and settled in England in 1876, becoming a British citizen in 1915 in support of the war effort. His novels, including "The Portrait of a Lady", "The Wings of the Dove" and "The Golden Bowl", study Americans in Europe and the subtle workings of consciousness. He also wrote the ghost story "The Turn of the Screw". His prefaces on the craft of fiction are still studied.
Robert Louis Stevenson|Scottish writer (1850–1894) of Treasure Island and Dr Jekyll and Mr Hyde, and of fine essays on life and travel.|Stevenson was born in Edinburgh to a family of lighthouse engineers, and was sickly from childhood with lung disease. He studied engineering and law but chose writing. He travelled by canoe and donkey through France, and crossed America to marry Fanny Osbourne, an American divorcee. He wrote "Treasure Island" for his stepson, then "Strange Case of Dr Jekyll and Mr Hyde" and "Kidnapped". Seeking a healthier climate, he settled in Samoa, where the people called him Tusitala, "teller of tales". He died there at 44 and was buried on a mountaintop.
Vincent van Gogh|Dutch painter (1853–1890). In about ten years he made more than 2,000 works, and his letters to his brother Theo are among the most moving ever written.|Van Gogh was born in the Netherlands, the son of a pastor. He worked as an art dealer, a teacher and a lay preacher among Belgian coal miners before deciding, at 27, to become an artist. His brother Theo, an art dealer, supported him for the rest of his life; their letters reveal a thoughtful, deeply feeling man. In Paris he discovered bright colour, and in Arles in the south of France he painted sunflowers, wheat fields and starry skies. After episodes of mental illness, he entered an asylum at Saint-Rémy. He sold perhaps one painting in his life and died at 37.
Lucretius|Roman poet and Epicurean philosopher (c. 99–55 BC) whose On the Nature of Things explains the world as atoms and void, and death as nothing to fear.|Almost nothing is known of the life of Titus Lucretius Carus. He wrote one work, "On the Nature of Things", a long Latin poem setting out the philosophy of Epicurus: the universe is made of atoms moving in the void, the gods do not interfere, and the soul dies with the body, so there is nothing to fear in death. Cicero admired the poem. It was nearly lost, until the book-hunter Poggio Bracciolini found a copy in a German monastery in 1417. Its rediscovery influenced Montaigne, Galileo, Newton and Thomas Jefferson.
Anaximander|Greek philosopher of Miletus (c. 610–546 BC) who taught that all things come from the boundless, the apeiron.|Anaximander of Miletus, a student of Thales, wrote what may be the first Greek prose book about nature. He proposed that everything came from the "apeiron", the boundless, and that opposites like hot and cold "pay penalty" to each other for their injustice. He suggested the Earth floats freely in space, unsupported, and that humans developed from fish-like creatures. He is said to have made the first map of the known world and to have introduced the sundial to Greece. He is often called the first scientist.
Xenophanes|Greek poet and philosopher (c. 570–478 BC) who mocked gods made in human image.|Xenophanes was born in Colophon in Ionia and left at 25, perhaps fleeing the Persian conquest, travelling as a wandering poet for nearly seventy years. He mocked the Greek gods of Homer and Hesiod for stealing and lying, and noted that Ethiopians picture their gods as dark-skinned and Thracians as blue-eyed; if horses had hands, he said, they would draw gods like horses. He proposed one god, unlike humans. He found fossil shells in mountains and concluded the land was once under the sea. He held that no one can know the full truth, though we can find out better over time.
Parmenides|Greek philosopher of Elea (5th century BC) who argued that being is one and unchanging and that change is illusion.|Parmenides came from Elea, a Greek colony in southern Italy, and is said to have written laws for his city. His one work, a poem in which a goddess reveals the truth, survives in fragments. He argued that "what is" cannot come from nothing or pass into nothing, so change and motion must be illusions of the senses. Plato describes him visiting Athens in old age and meeting the young Socrates. His student Zeno defended him with famous paradoxes. His reasoning from pure logic, against appearances, shaped all later metaphysics.
Empedocles|Greek philosopher of Sicily (c. 494–434 BC) who taught that the world is made of four elements moved by Love and Strife.|Empedocles was a poet, physician, politician and mystic from Akragas in Sicily, who claimed to be a god walking among mortals. He taught that everything is made of four "roots", earth, water, air and fire, mixed and separated by two forces, Love and Strife, in an endless cycle. This four-element theory lasted for two thousand years. He also believed in reincarnation and was a vegetarian. Legend says he leapt into the crater of Mount Etna to prove his immortality, and the volcano threw back one of his bronze sandals.
Anaxagoras|Greek philosopher (c. 500–428 BC) who taught that Mind sets the universe in order.|Anaxagoras came from Clazomenae in Ionia and was the first philosopher to settle in Athens, where he taught for thirty years and was a friend of Pericles. He proposed that Mind (nous) set the universe in motion and that everything contains a portion of everything else. He explained eclipses correctly and said the Sun was a red-hot stone larger than the Peloponnese, which led to his trial for impiety. Pericles helped him escape, and he spent his last years in Lampsacus, where he asked that children be given a holiday each year on the anniversary of his death.
Michel de Montaigne|French writer (1533–1592) who invented the essay, examining himself with honesty and humour to understand the human condition.|Montaigne was born near Bordeaux; his father had him woken each morning by music and taught only in Latin as a small child. He became a magistrate. After the death of his closest friend, Étienne de La Boétie, and his father, he retired at 38 to the tower of his château, surrounded by his library, and began writing what he called "essais", attempts. In them he studied himself frankly, from kidney stones to cannibals, friendship and death, asking "What do I know?" He served two terms as mayor of Bordeaux during the Wars of Religion. His "Essays" invented a new form of writing.
Blaise Pascal|French mathematician, physicist and religious thinker (1623–1662) whose unfinished Pensées explore human greatness and misery.|Pascal was born in Clermont, France; his mother died when he was three, and his father educated him at home. At 16 he wrote an important theorem on conic sections; at 19 he built a mechanical calculator to help with his father's tax work. He studied vacuum and pressure, and with Fermat founded probability theory. On the night of 23 November 1654 he had an intense religious experience, recorded on a note sewn into his coat. He then devoted himself to defending Christianity, writing "The Provincial Letters" and notes for an apology, published after his death at 39 as "Pensées".
David Hume|Scottish philosopher and historian (1711–1776) of the Enlightenment, a sceptic about reason, causation and the self.|Hume grew up in Scotland and entered Edinburgh University at about ten or twelve. After a breakdown from overwork, he went to France and wrote "A Treatise of Human Nature" (1739) in his twenties; it "fell dead-born from the press", he said. His later "Essays" and "History of England" were successful, but his scepticism about religion kept him from university posts. He argued that our knowledge rests on habit, not reason, that we cannot prove cause and effect, and that the self is a bundle of perceptions. Known as "le bon David" for his good nature, he faced death cheerfully, astonishing Boswell.
Augustine|Augustine of Hippo (354–430), North African bishop and theologian whose Confessions is the first great autobiography of the inner life.|Augustine was born in Thagaste, in Roman North Africa, to a pagan father and a devout Christian mother, Monica. He studied rhetoric in Carthage, lived for years with a woman he never named who bore him a son, and followed the Manichaean religion. In Milan, influenced by Bishop Ambrose and by Neoplatonism, he converted in 386 after hearing a child's voice chanting "take up and read". He became Bishop of Hippo and wrote "Confessions", often called the first autobiography, and "The City of God". He died in 430 as the Vandals besieged his city.
Sophocles|Athenian tragedian (c. 497–406 BC) of Oedipus the King and Antigone.|Sophocles was born in Colonus, near Athens, to a wealthy family. He was handsome, musical and well-liked, led the boys' chorus celebrating the victory at Salamis, and later served as a general and treasurer. He wrote over 120 plays and won first prize at the Dionysia many times, never placing lower than second. Only seven survive, including "Oedipus the King", which Aristotle considered the model tragedy, "Antigone" and "Oedipus at Colonus", written when he was about ninety. He introduced a third actor and painted scenery. He lived to about ninety.
Aeschylus|Athenian tragedian (c. 525–456 BC), the "father of tragedy", author of the Oresteia.|Aeschylus was born in Eleusis, near Athens, and fought against the Persians at Marathon, where his brother was killed. His epitaph mentions his courage at Marathon, not his plays. He is called the father of tragedy for adding a second actor, making true dialogue possible. He wrote some 70 to 90 plays; seven survive, including "The Persians" and the "Oresteia" trilogy, which traces a cycle of revenge to its end in a court of law. Legend says he died in Sicily when an eagle dropped a tortoise on his bald head, mistaking it for a stone.
Euripides|Athenian tragedian (c. 480–406 BC) of Medea and The Bacchae, known for his psychological realism.|Euripides was born on the island of Salamis, said to be on the day of the great battle in 480 BC. He was reportedly a private, bookish man who wrote in a cave by the sea. He won first prize only four times in his life, but his plays became the most popular after his death; nineteen survive, more than for any other Greek tragedian. "Medea", "The Trojan Women" and "The Bacchae" show human passion, the suffering of women and the cruelty of war with startling realism. He left Athens late in life and died in Macedonia.
W. B. Yeats|Irish poet and dramatist (1865–1939) of "The Second Coming" and "Sailing to Byzantium", whose verse blends myth, mysticism and politics. Nobel Prize 1923.|William Butler Yeats was born in Dublin, the son of the painter John Butler Yeats, and spent childhood summers in County Sligo, whose landscape filled his poems. He was fascinated by Irish legend, folklore and the occult. He fell hopelessly in love with the revolutionary Maud Gonne, who refused his proposals. With Lady Gregory he founded the Abbey Theatre. He served as a senator of the new Irish Free State and won the Nobel Prize in 1923. Unusually, his greatest poems, like "Sailing to Byzantium" and "The Second Coming", came after he was fifty.
John Milton|English poet (1608–1674) who, blind, dictated Paradise Lost, the epic of the Fall.|Milton was born in London, the son of a composer and scrivener, and studied at Cambridge, then read for years at home. He supported Parliament against King Charles I and wrote pamphlets for freedom of divorce and of the press ("Areopagitica"). He served as Latin secretary to Cromwell's government. By 1652 he was totally blind. After the monarchy was restored he was briefly imprisoned and lost much of his wealth. He then dictated "Paradise Lost" to his daughters and assistants: an epic of Satan's rebellion and the fall of Adam and Eve, written "to justify the ways of God to men".
T. S. Eliot|American-British poet (1888–1965) of The Waste Land and Four Quartets. Nobel Prize 1948.|Thomas Stearns Eliot was born in St Louis, Missouri, and studied philosophy at Harvard, the Sorbonne and Oxford. He settled in England, worked for eight years at Lloyds Bank, and married Vivienne Haigh-Wood, an unhappy marriage marked by illness. "The Love Song of J. Alfred Prufrock" and "The Waste Land" (1922) changed modern poetry. He became a director at the publisher Faber and Faber, joined the Church of England and took British citizenship in 1927. "Four Quartets" is his great meditation on time and faith. He won the Nobel Prize in 1948.
Friedrich Hölderlin|German poet (1770–1843) of hymns and elegies longing for the lost gods of Greece, who spent his last decades in mental illness.|Hölderlin studied theology at the seminary in Tübingen, rooming with Hegel and Schelling. Rather than become a pastor, he worked as a tutor in wealthy households. In Frankfurt he fell in love with Susette Gontard, his employer's wife, his "Diotima"; he was dismissed, and her death in 1802 devastated him. He wrote the novel "Hyperion" and hymns and elegies longing for the lost gods of Greece. Diagnosed as insane in 1806, he lived his last 36 years in a tower room in Tübingen, cared for by a carpenter's family, still writing short poems.
Thomas Hardy|English novelist and poet (1840–1928) of Tess of the d'Urbervilles and Jude the Obscure, whose poems face loss and indifferent fate.|Hardy was born in a Dorset cottage, the son of a stonemason, and trained as an architect. His novels set in "Wessex", his version of south-west England, include "Far from the Madding Crowd", "Tess of the d'Urbervilles" and "Jude the Obscure". After the outrage over "Jude", he gave up fiction and wrote only poetry for the last thirty years of his life. His first wife Emma's death in 1912 inspired some of his finest love poems. His heart was buried with Emma in Stinsford and his ashes in Westminster Abbey.
Meister Eckhart|German Dominican mystic and theologian (c. 1260–1328) who taught letting go and the birth of God in the soul.|Eckhart von Hochheim was a German Dominican friar who taught theology in Paris, held senior posts in his order and became a popular preacher in German. His sermons spoke of the birth of God in the soul, of "letting go" (Gelassenheit) and of a ground of the soul where God and soul are one. Some of his statements were judged heretical; he defended himself before the pope's court but died around 1328 before the verdict. He influenced later mystics, Hegel, Heidegger, Erich Fromm and modern readers interested in Zen.
Julian of Norwich|English anchoress and mystic (c. 1343–after 1416), the first woman known to write a book in English, Revelations of Divine Love: "All shall be well."|Julian was an English anchoress, a woman who lived walled into a cell attached to St Julian's Church in Norwich, from which her name probably comes. In May 1373, at about thirty, seriously ill and thought to be dying, she received sixteen visions of Christ's suffering and love. She recovered and spent decades reflecting on them. Her "Revelations of Divine Love" is the earliest surviving book in English known to be written by a woman. It speaks of God's motherly love and contains her assurance that "all shall be well, and all shall be well, and all manner of thing shall be well".
Thomas à Kempis|German-Dutch monk (c. 1380–1471) traditionally credited with The Imitation of Christ, a classic of Christian devotion.|Thomas Hemerken was born in Kempen, Germany, and studied with the Brothers of the Common Life at Deventer, part of a movement called the Modern Devotion that emphasised inner piety. He became a canon at the monastery of Mount St Agnes near Zwolle in the Netherlands, where he spent the rest of his long life copying manuscripts, writing and guiding novices. "The Imitation of Christ", generally attributed to him, is after the Bible perhaps the most widely read Christian book, a gentle guide to humility and the inner life.
Brother Lawrence|French Carmelite lay brother (c. 1614–1691) whose The Practice of the Presence of God found God among the pots and pans of the monastery kitchen.|Nicolas Herman was born in Lorraine, France, served as a soldier in the Thirty Years' War and was badly wounded. A vision of a tree in winter, trusting that spring would return, led to his conversion. He became a lay brother at the Carmelite monastery in Paris and worked for decades in the kitchen and later repairing sandals. He practised being aware of God's presence in every task, even among pots and pans. After his death, his letters and conversations were collected as "The Practice of the Presence of God".
Thomas Traherne|English poet and clergyman (1636–1674) whose Centuries of Meditations, rediscovered in 1896, are full of childlike wonder at the world.|Traherne was the son of a shoemaker in Hereford, studied at Oxford and became a rural parish priest and later chaplain to a nobleman. He died at about 37, almost unknown. His greatest work, "Centuries of Meditations", was discovered in manuscript on a London bookstall in 1896 and first thought to be by Henry Vaughan. It celebrates the wonder of childhood vision and the world as a gift: "You never enjoy the world aright, till the sea itself floweth in your veins." Other manuscripts have been found as recently as 1997.
William Law|English priest and writer (1686–1761) of A Serious Call to a Devout and Holy Life.|Law studied at Cambridge and was ordained, but in 1714 he refused to swear allegiance to the new king, George I, and lost his fellowship and any chance of advancement. He became tutor to the father of the historian Edward Gibbon. His book "A Serious Call to a Devout and Holy Life" (1729) deeply influenced John Wesley and Samuel Johnson. In later life he retired to his birthplace, King's Cliffe, where with two women companions he ran schools and almshouses and studied the mystic Jacob Boehme.
Emily Brontë|English novelist and poet (1818–1848) of Wuthering Heights.|Emily Brontë grew up in Haworth parsonage on the Yorkshire moors, where her mother and two older sisters died when she was small. She and her siblings created imaginary worlds; she and Anne wrote of "Gondal". Shy and attached to home, she was miserable whenever she left it. She loved the moors and her dog Keeper. Her only novel, "Wuthering Heights" (1847), published under the name Ellis Bell, was considered savage at the time and is now one of the great English novels. Her poems are also admired. She died of tuberculosis at 30, refusing medical help until the end.
Charlotte Brontë|English novelist (1816–1855) of Jane Eyre and Villette.|Charlotte, the eldest surviving Brontë sister, was sent to a harsh school where her two older sisters caught the illness that killed them, an experience she put into "Jane Eyre". She worked as a teacher and governess and studied in Brussels, where she fell in love with her married teacher. "Jane Eyre" (1847), published as by Currer Bell, was an immediate success. Within a year her brother Branwell and sisters Emily and Anne all died. She wrote "Shirley" and "Villette", married her father's curate Arthur Bell Nicholls and died during pregnancy at 38.
Anne Brontë|English novelist and poet (1820–1849) of Agnes Grey and The Tenant of Wildfell Hall.|Anne, the youngest Brontë, worked for years as a governess, experiences she used in her first novel, "Agnes Grey". Her second novel, "The Tenant of Wildfell Hall" (1848), about a woman who leaves her abusive, alcoholic husband and supports herself as an artist, was a sensation and is considered one of the first feminist novels. Charlotte later prevented its republication. Anne was gentler in manner than her sisters but determined to tell uncomfortable truths. She died of tuberculosis at 29 in Scarborough, where she had gone to see the sea.
Ludwig Wittgenstein|Austrian-British philosopher (1889–1951) of the Tractatus and the Philosophical Investigations, on the limits and uses of language.|Wittgenstein was born into one of the richest families in Vienna, where Brahms played in their home; three of his brothers took their own lives. He studied engineering, then logic with Bertrand Russell in Cambridge. He wrote the "Tractatus Logico-Philosophicus" while a soldier in the First World War, believing it solved philosophy's problems. He gave away his fortune and worked as a village schoolteacher and gardener. Returning to Cambridge, he rejected his earlier views, and his "Philosophical Investigations" was published after his death. His last words were "Tell them I've had a wonderful life."
Alan Watts|British-American writer (1915–1973) who brought Zen and Daoism to Western readers.|Watts was born in Chislehurst, England, and became fascinated by Asian culture as a boy. At 15 he became secretary of the Buddhist Lodge in London and at 20 published "The Spirit of Zen". He moved to America in 1938, became an Episcopal priest, then left the church. In California he taught comparative philosophy and became a popular broadcaster on public radio, explaining Zen, Taoism and Hinduism to Western audiences with humour and clarity. His books include "The Wisdom of Insecurity" and "The Way of Zen". His talks remain widely listened to.
J. Krishnamurti|Indian philosopher and teacher (1895–1986) who rejected all authority in spiritual matters and urged direct self-awareness.|Jiddu Krishnamurti was born in a small town in southern India. As a boy he was "discovered" on a beach by leaders of the Theosophical Society, who raised him to be a coming World Teacher and founded an organisation, the Order of the Star, around him. In 1929, aged 34, he dissolved it, declaring that "truth is a pathless land" and that no organisation or guru can lead anyone to it. For the next 56 years he travelled and spoke worldwide, urging people to observe their own minds without authority. He founded schools in India, England and California.
Iris Murdoch|Irish-British novelist and philosopher (1919–1999) who wrote about goodness, attention and love.|Murdoch was born in Dublin and grew up in London, an only child. She studied classics and philosophy at Oxford, worked for refugees after the war and taught philosophy at Oxford. She wrote 26 novels, including "Under the Net", "The Bell" and "The Sea, the Sea", which won the Booker Prize in 1978. Her philosophical book "The Sovereignty of Good" argues that moral life is about attention, "unselfing", seeing other people justly and lovingly. She was married for over forty years to the critic John Bayley, who wrote about her decline with Alzheimer's disease.
Ernest Becker|American anthropologist (1924–1974) whose The Denial of Death argued that our cultures are defences against mortality.|Becker was born in Massachusetts to Jewish immigrant parents. As a young soldier in the Second World War he helped liberate a Nazi concentration camp. He studied anthropology and taught at several universities, being dismissed from some for his unconventional views. In "The Denial of Death" (1973) he argued that much of human culture, from heroism to religion, is a defence against the terror of mortality. He died of colon cancer in March 1974, two months before it won the Pulitzer Prize. His ideas inspired "terror management theory" in psychology.
Hafiz|Persian poet (c. 1315–1390) of Shiraz, master of the ghazal, whose poems of wine, love and the divine are still recited across Iran.|Shams al-Din Muhammad Hafiz was born in Shiraz, Persia; his title Hafiz means one who has memorised the Qur'an. Legend says he worked in a bakery as a young man and spent forty nights in vigil to win poetic gifts. He became a court poet, falling in and out of favour with changing rulers. His "Divan", about 500 ghazals, on love, wine and the divine, is found in nearly every Iranian home and is used for fortune-telling. Goethe admired him deeply. Many popular English "Hafiz" poems are free modern versions rather than translations.
Al-Ghazali|Persian theologian and mystic (c. 1058–1111) who left a brilliant career to seek certainty in Sufism.|Abu Hamid al-Ghazali was born in Tus, Persia. Brilliant and famous, he became head of the Nizamiyya college in Baghdad in his thirties. Then, in 1095, a spiritual crisis left him unable to speak or teach. He gave away his wealth and wandered for ten years as a Sufi, seeking direct experience of God. His autobiography "Deliverance from Error" describes this journey from doubt to certainty. His "Revival of the Religious Sciences" brought Sufism into mainstream Islam, and "The Incoherence of the Philosophers" challenged Aristotelian philosophy. He is among the most influential Muslim thinkers.
Sa'di|Saadi of Shiraz (c. 1210–1292), Persian poet of the Gulistan (The Rose Garden) and the Bustan (The Orchard).|Saadi was born in Shiraz and studied in Baghdad at the Nizamiyya college. During the turmoil of the Mongol invasions he travelled for decades through Iraq, Syria, Arabia and beyond; one story tells of his capture by Crusaders and forced labour digging trenches. Returning to Shiraz around 1257, he wrote the "Bustan" (The Orchard) in verse and the "Gulistan" (The Rose Garden) in prose and verse, full of witty, humane tales. Lines from his poem "Bani Adam", that all humans are members of one body, are displayed at the United Nations.
Saadi|Persian poet of Shiraz (c. 1210–1292) whose Gulistan (The Rose Garden) and Bustan (The Orchard) mix tales and verse on wisdom, love and humanity.|Saadi was born in Shiraz and studied in Baghdad at the Nizamiyya college. During the turmoil of the Mongol invasions he travelled for decades through Iraq, Syria, Arabia and beyond; one story tells of his capture by Crusaders and forced labour digging trenches. Returning to Shiraz around 1257, he wrote the "Bustan" (The Orchard) in verse and the "Gulistan" (The Rose Garden) in prose and verse, full of witty, humane tales. Lines from his poem "Bani Adam", that all humans are members of one body, are displayed at the United Nations.
Jami|Persian poet and Sufi (1414–1492), often called the last great classical Persian poet.|Nur al-Din Abd al-Rahman Jami was born in Khorasan and lived mostly in Herat, the brilliant capital of the Timurid dynasty, where he was honoured by rulers and artists. He was a scholar and Sufi of the Naqshbandi order. He is considered the last great classical Persian poet. His works include "Haft Awrang" (Seven Thrones), containing the romance of Yusuf and Zulaikha, and "Baharistan" (Spring Garden), modelled on Saadi's Gulistan. His prose "Lawa'ih" (Flashes of Light) explains Sufi teachings on divine unity.
Ibn 'Arabi|Andalusian Sufi mystic and philosopher (1165–1240) who taught the unity of being.|Muhyiddin Ibn Arabi was born in Murcia in Muslim Spain and had visionary experiences as a young man; the philosopher Averroes once sought him out to meet him. He travelled across North Africa, then went east on pilgrimage to Mecca, where he began his vast "Meccan Revelations", and settled in Damascus. He wrote hundreds of works, including "The Bezels of Wisdom". He taught the "unity of being" and the endless self-disclosure of God in creation. His poem "The Interpreter of Desires" declares that his heart has become capable of every form. Sufis call him "the Greatest Master".
Matsuo Bashō|Japanese poet (1644–1694), the great master of haiku, who travelled Japan and wrote The Narrow Road to the Deep North.|Matsuo Kinsaku was born near Kyoto to a family of low-ranking samurai and served a young lord who shared his love of poetry. When his lord died, he moved to Edo (Tokyo) and became a poetry master. His students planted a banana plant (bashō) by his hut, which gave him his pen name. He turned the playful opening verse of linked poetry into a serious art, the form now called haiku, and studied Zen. He made long journeys on foot through Japan, writing travel diaries mixing prose and haiku, including "The Narrow Road to the Deep North". He died on a journey in Osaka.
Yosa Buson|Japanese poet and painter (1716–1784), one of the great haiku masters after Bashō.|Buson was born in a village near Osaka and moved to Edo at about twenty to study painting and poetry. After his teacher's death he wandered northern Japan for years, following in Bashō's footsteps. He settled in Kyoto, married and had a daughter, and became one of the leading painters of his day in the literati style, often combining paintings with haiku (haiga). His haiku are vivid and pictorial, like small paintings. He led a revival of haiku in Bashō's spirit, and with Bashō and Issa is considered one of its three great masters.
Kaga no Chiyo|Japanese poet (1703–1775), one of the finest women haiku poets.|Chiyo-ni was born in Matto, in Kaga province, Japan, the daughter of a picture-framer, and studied haiku from childhood with a student of Bashō's school. She became famous across Japan while still young. At about fifty she became a Buddhist nun, taking the name Soen. Her most famous haiku tells of finding her well-bucket entangled by a morning-glory vine, and borrowing water rather than disturb it. She is one of the most celebrated women haiku poets, and her poems were carried as gifts by Japanese envoys to Korea.
Saigyō|Japanese Buddhist monk and poet (1118–1190) of wandering, moon and cherry blossoms.|Satō Norikiyo was born into a warrior family and served as a guard to a retired emperor. At 22, he suddenly left his post, his wife and children, and became a Buddhist monk, taking the name Saigyō, "western journey", toward the western paradise. He lived in mountain huts and travelled across Japan, writing waka poems about the moon, cherry blossoms and loneliness. Ninety-four of his poems were chosen for the great anthology "Shin Kokinshū". He hoped to die under cherry blossoms at the full moon of spring, and is said to have done so. Bashō revered him.
Sutta Nipata|One of the oldest collections of the Buddha's discourses in the Pali Canon.|The Sutta Nipāta, "Group of Discourses", is a collection of 71 suttas in the Pali Canon, mostly in verse. Scholars consider parts of it, especially the "Chapter of Eights" and "Chapter on the Way to the Far Shore", among the oldest Buddhist texts. It includes the Metta Sutta on loving-kindness, the "Rhinoceros Sutta", which urges wandering alone like a rhinoceros, and dialogues with seekers. Its tone is simple and direct, giving a sense of the Buddha's early wandering community before monasteries were established.
Therigatha|"Verses of the Elder Nuns", poems by the first Buddhist women, among the oldest women's writing in the world.|The Therīgāthā, "Verses of the Elder Nuns", is a collection of 73 poems in the Pali Canon, attributed to early Buddhist women who had reached awakening. Composed orally from the 6th century BC and written down around the 1st century BC, it is among the oldest surviving collections of women's literature. The nuns came from every background, queens, courtesans, slaves and grieving mothers like Kisa Gotami, and speak of freedom from household drudgery, grief and desire. Their voices are vivid, joyful and often fiercely honest.
Theragatha|"Verses of the Elder Monks", poems by the Buddha's early disciples on their path to freedom.|The Theragāthā, "Verses of the Elder Monks", is a companion to the Therigatha in the Pali Canon, with 264 poems attributed to early disciples of the Buddha. The monks describe their lives before and after renunciation and the peace of awakening, often with lovely images of nature: rain on a hut roof, peacocks calling, forests and rivers. Some, like the verses of the former robber Angulimala, tell remarkable stories of transformation. They offer a picture of the first Buddhist community in its own voices.
Psalms|The book of 150 songs and prayers in the Hebrew Bible, from praise to lament.|The Book of Psalms is a collection of 150 songs and prayers in the Hebrew Bible, gathered over many centuries; about half are attributed to King David. They range from praise and thanksgiving to lament, anger and despair, including "The Lord is my shepherd" (Psalm 23) and "Out of the depths I cry to you" (Psalm 130). They were sung in the Jerusalem Temple and remain at the heart of Jewish and Christian worship; monks chant the whole book each week. Their honesty about every human feeling has made them a companion in joy and grief for thousands of years.
Proverbs|A book of the Hebrew Bible collecting sayings of practical and moral wisdom.|The Book of Proverbs, in the Hebrew Bible, collects short sayings of practical and moral wisdom, traditionally attributed to King Solomon though gathered from several sources, including Egyptian wisdom texts. It praises hard work, honest speech, generosity and self-control, and warns against laziness, pride and folly. In its early chapters, Wisdom is portrayed as a woman calling in the streets, present with God at creation. It ends with a poem in praise of a capable, wise woman.
Song of Songs|A book of love poetry in the Hebrew Bible, read by Jews and Christians as an allegory of divine love.|The Song of Songs, also called the Song of Solomon, is a short book of love poetry in the Hebrew Bible, a dialogue of desire between a woman and her beloved, full of gardens, vineyards, spices and springtime. God is never mentioned. Jewish tradition read it as an allegory of God's love for Israel; Christian mystics such as Bernard of Clairvaux and John of the Cross read it as the soul's love for God. Its line "love is strong as death" is among the most quoted about love.
Book of Wisdom|The Wisdom of Solomon, a Jewish book of the 1st century BC on wisdom and righteousness.|The Wisdom of Solomon was probably written in Greek by a Jewish author in Alexandria, Egypt, in the 1st century BC. It is part of the Catholic and Orthodox Bibles and is included in the Apocrypha in Protestant Bibles. Speaking in the voice of Solomon, it praises Wisdom as a radiant, divine presence, "a breath of the power of God", and affirms that the souls of the righteous are in the hand of God. It blends Jewish faith with Greek philosophical ideas.
Sirach|The Wisdom of Ben Sira, a Jewish book of practical wisdom from about 180 BC.|The Wisdom of Ben Sira, also called Ecclesiasticus, was written in Hebrew in Jerusalem around 180 BC by the scribe Jesus ben Sira, and translated into Greek by his grandson. It is part of the Catholic and Orthodox Bibles. It offers advice on friendship, family, speech, work and respect for the old, and contains a famous hymn beginning "Let us now praise famous men", ending with those who have no memorial but whose good deeds live on.
Charles Bukowski|German-born American poet and novelist (1920–1994) of raw, unsentimental poems about the down and out.|Bukowski was born in Germany and grew up in Los Angeles, beaten regularly by his father and scarred by severe acne. He drifted through odd jobs and cities, drinking heavily, then worked for years as a postal clerk, writing poems and stories about the poor, drunks and losers. At 49 the publisher John Martin offered him a monthly income to quit the post office; within weeks he wrote his first novel, "Post Office". He wrote thousands of poems in a raw, plain style. His gravestone reads "Don't try".
Anaïs Nin|French-Cuban-American writer (1903–1977) famous for her diaries.|Nin was born near Paris to a Cuban pianist father who abandoned the family when she was eleven. On the ship to New York, she began a diary that she kept for over sixty years. In Paris in the 1930s she befriended and loved Henry Miller, and helped finance his first book. She studied psychoanalysis with Otto Rank. Her published "Diary", beginning in 1966, made her famous, especially with the women's movement. She also wrote novels and erotica. She lived for years with two husbands on opposite coasts of America.
La Rochefoucauld|French nobleman and moralist (1613–1680) whose Maxims expose the self-love behind our virtues.|François VI, Duke of La Rochefoucauld, was a French nobleman who fought in wars and took part in the Fronde, a revolt of nobles against the crown, in which he was badly wounded. Disillusioned, he retired from public life and frequented the salons of Paris, where clever conversation was an art. His "Maxims" (1665), over 500 short sayings, argue that self-love lies behind most of our virtues: "Our virtues are most frequently but vices disguised." He was a lifelong friend of Madame de La Fayette.
Chamfort|Nicolas Chamfort (1741–1794), French writer of bitter, witty maxims about society.|Sébastien-Roch Nicolas, called Chamfort, was born the illegitimate son of a noblewoman and was raised by a grocer in Auvergne. He won literary prizes and became a successful playwright and member of the French Academy, admired in the salons. He supported the Revolution, then turned against the Terror and was threatened with arrest. He tried to take his own life rather than return to prison and died of his wounds months later. His "Maxims and Anecdotes", published after his death, are bitterly witty about society, love and human nature.
Joseph Joubert|French moralist (1754–1824) whose notebooks of luminous thoughts were published after his death.|Joubert was born in Montignac in south-west France, taught in a religious school and moved to Paris, where he became a friend of Diderot and Chateaubriand. He spent his life reading, conversing and writing in notebooks, publishing nothing. He served as a justice of the peace and an inspector of universities. He had poor health and often stayed in bed. After his death, Chateaubriand published a selection of his thoughts, later expanded as "Pensées". Joubert wrote that he wished "to put a whole book into a page, a whole page into a phrase, and that phrase into a word".
Georg Christoph Lichtenberg|German physicist and aphorist (1742–1799) whose "waste books" are full of wit and insight.|Lichtenberg was the youngest of seventeen children of a pastor near Darmstadt. A spinal deformity left him short and hunchbacked. He became a professor of physics at Göttingen, a brilliant and popular lecturer, discovering the branching electrical patterns called Lichtenberg figures and installing the first lightning rods in Göttingen. He kept notebooks he called "waste books", full of witty, sceptical observations on science, psychology and human folly, published after his death. Tolstoy, Nietzsche, Freud and Wittgenstein admired them.
Sir Thomas Browne|English physician and writer (1605–1682) of Religio Medici and Urn Burial, famed for his rich prose and curiosity.|Browne was born in London, studied medicine at Oxford, Montpellier, Padua and Leiden, and practised as a physician in Norwich for the rest of his life. He was knighted by Charles II in 1671. "Religio Medici" (The Religion of a Doctor), written for himself in his late twenties, explores faith and reason with tolerance and wonder. "Pseudodoxia Epidemica" examined popular errors, such as whether elephants have joints. "Urn Burial", prompted by urns dug up in a Norfolk field, is a meditation on death and oblivion in some of the richest prose in English.
Miguel de Unamuno|Spanish philosopher and novelist (1864–1936) of The Tragic Sense of Life.|Unamuno was born in Bilbao, in the Basque Country, and became professor of Greek and later rector of the University of Salamanca. He wrote novels, poems, plays and essays, above all "The Tragic Sense of Life" (1913), about the conflict between reason, which denies immortality, and the heart, which longs for it. He was exiled to the Canary Islands for criticising the dictator Primo de Rivera. In 1936, at Salamanca, he publicly rebuked the Francoist general Millán-Astray, saying "You will win, but you will not convince." He died under house arrest weeks later.
William James|American philosopher and psychologist (1842–1910), founder of pragmatism and author of The Varieties of Religious Experience.|William James was born in New York, the elder brother of the novelist Henry James. He studied painting, then medicine, and joined an expedition to Brazil. In his twenties he fell into a deep depression and recovered partly by deciding to believe in free will. He taught at Harvard, where he created one of the first psychology laboratories, and wrote "The Principles of Psychology" (1890), which coined the phrase "stream of consciousness". With Peirce he developed pragmatism. "The Varieties of Religious Experience" studies mystical experience with sympathy. He was a warm, curious and much-loved teacher.
Marcel Proust|French novelist (1871–1922) of In Search of Lost Time, the great novel of memory.|Proust was born in Paris, the son of a famous doctor, and suffered severe asthma from the age of nine. As a young man he moved in aristocratic salons, appearing to be a dilettante. After his mother's death in 1905 he withdrew more and more, living in a cork-lined bedroom, sleeping by day and writing by night. His seven-volume novel "In Search of Lost Time", begun around 1909, explores memory, love, jealousy, society and art, sparked famously by the taste of a madeleine dipped in tea. He died in 1922 still correcting the final volumes.
C. P. Cavafy|Greek poet of Alexandria (1863–1933) of "Ithaka" and "Waiting for the Barbarians".|Constantine Cavafy was born in Alexandria, Egypt, to Greek parents. After his father's death, the family lived for some years in England and Constantinople. He spent most of his life in Alexandria, working for thirty years as a clerk in the Irrigation Service, and lived with his mother. He published his poems only in pamphlets and broadsheets for friends, refining them for years. His poems, about history, desire, and remembered love between men, are plain, ironic and moving. "Ithaka" and "Waiting for the Barbarians" are known worldwide. E. M. Forster championed him.
John Donne|English poet and preacher (1572–1631), the greatest of the metaphysical poets: "No man is an island."|Donne was born in London to a Catholic family when Catholics were persecuted; his brother died in prison for sheltering a priest. He sailed with Essex against Cádiz and wrote witty, passionate love poems. His secret marriage to Anne More, niece of his employer, ruined his career and briefly landed him in jail. After years of poverty, he converted to the Church of England, was ordained and became Dean of St Paul's, one of the great preachers of his age. Anne died after their twelfth child. His "Devotions", written during serious illness, contains "No man is an island".
George Herbert|English poet and priest (1593–1633) of The Temple.|Herbert came from a noble Welsh-English family and was a star at Cambridge, becoming its Public Orator, seemingly destined for court. Instead he was ordained and, in 1630, became rector of the tiny parish of Bemerton near Salisbury, caring for his parishioners and rebuilding the church. He died of tuberculosis less than three years later, at 39. On his deathbed he sent his poems to a friend, saying to publish them if they might help "any dejected poor soul". "The Temple" became one of the most loved books of religious poetry in English.
Andrew Marvell|English poet (1621–1678) of "To His Coy Mistress" and "The Garden".|Marvell was born in Yorkshire, the son of a clergyman, and studied at Cambridge. He travelled in Europe, then worked as tutor to the daughter of the Parliamentarian general Lord Fairfax, where he probably wrote "Upon Appleton House" and "The Garden". He became assistant to Milton as Latin secretary under Cromwell, and after the Restoration helped save Milton from execution. He served as a Member of Parliament for Hull for nearly twenty years and wrote satires against corruption. "To His Coy Mistress" is among the most famous love poems in English.
Henry Vaughan|Welsh poet and physician (1621–1695) of mystical poems of light and eternity.|Vaughan was born in Breconshire, Wales, and called himself "the Silurist" after the ancient tribe of the region. He studied at Oxford and in London, probably fought for the King in the Civil War and practised as a country doctor for most of his life in his native valley of the Usk. The death of his younger brother William and his reading of George Herbert transformed his writing. "Silex Scintillans" (The Flashing Flint) contains mystical poems such as "The World", which begins "I saw Eternity the other night".
Gerard Manley Hopkins|English poet and Jesuit priest (1844–1889) whose sprung rhythm celebrates "the dearest freshness deep down things".|Hopkins was born in Essex and studied classics brilliantly at Oxford, where he converted to Catholicism. He became a Jesuit priest and burned his early poems, but in 1875 wrote "The Wreck of the Deutschland" about nuns lost in a shipwreck, inventing "sprung rhythm". His poems of nature, such as "The Windhover" and "Pied Beauty", see God's glory in the "inscape" of every thing. As a professor in Dublin, overworked and lonely, he wrote the dark "terrible sonnets". He died of typhoid at 44. His friend Robert Bridges published his poems in 1918.
Matthew Arnold|English poet and critic (1822–1888) of "Dover Beach".|Arnold was the son of Thomas Arnold, the famous headmaster of Rugby School. He was a poet, critic and, for 35 years, an inspector of schools, travelling widely to see how ordinary children were educated. His poems include "Dover Beach", with its image of the "sea of faith" withdrawing. In "Culture and Anarchy" he defined culture as "the best which has been thought and said", and urged its spread against narrow materialism. He was Professor of Poetry at Oxford, lecturing in English rather than Latin.
Robert Browning|English poet (1812–1889), master of the dramatic monologue.|Browning grew up in Camberwell, London, educated largely in his father's library of 6,000 books. His early poems were little noticed. In 1845 he wrote to the famous poet Elizabeth Barrett, an invalid, and they fell in love; they married secretly and fled to Italy, defying her tyrannical father. They lived in Florence until her death in 1861. He perfected the dramatic monologue, as in "My Last Duchess" and "Andrea del Sarto". His long poem "The Ring and the Book" finally brought him fame. He died in Venice.
Christina Rossetti|English poet (1830–1894) of "Goblin Market" and devotional verse.|Christina Rossetti grew up in London in a family of Italian exiles; her brother Dante Gabriel Rossetti was a founder of the Pre-Raphaelite Brotherhood, and she modelled for his paintings. A devout Anglican, she twice refused marriage on religious grounds. She volunteered for years at a home for "fallen women". "Goblin Market" (1862) made her famous. She wrote poems of love, longing and faith, nursery rhymes, and the Christmas carol "In the Bleak Midwinter". She suffered from Graves' disease and later cancer.
Arthur Hugh Clough|English poet (1819–1861) of doubt and quiet hope.|Clough spent his early childhood in Charleston, South Carolina, then was sent to Rugby School under Thomas Arnold, where he was the star pupil, and to Oxford. Religious doubts led him to resign his Oxford fellowship rather than affirm the Church of England's articles. He worked as head of a university hall in London, as an examiner in the Education Office, and helped his wife's cousin Florence Nightingale with her work. His poems, including "Say not the Struggle nought Availeth" and "Amours de Voyage", are honest about doubt. He died in Florence at 42; Matthew Arnold mourned him in "Thyrsis".
Algernon Charles Swinburne|English poet (1837–1909) known for his musical, rebellious verse.|Swinburne was born into an aristocratic family in London and grew up on the Isle of Wight. He left Oxford without a degree. "Poems and Ballads" (1866), with its musical, sensual and anti-religious verse, caused a scandal and made him famous. He was a friend of the Pre-Raphaelites and championed Blake and Baudelaire. Heavy drinking ruined his health, and in 1879 his friend Theodore Watts-Dunton took him into his home in Putney, where he lived quietly for thirty years. "The Garden of Proserpine" is among his best-known poems.
Lord Byron|English Romantic poet (1788–1824) of Don Juan, who died aiding Greek independence.|George Gordon Byron inherited his title at ten and was born with a club foot. "Childe Harold's Pilgrimage" (1812) made him an overnight celebrity: "I awoke one morning and found myself famous." Scandals, including rumours of an affair with his half-sister, drove him from England in 1816, never to return. In Switzerland and Italy he wrote his comic masterpiece "Don Juan". He gave money and his life to the Greek war of independence, dying of fever at Missolonghi at 36, and is still honoured as a hero in Greece. His daughter was the mathematician Ada Lovelace.
Stéphane Mallarmé|French Symbolist poet (1842–1898).|Mallarmé was born in Paris; his mother and sister died when he was young. He taught English in provincial and Paris schools for most of his life, a job he disliked. His Tuesday evening gatherings at his apartment, the "mardis", drew Verlaine, Gide, Valéry, Rilke and Debussy. His difficult, musical poems, such as "L'Après-midi d'un faune", which inspired Debussy's prelude, aimed to suggest rather than describe: "to name an object is to suppress three-quarters of the pleasure". His typographic poem "A Throw of the Dice" anticipated modern poetry.
Paul Verlaine|French Symbolist poet (1844–1896) of musical, melancholy verse.|Verlaine was born in Metz, the only child of an army officer. He worked as a clerk at Paris city hall and published early poems such as "Fêtes galantes". His marriage collapsed when he began a stormy relationship with the teenage Arthur Rimbaud; in Brussels in 1873 he shot Rimbaud in the wrist and spent two years in prison, where he returned to Catholicism. His musical poems, like "Chanson d'automne", begin "De la musique avant toute chose", music before everything. Despite fame among the young, he died poor and alcoholic in Paris.
Arthur Rimbaud|French poet (1854–1891) who wrote all his work before twenty-one, including A Season in Hell.|Rimbaud was born in Charleville in northern France; his father, an army captain, left when he was six. A brilliant, rebellious student, he wrote "The Drunken Boat" at 16 and ran away to Paris, where he lived with Verlaine. He declared that the poet must become a "seer" through a "long, immense and reasoned derangement of all the senses". He wrote "A Season in Hell" and "Illuminations" before abandoning poetry entirely at about twenty. He became a trader in Aden and Ethiopia, and died in Marseilles at 37 after his leg was amputated.
T'ao Ch'ien|Tao Yuanming (365–427), Chinese poet who left office for a life of farming, wine and chrysanthemums.|Tao Yuanming, also called Tao Qian, came from a family of officials in decline. He took minor government posts to support his family but hated their servility, saying he would not bend his back for five pecks of rice. At about forty he resigned for good and returned to farm, a choice celebrated in "Returning Home". His poems of plain country life, chrysanthemums by the fence, wine and friendship, were little valued in his time but later admired as the ideal of the recluse. His story "Peach Blossom Spring" gave China its image of a hidden paradise.
Li Po|Li Bai (701–762), Tang poet of wine, the moon and wandering.|Li Bai (Li Po) was born probably in Central Asia and grew up in Sichuan. He wandered China as a young man, practising swordsmanship and Daoism. In about 742 he was summoned to the court of Emperor Xuanzong in Chang'an, but his drinking and freedom got him dismissed after two years. He met Du Fu, who admired him. During the An Lushan rebellion he joined the wrong prince and was exiled, later pardoned. Legend says he drowned trying to embrace the moon's reflection from a boat. His poems of wine, moonlight, friendship and mountains are among the best loved in China.
Po Chü-i|Bai Juyi (772–846), Tang poet of plain, humane verse.|Bai Juyi (Po Chü-i) passed the imperial examinations young and served as an official, rising and falling in favour. He believed poetry should serve the people and criticised injustice; he is said to have read his poems to an old peasant woman and changed anything she couldn't understand. Demoted for outspoken memorials, he served as governor of Hangzhou and Suzhou, building a causeway on West Lake still named after him. His long poems "Song of Everlasting Sorrow" and "Song of the Pipa" were famous across East Asia. In old age he became a devout Buddhist.
Wang Wei|Tang poet and painter (699–759) of quiet Buddhist landscapes.|Wang Wei passed the imperial examination at about twenty and served as a court official, rising high. He was also a celebrated painter, said to have founded the southern school of landscape painting, and a musician. After his wife's death he never remarried and became a devout Buddhist. During the An Lushan rebellion he was captured and forced to serve the rebels, later pardoned. At his country estate at Wangchuan he wrote quiet poems of mountains, empty forests and moonlight that seem to dissolve the self into nature. Su Shi said there was painting in his poems and poetry in his paintings.
Tu Fu|Du Fu (712–770), Tang poet often called China's greatest.|Du Fu (Tu Fu) failed the imperial examinations and spent years seeking an official post. The An Lushan rebellion of 755 threw China into chaos; one of his children starved to death, and he was captured by rebels. He wandered as a refugee with his family, living for a time in a thatched cottage in Chengdu, and died on a boat on the Yangtze. Little recognised in his lifetime, he is now often considered China's greatest poet, writing with compassion about war, poverty, family and the passing of time.
Mikhail Lermontov|Russian Romantic poet and novelist (1814–1841) of A Hero of Our Time.|Lermontov's mother died when he was three, and he was raised by his wealthy grandmother. He was expelled from Moscow University and joined the army. His poem "Death of a Poet", blaming high society for Pushkin's death in a duel, got him exiled to the Caucasus, whose mountains inspired his best work. His novel "A Hero of Our Time" (1840), about the cynical officer Pechorin, is a founding work of Russian psychological fiction. Like Pushkin, he died in a duel, at 26, at Pyatigorsk.
Alexander Pushkin|Russia's national poet (1799–1837), author of Eugene Onegin.|Pushkin was born in Moscow into an old noble family; his great-grandfather, Abram Gannibal, was an African taken to Russia as a boy and raised by Peter the Great. His poems mocking authority got him exiled to the south and then to his family estate. He wrote the novel in verse "Eugene Onegin", the drama "Boris Godunov" and many lyrics and tales, creating the modern Russian literary language. Defending his wife's honour against rumours, he fought a duel with the French officer d'Anthès and died of his wounds at 37. Russians call him "our everything".
Victor Hugo|French poet and novelist (1802–1885) of Les Misérables and Notre-Dame de Paris.|Hugo, the son of a general in Napoleon's army, was a celebrated poet by his twenties, and his play "Hernani" sparked a battle between Romantics and classicists. "Notre-Dame de Paris" (1831) helped save the cathedral. The drowning of his daughter Léopoldine in 1843 devastated him. He opposed Napoleon III's coup in 1851 and spent nineteen years in exile on the Channel Islands, where he finished "Les Misérables". He campaigned against the death penalty and poverty. When he died in 1885, two million people followed his coffin to the Panthéon.
James Joyce|Irish novelist (1882–1941) of Dubliners and Ulysses.|Joyce was born in Dublin, the eldest of ten surviving children of a charming but improvident father. Educated by Jesuits, he left Ireland in 1904 with Nora Barnacle, a hotel chambermaid, and lived in Trieste, Zurich and Paris, often in poverty, teaching English. "Dubliners" and "A Portrait of the Artist as a Young Man" were followed by "Ulysses" (1922), which follows ordinary Dubliners through one day, 16 June 1904, the day of his first outing with Nora. It was banned in Britain and the US for years. He spent seventeen years on "Finnegans Wake". He had many eye operations.
Thomas Mann|German novelist (1875–1955) of The Magic Mountain. Nobel Prize 1929.|Mann was born in Lübeck into a wealthy merchant family whose decline he described in his first novel, "Buddenbrooks", written in his twenties. "Death in Venice" and "The Magic Mountain", set in a tuberculosis sanatorium, followed. He won the Nobel Prize in 1929. Opposing Hitler, he left Germany in 1933 and settled in the United States, broadcasting anti-Nazi speeches to Germany during the war. There he wrote "Joseph and His Brothers" and "Doctor Faustus". He spent his last years in Switzerland.
Baruch Spinoza|Dutch philosopher (1632–1677) of the Ethics, who saw God and Nature as one.|Spinoza was born in Amsterdam to a family of Portuguese Jews who had fled the Inquisition. At 23 he was excommunicated by the Jewish community with a harsh ban, for "abominable heresies". He lived modestly, grinding lenses for microscopes and telescopes, and declined a professorship at Heidelberg to keep his freedom. His "Theologico-Political Treatise" argued for freedom of thought and was condemned. His "Ethics", written in geometric form, identifies God with Nature. He died at 44, probably from lung disease made worse by glass dust, and the "Ethics" was published after his death.
Nikos Kazantzakis|Greek writer (1883–1957) of Zorba the Greek.|Kazantzakis was born in Heraklion, Crete, under Ottoman rule, and remembered the island's uprisings. He studied law and then philosophy with Bergson in Paris, and was drawn to Nietzsche, the Buddha, Lenin and Christ in turn. He travelled widely and wrote an epic sequel to the "Odyssey" in 33,333 lines. His novels "Zorba the Greek", "Christ Recrucified" and "The Last Temptation of Christ" made him world-famous; the Greek Orthodox Church tried to excommunicate him. His grave in Heraklion reads: "I hope for nothing. I fear nothing. I am free."
Martin Buber|Austrian-Israeli philosopher (1878–1965) of I and Thou.|Buber was born in Vienna and, after his parents' divorce, raised by his grandfather, a scholar in Lviv. He became a leader of cultural Zionism and collected and retold the tales of the Hasidic masters. "I and Thou" (1923) distinguishes treating others as objects ("I-It") from meeting them as whole persons ("I-Thou"), where, he believed, we also meet God. He emigrated from Nazi Germany to Jerusalem in 1938 and taught at the Hebrew University, where he argued for a shared state for Jews and Arabs.
Hannah Arendt|German-American political thinker (1906–1975) of The Human Condition and Eichmann in Jerusalem.|Arendt grew up in Königsberg in a secular Jewish family and studied philosophy with Heidegger and Jaspers. Arrested by the Gestapo in 1933, she fled to Paris, was interned in France, and escaped to New York in 1941. "The Origins of Totalitarianism" (1951) made her name. "The Human Condition" explores labour, work and action. Reporting on the trial of the Nazi official Adolf Eichmann, she coined the phrase "the banality of evil", which caused fierce controversy. She taught at the New School and elsewhere, and valued friendship and thinking for oneself above all.
Simone de Beauvoir|French philosopher and novelist (1908–1986) of The Second Sex.|Beauvoir was born in Paris into a bourgeois Catholic family that lost its wealth; she lost her faith as a teenager. In 1929, at 21, she came second in the national philosophy exam, just behind Sartre, and began a lifelong partnership with him. She taught philosophy and wrote novels, including "The Mandarins", which won the Prix Goncourt. "The Second Sex" (1949), which argues "one is not born, but rather becomes, a woman", became a founding text of modern feminism. Her memoirs and "A Very Easy Death", about her mother, are much admired.
Samuel Beckett|Irish writer (1906–1989) of Waiting for Godot. Nobel Prize 1969.|Beckett was born in Foxrock near Dublin and studied at Trinity College. In Paris he became James Joyce's friend and assistant. During the war he joined the French Resistance and later fled to hide in the south, earning the Croix de Guerre. Writing mainly in French after the war, he produced "Waiting for Godot", in which two tramps wait for someone who never comes, and the novels "Molloy" and "The Unnamable". He won the Nobel Prize in 1969 and gave away much of the money. His work strips life to its bare essentials with bleak humour.
Erich Fromm|German-American psychoanalyst (1900–1980) of The Art of Loving.|Fromm was born in Frankfurt into an Orthodox Jewish family and studied sociology and psychoanalysis. He worked with the Frankfurt School, then emigrated to the United States in 1934 and later lived in Mexico and Switzerland. "Escape from Freedom" (1941) explained why people embrace authoritarianism. "The Art of Loving" (1956), arguing that love is a skill requiring care, responsibility, respect and knowledge, sold millions. "To Have or to Be?" contrasts a life of possessing with a life of being. He was also a peace activist.
Rollo May|American existential psychologist (1909–1994) of Love and Will.|May was born in Ohio and had a difficult childhood. After college he taught in Greece and attended seminars with the psychologist Alfred Adler. He studied theology with Paul Tillich, who became a close friend. While training as a psychologist, he spent eighteen months in a sanatorium with tuberculosis, where facing possible death shaped his thinking about anxiety. He became a leading figure in existential psychotherapy. His books include "The Meaning of Anxiety", "Love and Will" and "The Courage to Create".
Thomas Merton|American Trappist monk and writer (1915–1968) of The Seven Storey Mountain.|Merton was born in France to artist parents; his mother died when he was six and his father when he was fifteen. He drifted through Cambridge and Columbia University, converted to Catholicism and in 1941 entered the Trappist Abbey of Gethsemani in Kentucky. His autobiography "The Seven Storey Mountain" (1948) became a bestseller. From his monastery he wrote over seventy books on contemplation, peace and civil rights, and corresponded with people worldwide. He explored Zen and met the Dalai Lama. He died in Bangkok in 1968 from an accidental electric shock.
Thich Nhat Hanh|Vietnamese Zen master and peace activist (1926–2022) who taught mindfulness worldwide.|Thich Nhat Hanh became a novice monk at 16 in Hue, Vietnam. During the Vietnam War he developed "engaged Buddhism", founding a school of social service whose young volunteers rebuilt bombed villages. He travelled to the US to call for peace; Martin Luther King Jr. nominated him for the Nobel Peace Prize. Barred from returning home, he lived in exile for 39 years, founding Plum Village in France. His books, like "The Miracle of Mindfulness" and "Peace Is Every Step", teach mindful breathing and walking. After a stroke he returned to Hue, where he died at 95.
