Irvin Yalom|American existential psychiatrist and novelist (born 1931).|Yalom was born in Washington, D.C., to Russian Jewish immigrants who ran a grocery store; as a boy he escaped into books from the local library. He became a psychiatrist and a professor at Stanford. His "Theory and Practice of Group Psychotherapy" became a standard text, and "Existential Psychotherapy" set out four "ultimate concerns": death, freedom, isolation and meaninglessness. He also wrote teaching novels, such as "When Nietzsche Wept", and "Staring at the Sun", about overcoming the fear of death. He was married for 65 years to the writer Marilyn Yalom.
Joseph Campbell|American mythologist (1904–1987) of The Hero with a Thousand Faces.|Campbell grew up in New York, fascinated by Native American culture after seeing Buffalo Bill's show. He studied medieval literature in Paris and Munich, then spent five years during the Depression reading in a cabin in Woodstock. He taught at Sarah Lawrence College for 38 years. "The Hero with a Thousand Faces" (1949) argued that myths around the world share a common pattern, the hero's journey; George Lucas drew on it for "Star Wars". His television conversations with Bill Moyers, "The Power of Myth", reached millions and gave the advice "Follow your bliss."
Miyamoto Musashi|Japanese swordsman and artist (1584–1645), author of The Book of Five Rings.|Musashi was a Japanese swordsman who, by his own account, fought his first duel at 13 and won more than sixty, never losing. His most famous duel, against Sasaki Kojirō on Ganryū Island in 1612, he is said to have won with a wooden sword carved from an oar. He developed a style of fighting with two swords. He was also a skilled painter and calligrapher. In his last years, living in a cave called Reigandō, he wrote "The Book of Five Rings", a guide to strategy, and "The Way of Walking Alone", 21 precepts for life.
Susan Sontag|American essayist (1933–2004) of Against Interpretation and On Photography.|Sontag grew up in Arizona and California; her father died in China when she was five. She entered university at 15 and married her teacher Philip Rieff at 17. Her essays "Notes on 'Camp'" and "Against Interpretation" made her famous in the 1960s. "On Photography" and "Illness as Metaphor", written after her own cancer diagnosis, changed how people think about images and disease. She staged "Waiting for Godot" in besieged Sarajevo in 1993. She wrote novels too, including "The Volcano Lover". Her partner in her last years was the photographer Annie Leibovitz.
Max Ehrmann|American poet and lawyer (1872–1945), author of "Desiderata".|Ehrmann was born in Terre Haute, Indiana, to German immigrant parents. He studied philosophy and law at Harvard, practised law and worked in his family's meatpacking business before devoting himself to writing at about 40. In 1927 he wrote the prose poem "Desiderata", "things to be desired", which begins "Go placidly amid the noise and haste". It became famous after his death, especially when it was found in a church in Baltimore and mistakenly thought to be ancient. He married his friend Bertha Pratt King shortly before his death.
Rudyard Kipling|English writer (1865–1936) of The Jungle Book and "If—". Nobel Prize 1907.|Kipling was born in Bombay, where his father taught at an art school. At five he was sent to England to a foster home in Southsea, where he was cruelly treated. He returned to India at 16 as a journalist and wrote stories that made him famous. "The Jungle Book", "Kim" and the "Just So Stories" are still read. He won the Nobel Prize in 1907, the first English-language writer to do so. His views on empire are now controversial. His daughter Josephine died young, and his son John was killed in the First World War. His poem "If—" is among the most quoted in English.
Henri-Frédéric Amiel|Swiss philosopher (1821–1881) whose vast Journal Intime is a classic of introspection.|Amiel was born in Geneva and orphaned at twelve. He studied in Germany for five years and returned to Geneva as professor of aesthetics and then philosophy. He published poems and essays that were little noticed and was considered a disappointment by his friends. But from 1847 until his death he kept a private journal of nearly 17,000 pages, full of self-analysis, doubt, religious feeling and longing. Published after his death as the "Journal intime", it made him famous, praised by Tolstoy and Matthew Arnold.
Boethius|Roman philosopher (c. 477–524) who wrote The Consolation of Philosophy in prison awaiting execution.|Anicius Manlius Severinus Boethius was a Roman aristocrat who served the Ostrogothic king Theodoric in Italy, becoming consul and head of government. He planned to translate all of Plato and Aristotle into Latin, and his works on logic, music and arithmetic shaped medieval education. Accused of treason, he was imprisoned and executed around 524. In prison he wrote "The Consolation of Philosophy", in which Lady Philosophy visits him and teaches that true happiness lies in virtue and in God, not in Fortune's wheel. It was one of the most read books of the Middle Ages.
Novalis|German Romantic poet (1772–1801) of the Hymns to the Night.|Friedrich von Hardenberg took the pen name Novalis. He studied law and mining science and worked as an inspector of salt mines. At 22 he fell in love with twelve-year-old Sophie von Kühn and became engaged to her when she was older; her death at fifteen from tuberculosis devastated him and inspired his "Hymns to the Night". His unfinished novel "Heinrich von Ofterdingen" introduced the "blue flower", symbol of Romantic longing. His fragments mix philosophy, science and mysticism. He died of tuberculosis at 28.
Antonio Machado|Spanish poet (1875–1939): "Traveller, there is no road; the road is made by walking."|Machado was born in Seville and grew up in Madrid. He worked in Paris as a translator, then taught French in Soria, where he married the young Leonor Izquierdo; her death three years later shattered him, and he moved to Baeza. His poems of the Castilian landscape, "Campos de Castilla", and his "Proverbs and Songs", including "Traveller, there is no road; the road is made by walking", are among the most loved in Spanish. He supported the Republic in the Civil War, fled across the Pyrenees in 1939 and died days later in Collioure, France.
G. K. Chesterton|English writer (1874–1936) of paradox, the Father Brown stories and Orthodoxy.|Gilbert Keith Chesterton was born in London and studied art at the Slade School before becoming a journalist. A huge man in a cape and slouch hat, famous for his wit and absent-mindedness, he wrote some 80 books, hundreds of poems and thousands of essays. His Father Brown detective stories are still adapted for television. In "Orthodoxy" (1908) he defended Christian faith; he converted to Catholicism in 1922. He debated friendly opponents such as George Bernard Shaw and H. G. Wells. His paradoxes delight readers, such as "Angels can fly because they take themselves lightly."
Homer|Legendary Greek poet (c. 8th century BC) of the Iliad and the Odyssey.|Nothing certain is known about Homer. Ancient Greeks pictured him as a blind singer from Ionia, and seven cities claimed to be his birthplace. The "Iliad", about the wrath of Achilles in the Trojan War, and the "Odyssey", about Odysseus' ten-year journey home, were composed in the 8th or 7th century BC, probably from a long oral tradition of bards who improvised with set phrases. They were the foundation of Greek education and remain the first great works of Western literature, still read for their humanity, their similes and their portraits of courage, grief and homecoming.
Sappho|Greek lyric poet of Lesbos (c. 630–570 BC), the first great woman poet, whose love poems survive in fragments.|Sappho lived on the island of Lesbos around 600 BC, probably from an aristocratic family; she may have been exiled to Sicily for a time. She had a daughter named Cleïs and may have led a circle of young women. She wrote nine books of lyric poetry, sung to the lyre, mostly about love and desire, including love between women. Plato called her the tenth Muse. Almost all her work was lost; we have one complete poem and many fragments, some found on Egyptian papyri, including a new poem discovered in 2014.
Antoine de Saint-Exupéry|French writer and aviator (1900–1944) of The Little Prince and Wind, Sand and Stars, lost on a reconnaissance flight in the war.|Saint-Exupéry was born in Lyon into an aristocratic family; his father died when he was four. He became a pioneering pilot, flying mail routes across the Sahara and the Andes. In 1935 he crashed in the Libyan desert and nearly died of thirst before a Bedouin rescued him, as he recounts in "Wind, Sand and Stars". In exile in New York during the war, he wrote and illustrated "The Little Prince" (1943), one of the most translated books ever. He rejoined the Free French air force and disappeared on a reconnaissance flight over the Mediterranean in July 1944.
C. S. Lewis|British writer and scholar (1898–1963) of the Narnia books, Mere Christianity and A Grief Observed.|Clive Staples Lewis was born in Belfast; his mother died when he was nine. He was wounded in the First World War. As a fellow at Oxford, he joined friends including J. R. R. Tolkien, who helped lead him from atheism back to Christianity in 1931. His wartime radio talks became "Mere Christianity". He wrote "The Screwtape Letters", science fiction and the seven "Chronicles of Narnia", beginning with "The Lion, the Witch and the Wardrobe". In his late fifties he married the American writer Joy Davidman; after her death from cancer he wrote "A Grief Observed".
Stefan Zweig|Austrian writer (1881–1942) of novellas, biographies and the memoir The World of Yesterday.|Zweig was born in Vienna to a wealthy Jewish family. In the 1920s and 30s he was one of the most widely read and translated authors in the world, known for his novellas, such as "Letter from an Unknown Woman" and "Chess Story", and his vivid biographies. A pacifist and believer in European unity, he left Austria in 1934 as Nazism rose, living in England, the US and finally Brazil. His memoir "The World of Yesterday" mourns the lost Europe of his youth. In 1942, in Petrópolis, despairing at the war, he and his wife Lotte took their own lives.
Clarice Lispector|Brazilian writer (1920–1977) of intense, inward novels such as The Passion According to G.H.|Lispector was born in Ukraine to a Jewish family fleeing pogroms; they reached Brazil when she was a baby. Her mother died when Clarice was nine. She studied law, worked as a journalist and published her first novel, "Near to the Wild Heart", at 23 to great acclaim. She married a diplomat and lived abroad for fifteen years before returning to Rio de Janeiro. Her novels and stories, such as "The Passion According to G.H." and "The Hour of the Star", explore inner life in strange, intense prose. She is now seen as one of the great writers of the 20th century.
Dag Hammarskjöld|Swedish diplomat (1905–1961), UN Secretary-General, whose spiritual diary Markings was found after his death.|Hammarskjöld was the son of a Swedish prime minister. An economist, he became a senior civil servant and in 1953 Secretary-General of the United Nations. He shaped the role, creating UN peacekeeping forces and travelling to troubled regions himself. In 1961, on a peace mission during the Congo crisis, he died when his plane crashed in Northern Rhodesia (Zambia), in circumstances still investigated. He was awarded the Nobel Peace Prize after his death. His private spiritual journal, found in his apartment, was published as "Markings".
Gabriel García Márquez|Colombian novelist (1927–2014) of One Hundred Years of Solitude and Love in the Time of Cholera. Nobel Prize 1982.|García Márquez was raised by his grandparents in Aracataca, a small town in Colombia, listening to his grandmother's tales of ghosts and his grandfather's memories of war. He studied law, then became a journalist. He wrote "One Hundred Years of Solitude" (1967) in about eighteen months in Mexico City while his wife pawned their possessions to keep the family going. The saga of the Buendía family in the town of Macondo made "magical realism" world-famous. He won the Nobel Prize in 1982. "Love in the Time of Cholera" followed in 1985.
Attar|Persian Sufi poet (c. 1145–1221) of The Conference of the Birds.|Farid al-Din Attar was born in Nishapur, Persia, and worked as a pharmacist and perfumer ("attar"), seeing hundreds of patients a day. A Sufi, he wrote "The Conference of the Birds", in which the birds of the world set out to find their king, the Simorgh; after crossing seven valleys, only thirty birds ("si morgh" in Persian) arrive, and discover that the Simorgh is themselves. He also wrote "Memorial of the Saints", lives of early Sufis. Rumi said Attar had crossed the seven cities of love while he was still turning a corner. He was likely killed when the Mongols sacked Nishapur.
Petrarch|Italian poet and scholar (1304–1374), father of humanism, whose sonnets to Laura shaped European love poetry.|Francesco Petrarca was born in Arezzo, the son of an exiled Florentine notary, and grew up in Avignon, then seat of the papacy. On Good Friday 1327 he saw Laura in church and loved her, unrequited, for the rest of his life; she died in the Black Death of 1348. His "Canzoniere", 366 poems mostly about her, perfected the sonnet and influenced poets for centuries. He hunted for lost classical manuscripts, discovering letters of Cicero, and is called the father of humanism. His ascent of Mont Ventoux in 1336 is sometimes seen as the first climb for the view.
Michelangelo|Italian sculptor, painter, architect and poet (1475–1564) of the David and the Sistine Chapel ceiling.|Michelangelo Buonarroti was born near Arezzo and raised in Florence, where Lorenzo de' Medici took him into his household as a teenager. At 24 he carved the "Pietà" in Rome, and at 29 the "David" in Florence. Under Pope Julius II he painted the Sistine Chapel ceiling over four years, mostly alone, and later its "Last Judgement". He saw sculpture as freeing the figure already in the stone. In old age he designed the dome of St Peter's and wrote passionate sonnets, many to Tommaso dei Cavalieri and Vittoria Colonna. He worked until days before his death at 88.
Gustave Flaubert|French novelist (1821–1880) of Madame Bovary, obsessed with finding the exact word.|Flaubert was born in Rouen, the son of a surgeon, and grew up in the hospital where his father worked. Epileptic attacks ended his law studies, and he lived most of his life with his mother at Croisset on the Seine, writing slowly, searching for "le mot juste", the exact word, and testing sentences by shouting them aloud. "Madame Bovary" (1857), about a doctor's wife who seeks romance and is destroyed, was prosecuted for obscenity; he was acquitted. "Sentimental Education" and "Three Tales" followed. He mentored the young Maupassant and corresponded with George Sand.
Federico García Lorca|Spanish poet and playwright (1898–1936) of the Gypsy Ballads and Blood Wedding, killed in the Civil War.|Lorca was born near Granada into a prosperous family and was a talented pianist. In Madrid he befriended Salvador Dalí and Luis Buñuel. His "Gypsy Ballads" (1928) made him the most popular Spanish poet of his day. A year in New York produced "Poet in New York". He directed a travelling student theatre, La Barraca, bringing classics to villages, and wrote the tragedies "Blood Wedding", "Yerma" and "The House of Bernarda Alba". At the start of the Civil War, in August 1936, he was murdered by Nationalist forces near Granada; his body has never been found.
Octavio Paz|Mexican poet and essayist (1914–1998) of The Labyrinth of Solitude. Nobel Prize 1990.|Paz was born in Mexico City; his grandfather was a novelist and his father fought in the Mexican Revolution. He published his first book of poems at 19 and went to Spain during the Civil War. "The Labyrinth of Solitude" (1950) explores Mexican identity. As a diplomat he lived in Paris, Japan and India, where he was ambassador; he resigned in 1968 in protest at the government's massacre of students in Tlatelolco. His long poem "Sunstone" follows the circular Aztec calendar. He won the Nobel Prize in 1990.
José Saramago|Portuguese novelist (1922–2010) of Blindness. Nobel Prize 1998.|Saramago was born to a poor family of landless peasants in a Portuguese village. Unable to afford university, he trained as a mechanic and worked as a civil servant, translator and journalist. He published a novel at 25, then little fiction for decades. Fame came in his sixties with "Baltasar and Blimunda" and later "The Gospel According to Jesus Christ", which the Portuguese government blocked from a European prize, prompting him to move to Lanzarote. "Blindness" (1995) imagines a whole city struck blind. He won the Nobel Prize in 1998.
Anna Akhmatova|Russian poet (1889–1966) whose Requiem bore witness to Stalin's terror.|Anna Gorenko took her great-grandmother's Tatar name, Akhmatova, when her father objected to a poet in the family. Her early love lyrics made her famous before the Revolution. Her first husband, the poet Nikolai Gumilyov, was shot by the Bolsheviks in 1921, and her son Lev spent years in labour camps. Banned from publishing for long periods, she composed "Requiem" about standing in prison queues, memorising it with friends who burned the paper. Expelled from the Writers' Union in 1946, she outlived Stalin and was celebrated in her last years.
Marina Tsvetaeva|Russian poet (1892–1941) of passionate, fierce lyrics.|Tsvetaeva was born in Moscow; her father founded the Pushkin Museum and her mother was a pianist. She published her first poems at 18. During the famine after the Revolution, her younger daughter died of starvation in an orphanage. She emigrated in 1922, living in Berlin, Prague and Paris in poverty, writing intense poetry and corresponding with Rilke and Pasternak. In 1939 she returned to the Soviet Union, where her husband was shot and her daughter sent to the camps. Evacuated to Yelabuga during the war, alone and desperate, she took her own life in 1941.
Czesław Miłosz|Polish poet and essayist (1911–2004) who wrote on history, faith and memory. Nobel Prize 1980.|Miłosz was born in Lithuania, then part of the Russian Empire. In Nazi-occupied Warsaw he took part in underground literary life and helped Jews. After the war he served as a diplomat for communist Poland, then defected in 1951. "The Captive Mind" analysed how intellectuals accept totalitarianism. He taught Slavic literature at Berkeley for decades, where his poems were largely unknown until he won the Nobel Prize in 1980. His poems speak of history, faith, the beauty of the world and the duty to remember. He returned to Kraków in old age.
Tomas Tranströmer|Swedish poet (1931–2015) of luminous, dreamlike images. Nobel Prize 2011.|Tranströmer was born in Stockholm and raised by his mother, a teacher, after his parents divorced. He published his first collection, "17 Poems", at 23. He worked for decades as a psychologist at a youth prison and with disabled people and those with addictions. His spare poems, built on startling images, were translated into over sixty languages. In 1990 a stroke left him largely unable to speak, but he continued to write short poems and play the piano with his left hand. He won the Nobel Prize in 2011.
Paul Celan|Romanian-born German-language poet (1920–1970) of "Death Fugue", who wrote in the shadow of the Holocaust.|Paul Antschel was born into a German-speaking Jewish family in Czernowitz. In 1942 his parents were deported to a Nazi camp in Transnistria, where his father died of typhus and his mother was shot. Celan survived a labour camp. He settled in Paris in 1948, taking the name Celan, and worked as a translator and teacher of German. "Deathfugue", with its "black milk of daybreak", became one of the most important poems about the Holocaust. His later poems grew ever more compressed. He drowned in the Seine in 1970.
W. G. Sebald|German writer (1944–2001) of The Rings of Saturn and Austerlitz, on memory and loss.|Winfried Georg Sebald was born in a Bavarian village in 1944; his father had been a soldier, and Sebald grew up amid silence about the Nazi past. He moved to England in 1966 and taught European literature at the University of East Anglia for thirty years. His books, "The Emigrants", "The Rings of Saturn" and "Austerlitz", mix fiction, memoir, travel and history, with unexplained photographs, in long, melancholy sentences about memory and loss. He died in a car accident in Norfolk at 57, just as he was becoming famous.
Gaston Bachelard|French philosopher (1884–1962) of The Poetics of Space.|Bachelard was born in a small village in Champagne and worked as a postal clerk while studying mathematics and physics. After serving in the trenches of the First World War, he taught physics and chemistry in a secondary school, earned a doctorate in philosophy at 43 and became a professor at the Sorbonne. He wrote on how science overcomes old habits of thinking, and also on the imagination: "The Psychoanalysis of Fire", "Water and Dreams" and "The Poetics of Space", which studies houses, drawers and nests as spaces of dreaming.
Pierre Teilhard de Chardin|French Jesuit priest and palaeontologist (1881–1955) who saw evolution moving toward spirit.|Teilhard was born in Auvergne and became a Jesuit priest. A stretcher-bearer in the First World War, he was decorated for bravery. He was also a palaeontologist and geologist, spending twenty years in China, where he helped study Peking Man. He wrote a vision of evolution leading toward ever greater complexity and consciousness, culminating in an "Omega Point" in Christ. His superiors forbade him to publish his religious ideas, and "The Phenomenon of Man" appeared only after his death in New York on Easter Sunday 1955.
Mikhail Bulgakov|Russian writer (1891–1940) of The Master and Margarita.|Bulgakov was born in Kyiv, studied medicine and served as a doctor in the First World War and the Civil War, experiences behind "A Country Doctor's Notebook". He moved to Moscow to write. His play "The Days of the Turbins" was a hit and one of Stalin's favourites, but his satires were banned. He wrote to Stalin asking to leave the country; Stalin phoned him and found him a job at the Moscow Art Theatre instead. He worked secretly for twelve years on "The Master and Margarita", in which the devil visits Moscow. It was published 26 years after his death.
Knut Hamsun|Norwegian novelist (1859–1952) of Hunger and Growth of the Soil. Nobel Prize 1920.|Knut Pedersen was born to a poor family in Norway and grew up in the north, sent at nine to work for an uncle who mistreated him. He drifted through jobs, including twice in America as a farmhand and streetcar conductor. "Hunger" (1890), about a starving writer in Kristiania (Oslo), pioneered psychological modernism. "Growth of the Soil" helped win him the Nobel Prize in 1920. In old age he supported the Nazi occupation of Norway, even giving his Nobel medal to Goebbels, and was fined after the war. His great literary influence is shadowed by this.
V. S. Naipaul|Trinidad-born British writer (1932–2018) of A House for Mr Biswas. Nobel Prize 2001.|Vidiadhar Surajprasad Naipaul was born in Chaguanas, Trinidad, to a family descended from Indian indentured labourers. His father, a journalist and aspiring writer, encouraged him. At 18 he won a scholarship to Oxford, where he suffered a breakdown. He settled in England and wrote comic novels of Trinidad, then "A House for Mr Biswas" (1961), inspired by his father. His travel books on India, the Islamic world and Africa were admired and controversial. He won the Nobel Prize in 2001. His brother Shiva was also a writer.
Derek Walcott|Saint Lucian poet and playwright (1930–2017) of Omeros. Nobel Prize 1992.|Walcott was born in Castries, St Lucia, with a twin brother; his father, a painter and poet, died when he was one. He published his first poems at 18 with money borrowed from his mother. He spent many years in Trinidad, where he founded the Trinidad Theatre Workshop in 1959, and later taught in Boston. His poems and plays join Caribbean speech and landscape with the classical tradition. "Omeros" (1990) retells Homer among St Lucian fishermen. He won the Nobel Prize in 1992. He was also a painter.
Plutarch|Greek biographer and essayist (c. 46–120) of the Parallel Lives.|Plutarch was born in Chaeronea, in Boeotia, Greece, to a wealthy family, and studied philosophy in Athens. He travelled to Rome and Egypt but spent most of his life in his small hometown, serving as a magistrate and, for his last thirty years, as a priest at Delphi. His "Parallel Lives" pair famous Greeks and Romans, such as Alexander and Caesar, to show character in action; Shakespeare used them for his Roman plays. His "Moralia" are essays on everything from anger and friendship to the education of children. A letter to his wife after their daughter's death is very moving.
Ovid|Roman poet (43 BC–17/18 AD) of the Metamorphoses, a great treasury of myths, who died in exile.|Publius Ovidius Naso was born in Sulmo, east of Rome, to a wealthy family who wanted him to be a lawyer, but poetry came naturally to him. His witty love poems, such as "The Art of Love", made him famous. His great work, the "Metamorphoses", retells about 250 myths of transformation, from the creation to Julius Caesar's deification, and became a sourcebook for European art and literature. In 8 AD Augustus banished him to Tomis on the Black Sea for "a poem and a mistake". He never returned, and wrote sorrowful poems of exile.
Thucydides|Athenian historian (c. 460–400 BC) of the Peloponnesian War.|Thucydides was an Athenian general who caught the plague that devastated Athens in 430 BC and survived to describe its symptoms. In 424 BC he failed to save the city of Amphipolis from the Spartans and was exiled for twenty years. He used this time to write the "History of the Peloponnesian War", a war he believed would be the greatest ever. He sought accuracy, rejected the gods as explanations and wrote for posterity, "a possession for all time". The Melian dialogue and Pericles' funeral oration are among its most famous passages.
Teresa of Ávila|Spanish Carmelite nun and mystic (1515–1582) of The Interior Castle.|Teresa de Cepeda y Ahumada was born in Ávila, Spain; her grandfather was a converted Jew. As a child she ran away to seek martyrdom among the Moors. She entered a Carmelite convent at 20, suffered severe illness, and after years of struggle in prayer experienced profound visions in her forties. She founded seventeen convents of "Discalced" (barefoot) Carmelites, travelling across Spain in ox carts despite opposition. Her books "The Life", "The Way of Perfection" and "The Interior Castle" are classics of mysticism, full of humour and common sense. She was the first woman named a Doctor of the Church.
John of the Cross|Spanish mystic and poet (1542–1591) of The Dark Night of the Soul.|Juan de Yepes was born near Ávila into poverty; his father died when he was young. He became a Carmelite friar and joined Teresa of Ávila's reform. In 1577, friars opposed to reform kidnapped him and imprisoned him in a tiny cell in Toledo, beating him regularly. There he composed his great poems in his head, including "The Spiritual Canticle". After nine months he escaped through a window. He wrote "The Dark Night of the Soul" and "The Ascent of Mount Carmel", commentaries on his poems, describing the soul's journey through darkness to union with God.
Mencius|Chinese Confucian philosopher (c. 372–289 BC) who taught that human nature is good.|Meng Ke, called Mengzi or Mencius, was born in the state of Zou about a century after Confucius. His mother is famous in Chinese tradition for moving house three times to find a good environment for his education. He studied with a disciple of Confucius' grandson and travelled for decades among the rulers of warring states, urging them to govern through benevolence. He taught that human nature is good, with sprouts of compassion, shame, respect and judgement that need cultivating. He defended the people's right to overthrow a tyrant. His book is one of the Four Books of Confucianism.
Ryōkan|Japanese Zen monk and poet (1758–1831) who lived as a hermit and played with village children.|Ryōkan was born in a village in Echigo, Japan, the eldest son of a village headman and poet, but chose to become a Zen monk. After years of training, he lived as a hermit in a small hut on Mount Kugami, begging for food, playing with village children and writing poems and calligraphy. He called himself "Great Fool" (Taigu). Stories tell of him sharing his hut with a thief and leaving his only blanket. In old age he formed a loving friendship with the young nun Teishin, who collected his poems after his death.
Ikkyū|Japanese Zen monk and poet (1394–1481), eccentric and free-spirited.|Ikkyū Sōjun was said to be the illegitimate son of Emperor Go-Komatsu. Sent to a temple at five, he trained in Zen under strict masters and attained enlightenment, it is said, on hearing a crow caw while meditating in a boat. He rejected the corruption of the Zen establishment and wandered, frequenting taverns and brothels, writing wild, bawdy and devout poems. In old age he fell in love with a blind singer, Lady Mori. Despite his eccentricity, he was made abbot of the great Daitoku-ji temple in Kyoto and helped rebuild it after the Onin War.
Mary Shelley|English novelist (1797–1851) who wrote Frankenstein at nineteen.|Mary Wollstonecraft Godwin was the daughter of the feminist Mary Wollstonecraft, who died days after her birth, and the philosopher William Godwin. At 16 she ran away with the married poet Percy Bysshe Shelley. In 1816, at Lake Geneva with Byron, they challenged each other to write ghost stories, and she began "Frankenstein", published in 1818 when she was 20. Three of her four children died young, and Percy drowned in 1822. She supported herself and her surviving son by writing novels, including "The Last Man", stories and travel books.
Stendhal|French novelist (1783–1842) of The Red and the Black.|Marie-Henri Beyle was born in Grenoble; his beloved mother died when he was seven, and he hated his father. He followed Napoleon's armies to Italy, Germany and Russia, where he witnessed the retreat from Moscow. Italy became his spiritual home, and he served as French consul in Civitavecchia. He used more than a hundred pen names. His novels "The Red and the Black" and "The Charterhouse of Parma", which he dictated in 52 days, are masterpieces of psychological realism. He predicted that he would be understood around 1880.
Honoré de Balzac|French novelist (1799–1850) of the Human Comedy.|Balzac was born in Tours and sent to a strict boarding school. He failed in business as a publisher and printer, leaving him in debt for life. He wrote through the night fuelled by enormous amounts of black coffee, producing nearly 100 novels and stories in about twenty years, linked as "The Human Comedy", a portrait of all French society. "Père Goriot" and "Eugénie Grandet" are among the best known. He corresponded for eighteen years with the Polish countess Ewelina Hańska and married her five months before he died, aged 51.
Nikolai Gogol|Russian-Ukrainian writer (1809–1852) of Dead Souls and "The Overcoat".|Gogol was born in a Ukrainian village and grew up hearing Cossack tales and folk legends, which filled his first stories, "Evenings on a Farm near Dikanka". He moved to St Petersburg, failed as a civil servant and a history lecturer, and found fame with "The Nose", "The Overcoat" and the comedy "The Government Inspector". He lived mostly in Rome while writing "Dead Souls", a satire of a swindler buying dead serfs. Falling under the influence of a fanatical priest, he burned the second part of the novel and starved himself, dying at 42.
Immanuel Kant|German philosopher (1724–1804) of the Critiques, who asked what we can know, what we ought to do and what we may hope.|Kant was born in Königsberg, East Prussia, the son of a harness-maker, and never travelled more than about a hundred miles from it. He was a popular lecturer on geography, physics and philosophy, and so regular in his habits that neighbours set their clocks by his afternoon walk. At 57 he published the "Critique of Pure Reason" (1781), arguing that the mind shapes experience. The "Critique of Practical Reason" and "Groundwork of the Metaphysics of Morals" set out the categorical imperative. His essay "What Is Enlightenment?" urged "Dare to know!" He also proposed a plan for perpetual peace between nations.
Henri Bergson|French philosopher (1859–1941) of time, memory and creative evolution. Nobel Prize 1927.|Bergson was born in Paris to a Polish Jewish father, a composer, and an English mother. He won a prize in mathematics at 18 but chose philosophy. His lectures at the Collège de France drew such crowds that people came hours early. He argued that lived time, "duration", cannot be measured like space, and that a creative "vital impulse" drives evolution ("Creative Evolution", 1907). He won the Nobel Prize in Literature in 1927. Under the Vichy regime in 1940, refusing exemptions, he insisted on registering as a Jew. He died in occupied Paris in 1941.
Osip Mandelstam|Russian poet (1891–1938) who died in a Stalinist camp.|Mandelstam was born in Warsaw and grew up in St Petersburg in a Jewish family. With Akhmatova and Gumilyov he was a leader of the Acmeist poets, who valued clarity and solid things. In 1933 he wrote a poem mocking Stalin as the "Kremlin mountaineer" and read it to a few friends; he was arrested and exiled. Rearrested in 1938, he died in a transit camp near Vladivostok. His wife Nadezhda memorised his unpublished poems to save them and later wrote the memoirs "Hope Against Hope".
Boris Pasternak|Russian poet and novelist (1890–1960) of Doctor Zhivago. Nobel Prize 1958.|Pasternak was born in Moscow; his father was a painter who illustrated Tolstoy and his mother a concert pianist. He studied music and philosophy before devoting himself to poetry; "My Sister, Life" (1922) made him famous. Under Stalin he survived by translating Shakespeare and Goethe. His novel "Doctor Zhivago", about a doctor and poet in the Revolution, was smuggled to Italy and published in 1957. He was awarded the Nobel Prize in 1958 but forced by the Soviet authorities to decline it. He died in Peredelkino two years later.
Joseph Brodsky|Russian-American poet (1940–1996), exiled from the USSR. Nobel Prize 1987.|Brodsky was born in Leningrad and left school at 15, working in a factory, a morgue and on geological expeditions while teaching himself languages and poetry. Akhmatova recognised his gifts. In 1964 he was tried for "social parasitism"; asked who had made him a poet, he answered, "I think it comes from God." He was sentenced to hard labour in the far north. Expelled from the USSR in 1972, he settled in America, taught at several colleges and became US Poet Laureate. He won the Nobel Prize in 1987. He never saw his parents again.
Seamus Heaney|Irish poet (1939–2013) of earth, memory and the Troubles. Nobel Prize 1995.|Heaney was born on a farm in County Derry, Northern Ireland, the eldest of nine children. A scholarship took him to boarding school and Queen's University Belfast. His first collection, "Death of a Naturalist", celebrated rural life; his famous poem "Digging" compares his pen to his father's spade. During the Troubles he wrote about violence and history, and moved to the Irish Republic in 1972. He taught at Harvard and Oxford, translated "Beowulf", and won the Nobel Prize in 1995. His last words, in a text to his wife, were "Noli timere", don't be afraid.
Jack Gilbert|American poet (1925–2012) of love, loss and joy.|Gilbert was born in Pittsburgh and worked in steel mills and as an exterminator. His first book, "Views of Jeopardy" (1962), won the Yale Younger Poets prize and brought sudden fame, but he withdrew and lived for years on Greek islands and elsewhere, publishing little. He wrote of love, especially for the poet Linda Gregg and his wife Michiko Nogami, whose death from cancer shaped "The Great Fires". His poems are plain, intense and searching. His "Collected Poems" appeared in 2012, the year he died.
George Seferis|Greek poet and diplomat (1900–1971). Nobel Prize 1963.|Giorgos Seferiadis was born in Smyrna (İzmir) in Asia Minor. His family moved to Athens in 1914, and the destruction of Smyrna in 1922 left him forever an exile from his childhood home. He studied law in Paris and served as a Greek diplomat for decades, finally as ambassador in London. His poems, such as "Mythistorema", join ancient myth to modern Greek history and loss. He became the first Greek to win the Nobel Prize, in 1963. In 1969 he spoke out against the military junta; his funeral in 1971 became a silent protest.
Wendell Berry|American farmer, poet and essayist (born 1934) who writes about land, community and care.|Berry was born in Henry County, Kentucky, where his family had farmed for generations. He studied and taught in California, New York and Europe, but in 1965 returned to farm a hillside property near the Kentucky River, working it with horses. He has written over fifty books of poems, essays and fiction about the Port William community. His essays, such as "The Unsettling of America", defend small farms, local economies and care for the land. His poems include "The Peace of Wild Things" and the "Mad Farmer" sequence.
Rebecca Solnit|American writer (born 1961) of A Field Guide to Getting Lost and Hope in the Dark.|Solnit grew up in California and became a writer and activist on environmental, land and human rights issues. Her books range widely: "Wanderlust" is a history of walking; "A Field Guide to Getting Lost" explores uncertainty; "Hope in the Dark" argues for hope based on the unexpected ways change happens; "A Paradise Built in Hell" shows how communities rise to help in disasters. Her essay "Men Explain Things to Me" inspired the word "mansplaining". She writes for many magazines.
Umberto Eco|Italian novelist and semiotician (1932–2016) of The Name of the Rose.|Eco was born in Alessandria, Italy, and wrote his thesis on Thomas Aquinas's aesthetics. He became a professor of semiotics, the study of signs, at Bologna, and wrote on everything from James Joyce to comic strips and James Bond. At 48 he published his first novel, "The Name of the Rose" (1980), a murder mystery in a medieval monastery, which sold tens of millions of copies. "Foucault's Pendulum" mocked conspiracy theories. He owned some 30,000 books and argued that an unread library is a tool for research.
Aldous Huxley|English writer (1894–1963) of Brave New World and The Perennial Philosophy.|Huxley was born in Surrey into a famous scientific family; his grandfather was "Darwin's bulldog", T. H. Huxley. As a teenager an eye disease left him nearly blind, ending hopes of a medical career. He wrote witty novels about the English intelligentsia, then the dystopia "Brave New World" (1932), about a society kept content by pleasure and conditioning. He moved to California in 1937, studied mysticism in "The Perennial Philosophy" and wrote of his experiments with mescaline in "The Doors of Perception". He died on the day John F. Kennedy was shot.
Aleksandr Solzhenitsyn|Russian writer (1918–2008) of The Gulag Archipelago. Nobel Prize 1970.|Solzhenitsyn was born in Kislovodsk; his father died before his birth. He studied mathematics and served as an artillery officer in the Second World War. In 1945 he was arrested for criticising Stalin in a letter and spent eight years in labour camps, then internal exile, and survived cancer. "One Day in the Life of Ivan Denisovich" (1962), published with Khrushchev's approval, shook the Soviet Union. "The Gulag Archipelago" documented the camp system. He won the Nobel Prize in 1970, was expelled in 1974, lived in Vermont and returned to Russia in 1994.
Ocean Vuong|Vietnamese-American poet and novelist (born 1988) of On Earth We're Briefly Gorgeous.|Vuong was born near Saigon, Vietnam; at two, he and his family emigrated to Hartford, Connecticut, as refugees. He was the first in his family to learn to read, at eleven. He studied at Brooklyn College and became a poet; "Night Sky with Exit Wounds" (2016) won the T. S. Eliot Prize. His novel "On Earth We're Briefly Gorgeous" (2019) is a letter from a son to his mother, who cannot read. He teaches at New York University. After his mother's death he wrote "Time Is a Mother".
Pierre Hadot|French philosopher and historian (1922–2010) who showed ancient philosophy as a way of life.|Hadot was born in Paris and ordained a Catholic priest, but left the priesthood in 1952. He studied ancient philosophy, especially Plotinus and Marcus Aurelius, and became a professor at the Collège de France. His book "Philosophy as a Way of Life" argued that ancient philosophy was not abstract theory but a set of "spiritual exercises" for transforming how one lives, through attention, meditation on death and the view from above. His work influenced Michel Foucault and the modern revival of Stoicism.
Carl Sagan|American astronomer and writer (1934–1996) of Cosmos and Pale Blue Dot.|Sagan grew up in Brooklyn, the son of a garment worker, and was fascinated by the stars from childhood. He became an astronomer at Cornell and worked on NASA's Mariner, Viking and Voyager missions. He helped design the plaques and Golden Records sent with Pioneer and Voyager. His television series "Cosmos" (1980) was watched by hundreds of millions. He won a Pulitzer Prize for "The Dragons of Eden", warned of "nuclear winter", and wrote "Pale Blue Dot" and "The Demon-Haunted World", a defence of scientific thinking. He died of a rare blood disease at 62.
Ivan Goncharov|Russian novelist (1812–1891) of Oblomov.|Goncharov was born into a wealthy merchant family in Simbirsk. He worked for thirty years as a civil servant, including as a government censor. In 1852 he sailed around the world on a Russian naval frigate to Japan, writing a travel book about it. His novel "Oblomov" (1859), about a kindly nobleman who spends most of his life lying on his sofa, unable to act, gave Russian the word "oblomovshchina", oblomovism. He wrote slowly, publishing only three novels, and became bitterly convinced that Turgenev had stolen his ideas.
Lev Shestov|Russian existentialist philosopher (1866–1938) who set faith against reason.|Lev Schwarzmann was born in Kyiv, the son of a Jewish textile merchant, and worked in his father's business before turning to writing. He emigrated after the Bolshevik Revolution and taught in Paris. In books such as "Athens and Jerusalem" and "All Things Are Possible", he attacked the dominance of reason and "self-evident" truths in philosophy, defending faith, freedom and the individual's cry against necessity. He wrote on Dostoevsky, Tolstoy, Nietzsche and Kierkegaard, and influenced Camus and the existentialists.
Vasily Rozanov|Russian writer and thinker (1856–1919) of intimate, fragmentary books like Solitaria.|Rozanov was born in Vetluga, central Russia, into a poor family, and orphaned young. He taught in provincial schools for years and married Apollinaria Suslova, Dostoevsky's former lover, an unhappy marriage. He became a journalist in St Petersburg, writing on religion, family and sex. His books "Solitaria" and "Fallen Leaves" are fragments jotted down anywhere, on the backs of envelopes, in a cab, recording passing thoughts with startling intimacy. He died in poverty and hunger at Sergiev Posad in 1919.
Georg Büchner|German dramatist (1813–1837) of Woyzeck and Danton's Death, who died at 23.|Büchner was born near Darmstadt, the son of a doctor. A medical student, he co-wrote "The Hessian Courier", a pamphlet calling on peasants to revolt with the slogan "Peace to the cottages! War on the palaces!" Wanted by the police, he fled to Strasbourg and then Zurich, where he taught anatomy. In just over two years he wrote the play "Danton's Death", the story "Lenz", and "Woyzeck", about a poor soldier driven to murder, which is considered the first modern working-class tragedy. He died of typhus at 23.
Luigi Pirandello|Italian dramatist and novelist (1867–1936) of Six Characters in Search of an Author. Nobel Prize 1934.|Pirandello was born in Sicily, the son of a sulphur-mine owner. He studied in Rome and Bonn. When the family mine flooded, the shock drove his wife into lasting mental illness; he cared for her for years. His novel "The Late Mattia Pascal" and plays like "Six Characters in Search of an Author" (1921), in which characters abandoned by their author interrupt a rehearsal, explore the masks we wear and the instability of identity. He won the Nobel Prize in 1934.
Italo Svevo|Italian novelist (1861–1928) of Zeno's Conscience.|Aron Hector Schmitz was born in Trieste, then part of Austria-Hungary, to a Jewish family. He worked in a bank, then in his wife's family's marine paint business. His first two novels were ignored. He took English lessons from James Joyce, who admired his work. In 1923 he published "Zeno's Conscience", a comic novel written as the memoir of a man trying to quit smoking for a psychoanalyst. Joyce helped bring it to French critics, and Svevo became famous shortly before his death in a car accident in 1928.
August Strindberg|Swedish playwright and novelist (1849–1912).|Strindberg was born in Stockholm, the son of a shipping agent and a former servant, and felt like a "son of a servant" all his life. He tried acting, medicine, journalism and library work. His novel "The Red Room" made him famous. Married three times, he wrote searing plays about the war between the sexes, "The Father" and "Miss Julie". After a period of breakdown and alchemical experiments in Paris, described in "Inferno", he wrote dreamlike plays, such as "A Dream Play" and "The Ghost Sonata", that anticipated expressionism. He was also a painter and photographer.
Karl Jaspers|German psychiatrist and existentialist philosopher (1883–1969).|Jaspers was born in Oldenburg, Germany, and had a chronic lung condition all his life. He trained as a psychiatrist and wrote "General Psychopathology", a foundational text. He then turned to philosophy, exploring "limit situations" such as death, suffering and guilt that awaken us to existence. Because his wife Gertrud was Jewish, he was forced out of his professorship by the Nazis; they kept poison ready in case of deportation. After the war he wrote "The Question of German Guilt" and introduced the idea of the "Axial Age". He was Hannah Arendt's teacher and friend.
Martin Heidegger|German philosopher (1889–1976) of Being and Time.|Heidegger was born in Messkirch, Germany, the son of a church sexton, and trained for the priesthood before turning to philosophy. His "Being and Time" (1927) asked what it means "to be", analysing human existence as being-in-the-world, care, anxiety and being-towards-death. He had an affair with his student Hannah Arendt. In 1933 he joined the Nazi Party and became rector of Freiburg University, a stain his posthumously published notebooks deepened. He spent much time in a hut in the Black Forest. He influenced existentialism and much of modern philosophy.
Gabriel Marcel|French Christian existentialist philosopher and playwright (1889–1973).|Marcel was born in Paris; his mother died when he was four. He worked for the Red Cross in the First World War, tracing missing soldiers, which shaped his thinking about presence and absence. A playwright and philosopher, he converted to Catholicism in 1929. He distinguished "problems", which can be solved from outside, from "mysteries", like love and death, in which we are involved. "Being and Having" contrasts possession with participation. He hosted weekly discussions in his apartment that drew Sartre, Levinas and Ricoeur.
Nikolai Berdyaev|Russian religious philosopher (1874–1948) of freedom and creativity.|Berdyaev was born to an aristocratic family in Kyiv. As a young Marxist he was exiled to the north, then turned to Christianity. He criticised the Bolsheviks and in 1922 was expelled from Russia on the "philosophers' ship". In Paris he founded a religious-philosophical academy and wrote books such as "The Destiny of Man" and "Slavery and Freedom", arguing for the primacy of freedom and creativity and the dignity of each person over all collectives. He was nominated many times for the Nobel Prize in Literature.
José Ortega y Gasset|Spanish philosopher (1883–1955): "I am I and my circumstance."|Ortega was born in Madrid to a family of journalists and studied in Germany. He became professor of metaphysics at Madrid and founded the influential journal "Revista de Occidente". His phrase "I am I and my circumstance" sums up his view that life is always lived in a particular situation. "The Revolt of the Masses" (1930) warned against the rise of the "mass man" who demands rights without responsibilities. He supported the Spanish Republic but went into exile during the Civil War, returning in 1945.
Paul Tillich|German-American theologian (1886–1965) of The Courage to Be.|Tillich was born in Prussia, the son of a Lutheran pastor. As an army chaplain in the First World War he buried countless soldiers, and the experience shattered his old faith. A socialist professor, he was among the first non-Jewish academics dismissed by the Nazis in 1933. He moved to New York, where he taught at Union Theological Seminary, Harvard and Chicago. "The Courage to Be" (1952) explores anxiety and faith; he defined faith as "ultimate concern" and God as "the ground of being". He became famous far beyond theology.
Walker Percy|American novelist (1916–1990) of The Moviegoer.|Percy was born in Birmingham, Alabama. His father took his own life when Walker was 13, and his mother died soon after in a car accident; he was raised by his uncle, William Alexander Percy, in Mississippi. He became a doctor but contracted tuberculosis as an intern. During his recovery he read Kierkegaard and Dostoevsky, converted to Catholicism and began writing. His first novel, "The Moviegoer" (1961), about a stockbroker searching for meaning in New Orleans, won the National Book Award. "Lost in the Cosmos" is a playful "last self-help book".
Ralph Ellison|American novelist (1913–1994) of Invisible Man.|Ellison was born in Oklahoma City and named after Ralph Waldo Emerson; his father died when he was three. He studied music at the Tuskegee Institute, hoping to compose a symphony, then moved to New York, where Richard Wright encouraged him to write. He served as a cook in the merchant marine in the war. "Invisible Man" (1952), about a young Black man whom society refuses to see, won the National Book Award. He spent the rest of his life on a second novel; a fire destroyed part of the manuscript, and it was published after his death as "Juneteenth".
Robert Musil|Austrian novelist (1880–1942) of The Man Without Qualities.|Musil was born in Klagenfurt, Austria, and trained at a military academy, an experience behind his first novel, "The Confusions of Young Törless". He studied engineering, then philosophy and psychology, and invented a colour wheel. He served as an officer in the First World War. From the 1920s he worked on his enormous novel "The Man Without Qualities", about a man in Vienna on the eve of the war who refuses to settle into a fixed role. Living in poverty and exile in Switzerland, he died in 1942 with it unfinished.
André Malraux|French novelist and statesman (1901–1976) of Man's Fate.|Malraux left school at 17 and dealt in rare books. In 1923 he was arrested for removing sculptures from a Cambodian temple. In Indochina he edited an anti-colonial newspaper. "Man's Fate" (1933), about a failed communist uprising in Shanghai, won the Prix Goncourt. He organised an air squadron for the Republic in the Spanish Civil War, fought in the French Resistance and was captured. Under de Gaulle he became France's first Minister of Cultural Affairs, cleaning the façades of Paris and founding cultural centres. He wrote on "the museum without walls".
Eugène Ionesco|Romanian-French playwright (1909–1994) of the Theatre of the Absurd.|Ionesco was born in Romania to a Romanian father and French mother, spent his childhood in France, and settled in Paris in 1938. Learning English from a textbook, he was struck by its absurd sentences, which inspired his first play, "The Bald Soprano" (1950). His plays, including "The Chairs" and "Rhinoceros", in which a town's inhabitants turn into rhinoceroses, satirising conformity and fascism, made him a leader of the "Theatre of the Absurd". He was elected to the Académie française in 1970.
Robert Burton|English scholar and clergyman (1577–1640) whose Anatomy of Melancholy is a vast, witty encyclopedia of sadness.|Burton was a scholar at Christ Church, Oxford, for almost his whole life, serving as librarian and vicar of St Thomas's in Oxford. He never married and suffered from melancholy. To cure himself he wrote "The Anatomy of Melancholy" (1621), under the name Democritus Junior, a vast, rambling survey of the causes, symptoms and cures of depression, quoting hundreds of authors. He revised it for the rest of his life. It was said he could only be cheered by listening to the bargemen swearing at the river. Samuel Johnson said it was the only book that got him out of bed two hours early.
John Ruskin|English art critic and social thinker (1819–1900) of Modern Painters and Unto This Last.|Ruskin was the only child of a wealthy sherry merchant in London and was educated at home by a strict mother. At 24 he published the first volume of "Modern Painters", defending Turner. "The Stones of Venice" argued that Gothic architecture expressed the freedom and joy of its workers. He turned to social criticism in "Unto This Last", which Gandhi said changed his life: "There is no wealth but life." He founded the Guild of St George and drawing schools. His marriage to Effie Gray was annulled. He suffered mental breakdowns in his last decades.
William Hazlitt|English essayist and critic (1778–1830) of Table-Talk.|Hazlitt was the son of a Unitarian minister who had supported American independence. He trained as a painter, then met Coleridge, which turned him to writing. He became one of the great essayists and critics in English, writing on Shakespeare, the Romantic poets, painting and boxing. His essays include "On Going a Journey" and "The Pleasure of Hating". "Liber Amoris" told his obsessive love for his landlady's daughter. A lifelong radical, he admired Napoleon. He died in poverty in Soho, saying "Well, I've had a happy life."
Kamo no Chōmei|Japanese poet and recluse (c. 1155–1216) whose Hōjōki (An Account of My Hut) meditates on impermanence.|Chōmei was born into a family of Shinto priests at the Kamo shrine in Kyoto and hoped to succeed his father, but was passed over. A gifted poet and musician, he served at the imperial poetry bureau. Around fifty he became a Buddhist monk and lived in a tiny hut ten feet square in the hills of Hino, which he could take apart and move. There, in 1212, he wrote "Hōjōki" (An Account of My Hut), describing fires, earthquakes, famine and whirlwinds in Kyoto, and the peace of his simple life. It begins with the image of a river that never stops flowing.
Yoshida Kenkō|Japanese Buddhist monk and writer (c. 1283–1350) of Essays in Idleness (Tsurezuregusa).|Urabe Kaneyoshi was born into a family of Shinto priests and served as an officer at the imperial court. In about his thirties he became a Buddhist monk, but lived not in a monastery but in the capital and its outskirts, moving among poets and aristocrats. His "Essays in Idleness" (Tsurezuregusa), 243 short pieces, mix anecdotes, advice and reflections on impermanence and beauty, such as the beauty of the moon half-hidden by clouds or cherry blossoms after they fall. It has been read in Japanese schools for centuries.
Han Shan|"Cold Mountain", a legendary Chinese hermit poet of the Tang dynasty, beloved of Zen.|Han Shan, "Cold Mountain", is a legendary Chinese poet who lived as a hermit on Cold Mountain in the Tiantai range, perhaps in the 8th or 9th century. Stories describe him as a ragged, laughing figure who visited a monastery to eat leftovers with his friend Shide, a kitchen worker. His poems, said to have been written on rocks, trees and walls, speak of mountain solitude, Buddhist and Daoist insight and mockery of worldly people. Translations by Gary Snyder in 1958 made him a hero of the Beat Generation.
Baltasar Gracián|Spanish Jesuit writer (1601–1658) of The Art of Worldly Wisdom.|Gracián was born in Aragon, Spain, and became a Jesuit priest and professor. He served as an army chaplain and was said to have shown great bravery. His "Pocket Oracle and Art of Prudence" (1647), 300 brief maxims on living wisely in a dangerous world, has been translated as "The Art of Worldly Wisdom". His allegorical novel "El Criticón" influenced Schopenhauer, who translated the "Oracle" into German. He published without his order's permission, and was punished with bread and water and banishment to a small town, where he died.
Horace|Roman poet (65–8 BC) of the Odes and Satires: "Carpe diem."|Quintus Horatius Flaccus was born in Venusia, southern Italy, the son of a freed slave who spent his savings on the boy's education in Rome and Athens. He fought on the losing side at Philippi against Octavian and lost his property. Virgil introduced him to Maecenas, who gave him a farm in the Sabine hills, which he loved. His "Odes", "Satires" and "Epistles", polished and wise, celebrate friendship, moderation and the passing moment: "carpe diem", seize the day. His "Art of Poetry" shaped European literary theory for centuries.
Héloïse|French scholar and abbess (c. 1100–1164) whose letters to Abelard are among the great love letters.|Héloïse grew up in Paris under the care of her uncle Fulbert, a canon of Notre-Dame, and was famous for her learning in Latin, Greek and Hebrew. Her tutor, the philosopher Peter Abelard, became her lover; she bore a son, Astrolabe, and they married secretly. Her uncle's men castrated Abelard in revenge, and both entered religious life. She became abbess of the Paraclete convent. Years later, their letters, hers passionate and brilliant, made them the most famous lovers of the Middle Ages. They are buried together at Père Lachaise cemetery in Paris.
Peter Abelard|French philosopher and theologian (1079–1142), Héloïse's lover and husband.|Abelard was born in Brittany, the eldest son of a knight, but gave up his inheritance for philosophy. In Paris he became the most famous teacher of his day, defeating his masters in debate. His love affair with his student Héloïse ended in his castration by her uncle's men. He became a monk and continued to teach, his book "Sic et Non" (Yes and No) setting contradictory authorities side by side to provoke inquiry. He was twice condemned for heresy, the second time through Bernard of Clairvaux. His autobiography is "The Story of My Calamities".
The Cloud of Unknowing|An anonymous 14th-century English guide to contemplative prayer.|"The Cloud of Unknowing" is an anonymous English mystical guide written in the late 14th century, probably by a Carthusian monk or country priest. Written for a young person beginning contemplative life, it teaches that God cannot be known by thought but only by love. The seeker must put all thoughts beneath a "cloud of forgetting" and reach toward God through a "cloud of unknowing" with a "sharp dart of longing love", perhaps using a single word like "God" or "love". It is a forerunner of modern centering prayer.
Angelus Silesius|German mystic and poet (1624–1677) of The Cherubinic Wanderer.|Johann Scheffler was born in Breslau (Wrocław), Silesia, to a Lutheran family, and studied medicine. Drawn to mysticism, he became court physician, then converted to Catholicism in 1653, taking the name Angelus Silesius, "Silesian Angel". He became a priest and wrote fiercely against Protestants. His "Cherubinic Wanderer" (1657), short rhyming couplets on God and the soul, is a classic of mystical poetry: "The rose is without why; it blooms because it blooms." He gave his wealth to the poor and died in a monastery.
Elizabeth Barrett Browning|English poet (1806–1861) of Sonnets from the Portuguese and Aurora Leigh.|Elizabeth Barrett grew up at Hope End in Herefordshire, the eldest of twelve children, reading Greek and writing poetry from childhood. A spinal injury and lung illness made her an invalid, and the drowning of her favourite brother devastated her. By 1844 she was more famous than Robert Browning, who wrote to admire her poems; they married secretly in 1846 and fled to Italy, where her health improved. Her "Sonnets from the Portuguese", including "How do I love thee?", and the verse-novel "Aurora Leigh" are her best-known works. She campaigned against slavery and child labour.
Gustavo Adolfo Bécquer|Spanish Romantic poet (1836–1870) of the Rimas.|Bécquer was born in Seville; his father, a painter, died when he was five and his mother when he was ten. He moved to Madrid hoping for literary fame, but lived in poverty as a journalist and censor. His "Rimas" (Rhymes), short, intimate poems of love and longing, and his eerie "Leyendas" (Legends) were collected by friends after his death from tuberculosis at 34. They made him one of the most read Spanish poets, beginning modern Spanish poetry.
Fyodor Tyutchev|Russian poet and diplomat (1803–1873) of nature, night and the soul.|Tyutchev was born to an aristocratic family near Bryansk and spent twenty-two years as a diplomat in Munich and Turin, marrying two German women in turn. He wrote poetry privately and rarely published it, until Nekrasov and Turgenev championed it. His short metaphysical lyrics, on night, chaos, nature and love, are among the greatest in Russian. One famous poem, "Silentium!", urges keeping one's inner life silent; another says that "Russia cannot be understood with the mind". His late love for Elena Denisyeva inspired a moving cycle.
J. R. R. Tolkien|English writer and philologist (1892–1973) of The Hobbit and The Lord of the Rings.|John Ronald Reuel Tolkien was born in South Africa; his father died when he was three and his mother when he was twelve, and he was raised by a Catholic priest in Birmingham. He fought at the Somme, where most of his close friends were killed. As a professor of Anglo-Saxon at Oxford, he invented languages, then a mythology to hold them. "The Hobbit" (1937) began as a story for his children; "The Lord of the Rings" took twelve years. His lecture on "Beowulf" changed its study. He and his wife Edith share a grave inscribed "Beren" and "Lúthien".
Richard Jefferies|English nature writer (1848–1887) whose The Story of My Heart is a mystical autobiography of the soul in nature.|Jefferies was born on a small farm near Swindon in Wiltshire, and spent his boyhood roaming the downs. He became a local journalist, then wrote books of rural life and nature such as "The Gamekeeper at Home" and the children's classic "Bevis". His spiritual autobiography "The Story of My Heart" (1883) describes ecstatic experiences of the earth, sun and sea. Chronic tuberculosis, poverty and pain marked his last years; he wrote some of his finest essays dictating to his wife. He died at 38.
Maurice Maeterlinck|Belgian poet, playwright and essayist (1862–1949) of The Blue Bird and The Treasure of the Humble. Nobel Prize 1911.|Maeterlinck was born in Ghent, Belgium, and practised law briefly before turning to writing in Paris. His plays, such as "Pelléas and Mélisande", which Debussy turned into an opera, created a dreamlike, symbolist theatre of mystery and fate. His fairy play "The Blue Bird" (1908), in which two children search for the bird of happiness only to find it at home, was a worldwide success. He wrote popular books on the lives of bees, ants and termites, and on silence and the soul. He won the Nobel Prize in 1911.
John Muir|Scottish-American naturalist and writer (1838–1914), "father of the national parks", who walked and wrote the Sierra Nevada.|Muir was born in Dunbar, Scotland, and emigrated with his family to a Wisconsin farm at eleven, where his strict father worked him hard. An inventor as a young man, he was nearly blinded in a factory accident; when his sight returned, he decided to devote his life to nature. He walked a thousand miles to the Gulf of Mexico, then went to California's Yosemite Valley. He explored glaciers in Alaska, wrote passionately about wilderness, persuaded Theodore Roosevelt to protect Yosemite, and co-founded the Sierra Club in 1892. He is called "the father of the national parks".
A. E. Housman|English scholar and poet (1859–1936) of A Shropshire Lad.|Alfred Edward Housman was born in Worcestershire, the eldest of seven children; his mother died on his twelfth birthday. At Oxford he fell in love with a fellow student, Moses Jackson, who did not return his feelings, and he unexpectedly failed his final exams. He worked for ten years as a patent clerk while publishing scholarly papers, and became a professor of Latin at University College London and then Cambridge, a famously exacting scholar. "A Shropshire Lad" (1896), short lyrics of lost youth and early death, became hugely popular, especially among soldiers.
Edward Thomas|English poet and nature writer (1878–1917), friend of Robert Frost, killed at the Battle of Arras.|Thomas was born in London to Welsh parents and married young. He wrote for years as a hard-pressed reviewer and author of nature books and biographies to support his family, suffering bouts of depression. His friendship with Robert Frost encouraged him, at 36, to write poetry; in two years he wrote some 140 poems, including "Adlestrop" and "Old Man". He enlisted in 1915, though he could have avoided service, and was killed by a shell at the Battle of Arras in April 1917. Most of his poems were published after his death.
The Qur'an|The holy book of Islam, revealed to the Prophet Muhammad in the 7th century.|Muslims believe the Qur'an was revealed by God to the Prophet Muhammad through the angel Gabriel over about 23 years, from 610 AD until his death in 632, in Mecca and Medina. It was recited and memorised by his companions and compiled into a written book soon after his death. Its 114 chapters (surahs) speak of God's oneness and mercy, the prophets, justice, care for orphans and the poor, and the signs of God in nature. Its Arabic is considered inimitable, and it is recited daily in prayer by Muslims worldwide. Translations are seen as interpretations of its meaning.
Mirza Ghalib|Urdu and Persian poet of Delhi (1797–1869), the great master of the Urdu ghazal.|Mirza Asadullah Khan Ghalib was born in Agra to a family of Turkic aristocratic soldiers; his father died when he was five. He moved to Delhi, where he lived the rest of his life, mostly in debt, enjoying wine and gambling. He wrote in Persian and Urdu, and his ghazals, full of irony, wit and philosophical depth on love, loss and the divine, are among the most quoted in South Asia. All seven of his children died in infancy. He witnessed the destruction of Delhi after the 1857 uprising, described in his letters. He was poet laureate to the last Mughal emperor.
Hildegard of Bingen|German abbess, mystic, composer and healer (1098–1179).|Hildegard was the tenth child of a noble family in the Rhineland, given to the Church as a girl and enclosed with the anchoress Jutta. She had visions from childhood and at 42 was commanded, she said, to write them down, producing "Scivias". She became abbess and founded her own convent at Rupertsberg. She composed some of the most beautiful music of the Middle Ages, wrote on medicine and natural science, invented a secret language, corresponded with popes and emperors and went on preaching tours in her sixties. She was declared a Doctor of the Church in 2012.
Chuang Tzu|Zhuangzi (c. 369–286 BC), Chinese Daoist philosopher of paradox and freedom.|Chuang Tzu is an older spelling of Zhuangzi, the Chinese philosopher of the 4th century BC. According to tradition he lived in the state of Song, worked briefly as a minor official and refused high office, preferring freedom. The book bearing his name, partly written by him and partly by followers, uses stories, jokes and paradoxes, such as the butterfly dream and the cook who carves an ox effortlessly, to teach spontaneity, acceptance of change and freedom from fixed viewpoints. It is a founding text of Daoism.
George Berkeley|Irish philosopher and bishop (1685–1753) who argued that to be is to be perceived.|Berkeley was born in Ireland and studied at Trinity College Dublin, where in his twenties he wrote his main works. He argued that material objects exist only as perceived ("esse est percipi"), with God perceiving everything always. He spent three years in Rhode Island waiting for funds to found a college in Bermuda, which never came. He became Bishop of Cloyne in Ireland, cared for the poor during famine, and promoted tar-water as a cure-all. The city of Berkeley, California, is named after him.
John Locke|English philosopher (1632–1704) of empiricism and natural rights.|Locke was born in Somerset, the son of a lawyer who fought for Parliament in the Civil War. He studied and taught at Oxford and trained as a physician, becoming doctor and adviser to the Earl of Shaftesbury, whose life he saved with an operation. Suspected of plotting, he fled to Holland in 1683. After the Glorious Revolution he returned and published "An Essay Concerning Human Understanding", arguing that the mind begins as a blank slate, and "Two Treatises of Government", defending natural rights and government by consent. His "Letter Concerning Toleration" argued for religious freedom.
John Stuart Mill|English philosopher (1806–1873) of On Liberty and Utilitarianism.|Mill was educated by his father, the philosopher James Mill, in an intense experiment: Greek at three, Latin at eight. At twenty he suffered a breakdown and found healing in Wordsworth's poetry. He worked for 35 years for the East India Company. He loved Harriet Taylor for twenty years before they could marry, and credited her as co-author of much of his work. "On Liberty" (1859) defends free speech and individuality; "Utilitarianism" refines the greatest happiness principle. As an MP he proposed votes for women. He spent his last years in Avignon, near Harriet's grave.
William of Ockham|English Franciscan philosopher (c. 1287–1347).|William was born in the village of Ockham in Surrey and became a Franciscan friar, studying and teaching at Oxford. Summoned to the papal court at Avignon to answer charges of heresy, he took the side of his order in a dispute with Pope John XXII about poverty, fled in 1328 and was excommunicated. He lived under the protection of Emperor Louis IV in Munich, writing against papal power. In logic he championed economy of explanation, the principle later called "Occam's razor". He probably died of the Black Death.
John Rawls|American philosopher (1921–2002) of A Theory of Justice.|Rawls was born in Baltimore. As a boy, he caught diphtheria and pneumonia and passed them to two younger brothers, who died, leaving him with a lifelong stammer. He served as an infantryman in the Pacific in the Second World War, which shook his religious faith. He taught at Harvard for nearly forty years. "A Theory of Justice" (1971) asks what principles we would choose behind a "veil of ignorance", not knowing our place in society, and revived political philosophy. He was shy and modest, and declined many honours.
Charles Darwin|English naturalist (1809–1882) whose On the Origin of Species explained evolution by natural selection.|Darwin was born in Shrewsbury, the grandson of Erasmus Darwin and Josiah Wedgwood. He neglected medicine at Edinburgh and divinity at Cambridge for beetle-collecting. At 22 he sailed as naturalist on HMS Beagle for five years, observing fossils, finches and tortoises in South America and the Galápagos. Back home, he developed his theory of natural selection in secret for twenty years, until Alfred Russel Wallace sent him the same idea. "On the Origin of Species" (1859) followed. He lived quietly at Down House in Kent, often ill, and grieved deeply for his daughter Annie.
James Hutton|Scottish geologist (1726–1797), founder of modern geology.|Hutton was born in Edinburgh, studied medicine and chemistry, and made money from a process for producing sal ammoniac. He farmed in Berwickshire and became fascinated with soil and rocks. Observing places like Siccar Point, where tilted layers of rock lie beneath flat ones, he concluded that the Earth is shaped by slow, ongoing processes over immense time, finding "no vestige of a beginning, no prospect of an end". His "Theory of the Earth" (1788) made him the father of modern geology. He was part of the Scottish Enlightenment with Adam Smith and Joseph Black.
Pierre-Simon Laplace|French mathematician and astronomer (1749–1827).|Laplace was born in Normandy, the son of a farmer, and went to Paris at 19 with letters of introduction; d'Alembert, impressed by his mathematics, found him a teaching post. His "Celestial Mechanics" showed that the solar system is stable, applying Newton's laws in detail. He developed probability theory, proposed that the solar system formed from a spinning nebula, and imagined objects so massive that light cannot escape them. Asked by Napoleon where God was in his system, he is said to have replied, "I had no need of that hypothesis."
Rudolf Clausius|German physicist (1822–1888) who formulated the second law of thermodynamics.|Clausius was born in Köslin, Prussia, one of many children of a pastor and school inspector. He studied in Berlin and taught at Zurich, Würzburg and Bonn. In 1850 he set out the second law of thermodynamics, that heat does not flow on its own from cold to hot, and in 1865 he introduced the word "entropy". He summarised: "The energy of the universe is constant; the entropy of the universe tends to a maximum." In the Franco-Prussian war he led an ambulance corps of students and was wounded.
Nicolaus Copernicus|Polish astronomer (1473–1543) who put the Sun at the centre.|Copernicus was born in Toruń, Poland; after his father's death he was raised by his uncle, a bishop. He studied in Kraków, Bologna and Padua, learning law, medicine and astronomy. As a canon of Frombork Cathedral, he managed estates, practised medicine and wrote on currency reform. Privately he developed the idea that the Earth and planets orbit the Sun. Fearing ridicule, he delayed publishing "On the Revolutions of the Heavenly Spheres" until 1543; legend says he was handed the first printed copy on his deathbed.
Galileo Galilei|Italian astronomer and physicist (1564–1642), tried by the Inquisition for defending Copernicus.|Galileo was born in Pisa, the son of a musician, and studied medicine before turning to mathematics. He studied falling bodies and pendulums. In 1609 he built a telescope and discovered the moons of Jupiter, the phases of Venus and mountains on the Moon, evidence that supported Copernicus. His "Dialogue Concerning the Two Chief World Systems" (1632) led to his trial by the Inquisition, forced recantation and house arrest for life. There, going blind, he wrote "Two New Sciences", founding modern physics. His daughter, a nun, wrote him loving letters.
Werner Heisenberg|German physicist (1901–1976), a founder of quantum mechanics.|Heisenberg was born in Würzburg, the son of a professor of Greek. At 23, recovering from hay fever on the island of Heligoland, he worked out the first form of quantum mechanics. In 1927 he formulated the uncertainty principle. He won the Nobel Prize in 1932. He stayed in Germany under the Nazis and led its nuclear research programme in the war; whether he deliberately slowed it remains debated, and is the subject of the play "Copenhagen". After the war he rebuilt German science. He was a fine pianist.
Alfred Wegener|German scientist (1880–1930) who proposed continental drift.|Wegener was born in Berlin, earned a doctorate in astronomy and became a meteorologist and polar explorer, setting a record for a balloon flight with his brother. Noticing how the coasts of Africa and South America fit together, and that matching fossils occur on both, he proposed in 1912 that the continents had drifted apart. Geologists mocked him for decades. He died in 1930 on his fourth expedition to Greenland, after bringing supplies to colleagues at a remote ice station. Plate tectonics vindicated him in the 1960s.
Karl Popper|Austrian-British philosopher (1902–1994) of science and the open society.|Popper was born in Vienna into a family of Jewish origin; as a young man he worked as a cabinet-maker's apprentice and in Alfred Adler's child guidance clinics. "The Logic of Scientific Discovery" (1934) argued that science advances by bold conjectures that can be falsified. Fleeing Nazism, he taught in New Zealand, where he wrote "The Open Society and Its Enemies", a defence of liberal democracy against Plato, Hegel and Marx. He later taught at the London School of Economics and was knighted. He believed that we learn from our mistakes.
Thomas Kuhn|American historian of science (1922–1996).|Kuhn was born in Cincinnati and trained as a physicist at Harvard. Teaching a course in the history of science, he read Aristotle's physics and suddenly understood it as making sense on its own terms, which changed his thinking. "The Structure of Scientific Revolutions" (1962) argued that science moves through long periods of "normal science" within a "paradigm", interrupted by revolutions in which the whole framework changes. It became one of the most cited books of the century and popularised the term "paradigm shift".
Edward Lorenz|American mathematician and meteorologist (1917–2008), a founder of chaos theory.|Lorenz was born in West Hartford, Connecticut, studied mathematics and served as a weather forecaster in the US Army Air Corps in the Second World War. At MIT in 1961 he re-ran a weather simulation with numbers rounded to three decimal places instead of six, and found that the tiny difference produced completely different weather. This led to chaos theory and the "butterfly effect", named after his 1972 talk "Does the Flap of a Butterfly's Wings in Brazil Set Off a Tornado in Texas?" He was also a keen hiker and mountaineer.
Lynn Margulis|American biologist (1938–2011).|Margulis entered the University of Chicago at 15. She married the young astronomer Carl Sagan at 19. Her 1967 paper proposing that mitochondria and chloroplasts were once free-living bacteria that came to live inside other cells was rejected by about fifteen journals before being published; it is now accepted as fact. With James Lovelock she developed the Gaia hypothesis. She emphasised cooperation and symbiosis in evolution and was known for challenging orthodoxy. She taught for many years at the University of Massachusetts Amherst.
Ivan Pavlov|Russian physiologist (1849–1936). Nobel Prize 1904.|Pavlov was born in Ryazan, the son of a village priest, and studied at a seminary before reading Darwin and switching to science. As a physiologist in St Petersburg, he studied digestion in dogs, winning the Nobel Prize in 1904. He noticed that dogs began to salivate before food arrived, at the sight of the attendant, and studied this "conditioned reflex" for the rest of his life.
