Though critical of the Bolsheviks, he was allowed to keep working. He is said to have studied even his own dying, describing his symptoms to a colleague.
Abraham Maslow|American psychologist (1908–1970).|Maslow was born in Brooklyn, the eldest of seven children of Jewish immigrants from Russia; he was lonely and had a difficult relationship with his mother. He studied with the primate researcher Harry Harlow and later taught at Brandeis University. Rather than studying illness, he studied healthy, creative people. His "hierarchy of needs" (1943) proposed that once basic needs are met, people seek "self-actualisation". He studied "peak experiences" and helped found humanistic psychology. He died of a heart attack while jogging at 62.
Mihaly Csikszentmihalyi|Hungarian-American psychologist (1934–2021) of Flow.|Csikszentmihalyi was born in Fiume (now Rijeka) to a Hungarian family. As a boy during the Second World War he was interned in Italy and saw adults' lives fall apart; he found relief in playing chess. He heard Carl Jung lecture on flying saucers in Switzerland and decided to study psychology, emigrating to the US at 22. At the University of Chicago he studied artists, climbers and surgeons absorbed in their work and named the state "flow". His book "Flow" (1990) became a classic of positive psychology.
Carol Dweck|American psychologist (born 1946).|Dweck grew up in Brooklyn. In sixth grade her teacher seated pupils by IQ score, an experience she later saw as fostering a fear of failure. She studied psychology at Barnard and Yale and taught at Columbia and Stanford. Studying how children respond to difficult puzzles, she found that some relished the challenge while others gave up. She explained the difference as a "growth mindset", believing abilities can be developed, versus a "fixed mindset". Her book "Mindset" (2006) became hugely influential in schools and business, though later studies suggest its effects are smaller than first claimed.
Adam Smith|Scottish economist and philosopher (1723–1790) of The Wealth of Nations and The Theory of Moral Sentiments.|Smith was born in Kirkcaldy, Scotland; his father died before his birth, and as a small child, the story goes, he was briefly carried off by travellers. He studied at Glasgow and Oxford and became professor of moral philosophy at Glasgow. "The Theory of Moral Sentiments" (1759) explains morality through sympathy and an imagined "impartial spectator". After touring France as a tutor, where he met Voltaire, he spent ten years writing "The Wealth of Nations" (1776), which explained the division of labour, free markets and the "invisible hand". He ended his life as a customs commissioner in Edinburgh, famously absent-minded.
Thomas Malthus|English economist and cleric (1766–1834).|Malthus was born in Surrey, the son of a gentleman who admired Rousseau. He studied mathematics at Cambridge and became an Anglican curate. Arguing with his father's optimism about human progress, he published "An Essay on the Principle of Population" (1798), claiming that population tends to grow faster than food supplies, keeping most people near poverty. He became the first professor of political economy in Britain, at the East India Company's college. His ideas inspired both Darwin and Wallace in their theory of natural selection.
Thorstein Veblen|American economist (1857–1929).|Veblen was born in Wisconsin to Norwegian immigrant farmers and grew up speaking Norwegian. He earned a doctorate at Yale but could not find an academic job for seven years, and lived on his family's farm reading. An eccentric and difficult teacher, he moved between universities, often dismissed for scandals. "The Theory of the Leisure Class" (1899) introduced "conspicuous consumption" and "conspicuous leisure", wealth displayed to win status. He criticised business for valuing profit over useful production. He died in a cabin in California shortly before the 1929 crash.
Alexis de Tocqueville|French political thinker (1805–1859) of Democracy in America.|Tocqueville was born into an aristocratic Norman family; several relatives were guillotined in the Revolution, and his parents narrowly escaped. In 1831, aged 25, he travelled through the United States for nine months, officially to study prisons. "Democracy in America" (1835, 1840) analysed American equality, associations, religion and the risk of a "tyranny of the majority" with remarkable foresight. He served in the French parliament and briefly as foreign minister. "The Old Regime and the Revolution" explained the roots of 1789. He died of tuberculosis at 53.
Thomas Hobbes|English philosopher (1588–1679) of Leviathan.|Hobbes was born prematurely in Malmesbury, Wiltshire, he said, because his mother was frightened by news of the Spanish Armada: "fear and I were born twins". He was tutor and secretary to the Cavendish family for decades and met Galileo and Descartes. Fleeing the English Civil War to Paris, he wrote "Leviathan" (1651), arguing that without a strong sovereign, life would be "solitary, poor, nasty, brutish, and short", and that people agree to obey a ruler for security. He translated Homer in his eighties and lived to 91.
David Ricardo|British economist (1772–1823).|Ricardo was born in London, the third of seventeen children of a Sephardic Jewish stockbroker from Amsterdam. At 21 he eloped with a Quaker woman and was disowned by his family. He made a fortune on the stock exchange, reportedly by betting on Wellington's victory at Waterloo. After reading Adam Smith on holiday, he turned to economics. "On the Principles of Political Economy and Taxation" (1817) set out comparative advantage and the theory of rent. He served as an MP, arguing for free trade. He died at 51.
Frédéric Bastiat|French economist and writer (1801–1850).|Bastiat was born in Bayonne, France, and orphaned at nine. He worked in his uncle's business, then managed a farm in the Landes. Reading Adam Smith and the English free-trade campaigners, he began writing in his forties. In just six years he produced witty essays, such as the "Candlemakers' Petition", in which candlemakers ask for protection against the unfair competition of the sun, and "What Is Seen and What Is Not Seen". He was elected to the National Assembly in 1848. He died in Rome of tuberculosis at 49.
Joseph Schumpeter|Austrian-American economist (1883–1950).|Schumpeter was born in Moravia; his father, a factory owner, died when he was four, and his mother married a general, giving him an aristocratic education in Vienna. He became Austria's finance minister at 36 and then president of a bank that collapsed, leaving him in debt. His wife and newborn son died in 1926. He taught at Bonn and then Harvard. "Capitalism, Socialism and Democracy" (1942) described "creative destruction", in which innovation sweeps away old industries. He once said he wanted to be the world's greatest economist, lover and horseman.
Montesquieu|French political philosopher (1689–1755) of The Spirit of the Laws.|Charles-Louis de Secondat, Baron de Montesquieu, was born near Bordeaux. At his baptism, a beggar was chosen as his godfather, so he would remember the poor. He inherited a seat in the Bordeaux parliament from his uncle. His "Persian Letters" (1721) satirised French society through the eyes of Persian visitors. After twenty years of study and travel, he published "The Spirit of the Laws" (1748), arguing that liberty is best protected by separating legislative, executive and judicial power. It strongly influenced the American Constitution. He was nearly blind in later years.
Euclid|Greek mathematician of Alexandria (c. 300 BC) whose Elements was the textbook of geometry for two thousand years.|Almost nothing is known about Euclid's life. He taught mathematics in Alexandria, Egypt, around 300 BC, during the reign of Ptolemy I. A story says that when the king asked for a shortcut to geometry, Euclid replied, "There is no royal road to geometry." His "Elements", thirteen books building geometry and number theory from a few axioms, is probably the most influential textbook ever written, used for over two thousand years. Abraham Lincoln studied it to learn how to prove things.
Brahmagupta|Indian mathematician and astronomer (598–c. 668).|Brahmagupta was born in 598 in Rajasthan, India, and became head of the astronomical observatory at Ujjain. In 628, at about thirty, he wrote the "Brāhmasphuṭasiddhānta", a work in verse on astronomy and mathematics. It is the first known text to treat zero as a number with its own rules and to set out rules for negative numbers, called debts. He also gave formulas for the area of a cyclic quadrilateral and methods for solving equations. His work was translated into Arabic in Baghdad and helped carry Indian numerals to the Islamic world and Europe.
Georg Cantor|German mathematician (1845–1918), creator of set theory.|Cantor was born in St Petersburg to a Danish merchant father and a musical mother, and moved to Germany at eleven. He wanted to be an engineer, but his father allowed him to study mathematics. Working at the small University of Halle, he created set theory and proved that some infinities are larger than others. Leading mathematicians, especially his former teacher Kronecker, attacked his ideas. He suffered repeated bouts of depression and spent his last years in a sanatorium. David Hilbert said, "No one shall expel us from the paradise that Cantor has created."
Kurt Gödel|Austrian-American logician (1906–1978).|Gödel was born in Brno, then in Austria-Hungary; as a child his family called him "Mr Why". At 25, in Vienna, he proved his incompleteness theorems, showing that any consistent mathematical system rich enough for arithmetic contains true statements it cannot prove. He fled the Nazis in 1940, crossing Siberia and the Pacific to reach Princeton, where he became Einstein's close friend; they walked home together daily. At his US citizenship hearing he tried to explain a flaw he had found in the Constitution. Fearing poisoning, he ate only food his wife prepared, and starved after she was hospitalised.
Bertrand Russell|British philosopher and logician (1872–1970). Nobel Prize 1950.|Russell was born into an aristocratic family; his grandfather had twice been prime minister. Orphaned at four, he was raised by his strict grandmother. With Alfred North Whitehead he wrote "Principia Mathematica", trying to ground mathematics in logic, and he discovered Russell's paradox. His opposition to the First World War cost him his Cambridge post and six months in prison. "A History of Western Philosophy" was a bestseller, and he won the Nobel Prize in Literature in 1950. At 89 he was jailed again for a protest against nuclear weapons. He married four times.
Alan Turing|English mathematician (1912–1954), a founder of computer science.|Turing was born in London while his father served in India. At school he was passionate about science. At 24 he described a theoretical "universal machine", the basis of every modern computer, and proved some problems cannot be computed. During the Second World War, at Bletchley Park, he led work to break the German naval Enigma code, shortening the war. He designed early computers and proposed the "Turing test" for machine intelligence. In 1952 he was prosecuted for homosexuality and forced to undergo chemical castration. He died of cyanide poisoning in 1954. Britain apologised in 2009.
Thomas Bayes|English minister and mathematician (c. 1701–1761).|Bayes was the son of a Nonconformist minister in London and became a Presbyterian minister himself at Tunbridge Wells. He studied logic and theology at Edinburgh. He published only two works in his lifetime, one defending Newton's calculus. He was elected to the Royal Society. After his death, his friend Richard Price found among his papers an essay on reasoning about chances and published it in 1763. It contained the theorem now bearing Bayes's name, describing how to update beliefs in light of evidence, now essential to science, medicine and artificial intelligence.
Claude Shannon|American mathematician and engineer (1916–2001), founder of information theory.|Shannon grew up in Gaylord, Michigan, building model planes and a telegraph to a friend's house using barbed wire. His master's thesis at MIT showed that electrical switching circuits could carry out logic, the foundation of digital computers. At Bell Labs, he wrote "A Mathematical Theory of Communication" (1948), founding information theory and introducing the "bit". He also worked on cryptography during the war. He was famously playful, juggling while riding a unicycle through Bell Labs' halls and building a mechanical mouse that could learn a maze.
Leonhard Euler|Swiss mathematician (1707–1783).|Euler was born in Basel, Switzerland, the son of a pastor, and was taught by Johann Bernoulli. At 20 he went to St Petersburg's Academy of Sciences, then to Berlin under Frederick the Great, and back to Russia. He lost the sight of one eye in his thirties and became almost totally blind in later life, yet his output increased: he dictated work to his sons and assistants, holding vast calculations in memory. He wrote over 800 papers and books on every branch of mathematics and physics. He had thirteen children and worked with them on his lap.
Jacob Bernoulli|Swiss mathematician (1655–1705).|Jacob Bernoulli was born in Basel into a family of merchants, which would produce eight famous mathematicians. His father wanted him to be a minister; he studied theology but secretly taught himself mathematics. He became professor at Basel and, with his brother Johann, mastered and developed Leibniz's new calculus, though the brothers became bitter rivals. He discovered the number e in studying compound interest. His "Art of Conjecturing", published after his death, proved the law of large numbers. He asked for a logarithmic spiral on his gravestone with the words "I shall arise the same, though changed."
The Buddha|Siddhartha Gautama (c. 5th century BC), the Indian teacher whose awakening founded Buddhism.|Siddhartha Gautama was born in Lumbini, in today's Nepal, to the ruling Shakya clan. Shielded in luxury, he married and had a son. At 29, seeing an old man, a sick man, a corpse and a wandering ascetic, he left home to seek an end to suffering. After six years of austerity he chose a middle way, and at about 35, meditating under a fig tree at Bodh Gaya, he awakened. He gave his first teaching at Sarnath on the Four Noble Truths and spent 45 years walking the Ganges plain, teaching people of every caste. He died at about 80 at Kushinagar, urging his followers to strive on with diligence.
Shunryu Suzuki|Japanese Zen teacher (1904–1971) of Zen Mind, Beginner's Mind.|Suzuki was born in Japan, the son of a Soto Zen priest, and trained from childhood. He served as a temple priest in Japan, through the Second World War. In 1959, aged 54, he came to San Francisco to serve a Japanese-American congregation and found Americans eager to meditate with him. He founded the San Francisco Zen Center and Tassajara Zen Mountain Center, the first Zen monastery outside Asia. "Zen Mind, Beginner's Mind" (1970), edited from his talks, begins: "In the beginner's mind there are many possibilities, but in the expert's there are few." He died of cancer in 1971.
Edmund Burke|Irish statesman and philosopher (1729–1797).|Burke was born in Dublin to a Protestant father and Catholic mother. He studied at Trinity College and moved to London to study law, but turned to writing; his "Enquiry into the Sublime and the Beautiful" influenced aesthetics across Europe. As an MP for nearly thirty years, he supported the American colonists, Catholic relief and Irish trade, and led the long impeachment of Warren Hastings for abuses in India. His "Reflections on the Revolution in France" (1790) warned against destroying institutions built over generations, making him the founding thinker of modern conservatism.
Roland Barthes|French literary theorist (1915–1980).|Barthes was born in Cherbourg; his father, a naval officer, was killed in the First World War when Roland was a baby, and he was raised by his mother, to whom he was devoted. Tuberculosis kept him in sanatoriums for years. "Mythologies" (1957) analysed wrestling, soap powder and wine as myths of modern society. His essay "The Death of the Author" argued that a text's meaning lies with the reader. After his mother's death he wrote "Camera Lucida", a meditation on photography and grief. He died after being hit by a laundry van in Paris.
Walter Benjamin|German critic and philosopher (1892–1940).|Benjamin was born in Berlin to a wealthy, assimilated Jewish family. His doctoral thesis was rejected for a university career. He worked as a critic, translator of Proust and Baudelaire, and radio broadcaster, and was close to Brecht and the Frankfurt School. His essays, including "The Work of Art in the Age of Mechanical Reproduction" and "Theses on the Philosophy of History", are among the most influential of the century. He spent years on the unfinished "Arcades Project" about Paris. Fleeing the Nazis in 1940, he took his own life at the Spanish border when told he would be returned to France.
G. W. F. Hegel|German philosopher (1770–1831) of the Phenomenology of Spirit.|Hegel was born in Stuttgart, the son of a civil servant. At the Tübingen seminary he roomed with Hölderlin and Schelling; they planted a "liberty tree" to celebrate the French Revolution. He worked as a tutor, newspaper editor and headmaster before becoming a professor in Heidelberg and Berlin. He finished "The Phenomenology of Spirit" in Jena as Napoleon's troops entered the city in 1806; he saw Napoleon ride past and called him "the world-soul on horseback". His philosophy of history as the unfolding of freedom through contradiction influenced Marx and much modern thought. He died in a cholera epidemic.
Emmanuel Levinas|Lithuanian-French philosopher (1906–1995) of ethics as first philosophy.|Levinas was born in Kaunas, Lithuania, into a Jewish family and studied in France and Germany with Husserl and Heidegger. A French citizen, he served as an interpreter in the French army and was captured in 1940, spending the war in a prisoner-of-war camp, which protected him; most of his family in Lithuania was murdered. He directed a Jewish teachers' school in Paris for decades. "Totality and Infinity" (1961) argued that ethics, responsibility for the other person revealed in their face, comes before all philosophy. He also wrote Talmudic commentaries.
Gregor Mendel|Austrian monk and scientist (1822–1884), founder of genetics.|Johann Mendel was born to a poor farming family in Silesia and entered the Augustinian abbey of St Thomas in Brno to continue his education, taking the name Gregor. He failed the exam to become a certified teacher twice. In the monastery garden, between 1856 and 1863, he grew and crossed about 28,000 pea plants, discovering that traits are inherited in predictable ratios through "factors" now called genes. His 1866 paper was ignored. He became abbot and spent his last years in a tax dispute with the government. His work was rediscovered in 1900.
James Clerk Maxwell|Scottish physicist (1831–1879).|Maxwell was born in Edinburgh and grew up on his family's estate in Galloway, an endlessly curious child who asked "What's the go o' that?" He published a paper on oval curves at 14. He made the first colour photograph, explained Saturn's rings, and developed the kinetic theory of gases. His equations (1865) united electricity, magnetism and light, predicting radio waves; Einstein kept his picture on his study wall. He became the first Cavendish Professor of Physics at Cambridge. A devout Christian, he died of abdominal cancer at 48, the same age his mother had died of it.
Enrico Fermi|Italian-American physicist (1901–1954). Nobel Prize 1938.|Fermi was born in Rome, the son of a railway official. After his brother's sudden death, the grieving teenager threw himself into physics books. He became a professor at 24 and led a brilliant group of young physicists in Rome, producing new radioactive elements with slow neutrons, for which he won the Nobel Prize in 1938. He used the trip to Stockholm to emigrate with his Jewish wife to the US. In Chicago he built the first nuclear reactor in 1942. He was famous for estimating problems quickly, and asked of aliens, "Where is everybody?"
Charles Horton Cooley|American sociologist (1864–1929).|Cooley was born in Ann Arbor, Michigan, the son of a prominent judge. Shy and often in poor health, he took seven years to finish his engineering degree, travelled, and then turned to sociology, teaching at the University of Michigan for his whole career. In "Human Nature and the Social Order" (1902) he introduced the "looking-glass self": our sense of who we are grows from imagining how others see us. He also described "primary groups", like family and friends, as the nursery of human nature. He observed his own children closely in developing his ideas.
Vilfredo Pareto|Italian economist and sociologist (1848–1923).|Pareto was born in Paris to an Italian father exiled for his republican views. He trained as an engineer and managed railways and ironworks in Italy for twenty years, then became professor of political economy at Lausanne. He noticed that about 80 percent of the land in Italy was owned by 20 percent of the people, and similar patterns elsewhere; this became the "Pareto principle". He developed the idea of "Pareto efficiency" and a theory of elites, arguing that history is a "graveyard of aristocracies". He lived his last years in Switzerland with many cats.
Hesiod|Greek poet (c. 700 BC) of the Theogony and Works and Days.|Hesiod was a Greek poet who, by his own account, worked as a shepherd on Mount Helicon in Boeotia, where the Muses appeared and gave him the gift of song. His father had left the trading city of Cyme in Asia Minor to farm at Ascra, "bad in winter, hard in summer, never good". His "Theogony" tells the origins of the gods, from Chaos to Zeus. His "Works and Days", addressed to his idle brother Perses, who cheated him of his inheritance, gives advice on farming, justice and honest work, and tells of Pandora and the ages of man.
Thomas Bulfinch|American writer (1796–1867) whose The Age of Fable retold the classical myths for general readers.|Bulfinch was born in Newton, Massachusetts, the son of the architect Charles Bulfinch, who designed parts of the US Capitol. After graduating from Harvard he tried several businesses without much success and worked for most of his life as a clerk at the Merchants' Bank in Boston. In his spare time he wrote "The Age of Fable" (1855), retelling Greek, Roman, Norse and other myths for general readers, so that they could understand references in literature and art. With "The Age of Chivalry" and "Legends of Charlemagne", it became "Bulfinch's Mythology".
The Poetic Edda|Old Norse poems of gods and heroes, preserved in a 13th-century Icelandic manuscript.|The Poetic Edda is a collection of Old Norse poems about gods and heroes, preserved mainly in a single manuscript, the Codex Regius, written in Iceland around 1270 and rediscovered in 1643. The poems themselves are older, some perhaps from the Viking Age. They include the "Völuspá", a seeress's vision of the creation and destruction of the world at Ragnarök, the "Hávamál", the wise sayings of Odin, and heroic lays about Sigurd the dragon-slayer. They inspired Wagner, Tolkien and many others.
The Prose Edda|Snorri Sturluson's 13th-century Icelandic handbook of Norse myth.|The Prose Edda was written around 1220 by Snorri Sturluson, an Icelandic chieftain, lawspeaker, historian and poet. He wrote it as a handbook for young poets, to explain the myths behind the kennings, or riddling phrases, of traditional poetry. In the form of a conversation between a Swedish king and three mysterious figures, it tells the Norse myths from creation to Ragnarök: Odin's sacrifice, Thor's adventures, Loki's tricks and Baldur's death. Snorri was murdered in 1241 by men of the Norwegian king.
The Epic of Gilgamesh|Ancient Mesopotamian epic (c. 2100–1200 BC) of the king who sought immortality, among the oldest surviving works of literature.|The Epic of Gilgamesh is the oldest great work of literature to survive, from ancient Mesopotamia (modern Iraq). Poems about Gilgamesh, a king of Uruk, were written in Sumerian around 2100 BC; the fullest version, on twelve clay tablets in Akkadian, was found in the ruins of the library of Ashurbanipal at Nineveh and deciphered in the 1870s. It tells of Gilgamesh's friendship with the wild man Enkidu, Enkidu's death, and Gilgamesh's search for immortality, which ends in acceptance. It includes a flood story resembling Noah's.
The Egyptian Book of the Dead|Ancient Egyptian funerary texts of spells and prayers to guide the dead.|The Book of the Dead is the modern name for a collection of spells, called by Egyptians "the spells for coming forth by day", written on papyrus and placed in tombs from about 1550 BC. It grew from earlier Pyramid Texts and Coffin Texts. The spells guided the dead through the underworld. In the famous judgement scene, the heart of the deceased is weighed against the feather of Ma'at, truth, and the dead declare their innocence of 42 wrongs. Copies were often personalised and beautifully illustrated.
The Kalevala|The Finnish national epic, compiled by Elias Lönnrot from folk songs in 1835–1849.|The Kalevala is the national epic of Finland, compiled by the doctor and folklorist Elias Lönnrot, who travelled for years through Finnish and Karelian villages collecting songs from rune singers. He wove them into one epic, first published in 1835 and expanded in 1849. It tells of the creation of the world from a bird's egg, the old singer-sage Väinämöinen, the smith Ilmarinen who forges the magic Sampo, and the tragic Kullervo. It inspired the Finnish independence movement, Sibelius's music and Tolkien's languages.
The Mabinogion|Medieval Welsh tales of myth and legend.|The Mabinogion is a collection of eleven medieval Welsh tales, preserved in two 14th-century manuscripts, the White Book of Rhydderch and the Red Book of Hergest, though the stories are older. They include the "Four Branches of the Mabinogi", full of magic, shapeshifting and enchanted animals, such as the story of Blodeuwedd, a woman made of flowers, and early tales of King Arthur. Lady Charlotte Guest translated them into English in the 1840s, giving them their name.
The Kojiki|Japan's oldest chronicle (712), recording its myths of creation and the gods.|The Kojiki, "Record of Ancient Matters", is the oldest surviving book in Japan, completed in 712 AD at the order of Empress Genmei. It was dictated from memory by the court reciter Hieda no Are and written down by the scholar Ō no Yasumaro. It tells of the creation of Japan by the gods Izanagi and Izanami, the sun goddess Amaterasu hiding in a cave, the storm god Susanoo slaying an eight-headed serpent, and the descent of the imperial line. It is a central text of Shinto.
Ecclesiastes & Job|Two wisdom books of the Hebrew Bible that face the hardest questions: the vanity of all things under the sun, and why the good suffer.
Presocratics & Epicurus|The first Greek philosophers, from Thales to Democritus, who sought the nature of everything without appeal to myth, together with Epicurus and his heirs.
Greek tragedians|Aeschylus, Sophocles and Euripides, the three great dramatists of 5th-century Athens, whose tragedies still shape how we think of fate and suffering.
The Brontë sisters|Charlotte (1816–1855), Emily (1818–1848) and Anne (1820–1849), who grew up on the Yorkshire moors and wrote Jane Eyre, Wuthering Heights and The Tenant of Wildfell Hall.
Hafiz & the Sufi poets|The mystical poets of Islam, from Persia to India, who wrote of longing for the divine Beloved in the language of love and wine.
Haiku (Bashō, Issa, Buson)|The short Japanese poem of seventeen syllables, perfected by Bashō, Buson and Issa, which catches a single moment of nature and feeling.
Early Buddhist texts|The oldest teachings of the Buddha and his first disciples, preserved in the Pali Canon: discourses and verses by monks and nuns.
Psalms, Proverbs & Wisdom|The songs, sayings and wisdom books of the Hebrew Bible and its neighbours, from the Psalms and Proverbs to Sirach and the Book of Wisdom.
Christian mystics|Men and women who sought direct union with God, from Meister Eckhart and Julian of Norwich to Teresa of Ávila and John of the Cross.
The French aphorists|La Rochefoucauld, La Bruyère, Chamfort, Joubert, Vauvenargues and others, masters of the short, sharp maxim about human nature.
Donne & the metaphysical poets|John Donne, George Herbert, Andrew Marvell, Henry Vaughan and others in 17th-century England, who wrote with daring wit about love, death and God.
Tennyson, Arnold & the Victorians|The poets of Victorian England, among them Tennyson, Arnold, Browning, Christina Rossetti, Hardy and Hopkins, writing through an age of faith and doubt.
Shelley, Coleridge & Byron|The English Romantic poets of imagination, nature and revolt.
Rimbaud, Verlaine & the Symbolists|The French poets of the late 19th century who sought music, suggestion and the mystery behind things.
Li Po, Tu Fu & the Tang poets|The great poets of China's Tang dynasty (618–907), the golden age of Chinese poetry.
Pushkin & Lermontov|The founders of modern Russian poetry, both killed in duels young.
Desiderata (Max Ehrmann)|A prose poem written in 1927 by the American poet Max Ehrmann (1872–1945): "Go placidly amid the noise and haste..."
Ryōkan & Ikkyū|Two free-spirited Japanese Zen monks and poets: Ikkyū (1394–1481) and Ryōkan (1758–1831).
Kafka's love letters|Franz Kafka's letters to Felice Bauer and Milena Jesenská, intense, anxious and tender.
Van Gogh's later letters|Vincent van Gogh's letters from Arles, Saint-Rémy and Auvers (1888–1890), written in his last, most productive years.
Abelard & Héloïse|The 12th-century philosopher Peter Abelard and his student Héloïse, whose love affair ended in tragedy and whose letters became legendary.
Isaiah|A book of the Hebrew Bible, the prophecies of Isaiah of Jerusalem and his successors.|The Book of Isaiah is one of the major prophetic books of the Hebrew Bible. The prophet Isaiah son of Amoz preached in Jerusalem in the 8th century BC, warning kings and people of judgement for injustice. Scholars believe later chapters were written by prophets during and after the Babylonian exile, offering comfort to exiles: "Comfort ye my people". It contains visions of peace where swords become ploughshares and the wolf lies with the lamb. Christians read its "suffering servant" passages as foretelling Jesus. Handel's "Messiah" draws heavily on it.
Lamentations|A book of the Hebrew Bible: poems of grief over the fall of Jerusalem.|The Book of Lamentations is a collection of five poems in the Hebrew Bible mourning the destruction of Jerusalem and its Temple by the Babylonians in 586 BC. Tradition attributes it to the prophet Jeremiah. Four of the poems are acrostics, each verse beginning with the next letter of the Hebrew alphabet, as if to express grief from A to Z. In the middle comes hope: "His compassions fail not. They are new every morning." It is read in synagogues on Tisha B'Av, the day of mourning for the Temple.
Matthew|The Gospel of Matthew, one of the four accounts of the life and teaching of Jesus.|The Gospel of Matthew is the first book of the New Testament, written around 80–90 AD and traditionally attributed to the apostle Matthew, a former tax collector. Written for a largely Jewish-Christian community, it presents Jesus as the fulfilment of Hebrew prophecy. It contains the Sermon on the Mount, with the Beatitudes ("Blessed are the meek") and the Lord's Prayer, and many parables, such as the sheep and the goats, in which serving the hungry and the stranger is serving Christ.
James|The Letter of James in the New Testament, on faith shown in deeds.|The Letter of James is a short book of the New Testament, traditionally attributed to James, the brother of Jesus and leader of the church in Jerusalem, who was martyred around 62 AD. It is practical rather than doctrinal, more like the Hebrew wisdom books than Paul's letters. It insists that "faith without works is dead", warns about the power of the tongue, and condemns favouritism toward the rich. Martin Luther called it "an epistle of straw", but it remains loved for its down-to-earth wisdom.
1 Corinthians|Paul's first letter to the Corinthians, containing the great hymn to love.|The First Letter to the Corinthians was written by the apostle Paul around 53–55 AD from Ephesus to the young church he had founded in Corinth, a bustling Greek port city. The community was divided by quarrels, rivalries and confusion, and Paul addresses these practical problems. In chapter 13 he writes the famous hymn to love: "Love suffereth long, and is kind ... Love never faileth", ending "now abideth faith, hope, charity, these three; but the greatest of these is charity." It is read at countless weddings.
Hebrews|The Letter to the Hebrews in the New Testament.|The Letter to the Hebrews is a book of the New Testament written in the 1st century to Christians, probably of Jewish background, who were facing persecution and tempted to give up. Its author is unknown; tradition once credited Paul, but its polished Greek style differs from his. It presents Christ as the great high priest. Its eleventh chapter, the "hall of faith", recalls heroes who lived by faith, and opens with "Now faith is the substance of things hoped for, the evidence of things not seen."
Micah|A book of the Hebrew Bible: "do justly, love mercy, and walk humbly with thy God."|The Book of Micah is one of the twelve minor prophets of the Hebrew Bible. Micah came from the village of Moresheth in Judah and preached in the 8th century BC, a contemporary of Isaiah. He spoke for the rural poor against corrupt rulers, judges and priests who seized fields and houses. His book contains the vision of swords beaten into ploughshares, and the famous summary of religion: "what doth the Lord require of thee, but to do justly, and to love mercy, and to walk humbly with thy God?"
Japanese poem|Classical Japanese poems (waka) from the imperial anthologies.
Kokinshū|The first imperial anthology of Japanese waka poetry (c. 905).|The Kokinshū, "Collection of Poems Ancient and Modern", was the first imperial anthology of Japanese waka poetry, ordered by Emperor Daigo and completed around 905. Its four compilers, led by Ki no Tsurayuki, arranged about 1,100 poems by season and by themes such as love and parting. Tsurayuki's preface, which begins "Japanese poetry has its seeds in the human heart", is the first great essay on Japanese poetics. It set the standard for court poetry for a thousand years.
Leon Festinger|American social psychologist (1919–1989).|Festinger was born in New York to Russian Jewish immigrants. He studied with the social psychologist Kurt Lewin. In 1954 he and colleagues joined a small group that believed a flood would end the world, to see what happened when the prophecy failed; instead of giving up, many members believed more strongly. "When Prophecy Fails" and "A Theory of Cognitive Dissonance" (1957) explained how we change our beliefs to reduce the discomfort of contradiction. In later life he turned to archaeology and the history of technology.
Martin Seligman|American psychologist (born 1942), founder of positive psychology.|Seligman was born in Albany, New York. As a young researcher in the 1960s he discovered "learned helplessness": animals that could not escape shocks stopped trying, even when escape became possible. This became a model for depression. Later he studied how people can learn optimism. As president of the American Psychological Association in 1998, he launched "positive psychology", the study of what makes life worth living. His books include "Learned Optimism" and "Authentic Happiness". He directs the Positive Psychology Center at the University of Pennsylvania.
Thomas Gilovich|American psychologist (born 1954).|Gilovich is a professor of psychology at Cornell University, where he has studied how people evaluate evidence and make judgements. He is known for research showing that the "hot hand" in basketball, the belief that a player who has made several shots is more likely to make the next, is largely an illusion (a conclusion later partly revised), and for studies of the "spotlight effect", showing that others notice us far less than we think. His book "How We Know What Isn't So" (1991) is a classic on the errors of everyday reasoning.
Karl Marx and Friedrich Engels|German philosophers and revolutionaries, Marx (1818–1883) and Engels (1820–1895), authors of The Communist Manifesto.|Karl Marx was born in Trier, Germany, studied philosophy and became a radical journalist. Friedrich Engels was the son of a textile manufacturer who sent him to work in the family's mill in Manchester, where he wrote "The Condition of the Working Class in England". They met in 1844 and began a lifelong partnership. Together they wrote "The Communist Manifesto" (1848). Exiled to London, Marx lived in poverty, losing three children, while writing "Capital" in the British Museum Reading Room; Engels supported him financially and edited his work after his death.
Nikolai Lobachevsky|Russian mathematician (1792–1856), a founder of non-Euclidean geometry.|Lobachevsky was born in Nizhny Novgorod to a poor family; his father died when he was young, and his mother moved to Kazan, where he studied and spent his life. He became rector of Kazan University for nineteen years, rebuilding it after a fire and caring for students during a cholera epidemic. In 1829 he published a geometry in which, through a point, many lines can be parallel to a given line. Ridiculed in Russia, he persisted. Going blind, he dictated his final work, "Pangeometry". Gauss secretly admired his work.
Alfred Korzybski|Polish-American scholar (1879–1950).|Korzybski was born in Warsaw to a Polish noble family and trained as an engineer. He served in the Russian army in the First World War, was wounded, and came to North America on a military mission. Shaken by the war, he asked why humans could make such progress in science and so little in human affairs. In "Science and Sanity" (1933) he founded "general semantics", arguing that we confuse words with things, and coined the phrase "the map is not the territory". He founded an institute to teach his ideas.
Viktor Shklovsky|Russian literary theorist and writer (1893–1984).|Shklovsky was born in St Petersburg and studied at the university there. In 1916 he co-founded OPOJAZ, the Society for the Study of Poetic Language, part of Russian Formalism. His essay "Art as Device" (1917) introduced "ostranenie", making strange: art exists to make us see familiar things freshly, "to make the stone stony". He fought in the First World War and took part in the Revolution and Civil War, then fled to Finland and Berlin for a time. Back in the USSR, he survived by writing screenplays and memoirs, and lived to 91.
Charles Goodhart|British economist (born 1936).|Goodhart is a British economist who worked for many years at the Bank of England and then at the London School of Economics. In a 1975 paper on monetary policy he observed that any statistical regularity tends to break down once it is used for control. It was later popularised as "When a measure becomes a target, it ceases to be a good measure." Goodhart's law now applies to everything from school league tables to hospital waiting times. He was a member of the Bank of England's Monetary Policy Committee.
C. Northcote Parkinson|British naval historian (1909–1993).|Cyril Northcote Parkinson was born in Barnard Castle, England, studied history at Cambridge and served in the army in the Second World War. He became professor of history at the University of Malaya in Singapore. In 1955 he published a satirical essay in The Economist observing that "work expands so as to fill the time available for its completion", and that bureaucracies grow regardless of work to be done. The book "Parkinson's Law" (1958) was a bestseller. He also coined the "law of triviality": committees spend the most time on the least important things.
Robin Dunbar|British anthropologist (born 1947).|Dunbar is a British anthropologist and evolutionary psychologist who studied primate behaviour, including gelada baboons in Ethiopia, and taught at Liverpool, University College London and Oxford. Comparing primates' brain sizes with their group sizes, he predicted in the early 1990s that humans can maintain about 150 stable relationships, "Dunbar's number", supported by the size of villages, army companies and Christmas-card lists. He also argued that gossip evolved as a form of social grooming, in "Grooming, Gossip and the Evolution of Language".
Niccolò Machiavelli|Florentine diplomat and political thinker (1469–1527) whose The Prince described power as it is practised, not as it should be.|Machiavelli was born in Florence and served for fourteen years as a senior official and diplomat of the Florentine Republic, meeting kings, popes and Cesare Borgia. When the Medici returned to power in 1512, he was dismissed, imprisoned and tortured. Retired to his farm, he wrote "The Prince" (1513), a frank guide to gaining and keeping power, describing how rulers actually behave rather than how they should. He also wrote "Discourses on Livy", praising republics, a history of Florence and the comedy "The Mandrake". In letters he described changing into courtly clothes at night to converse with the ancients in his books.
Philippa Foot|British moral philosopher (1920–2010) who revived virtue ethics and posed the trolley problem.|Philippa Bosanquet was born in England, a granddaughter of US President Grover Cleveland, and was educated at home by governesses. At Oxford in the war years she formed a group of women philosophers with Elizabeth Anscombe, Iris Murdoch and Mary Midgley. Shocked by news of the concentration camps, she sought to show that morality is objective. She revived virtue ethics, comparing moral goodness to the health of living things in "Natural Goodness". In 1967 she introduced the "trolley problem", now discussed far beyond philosophy. She taught at Oxford and UCLA.
Robert Nozick|American philosopher (1938–2002) of Anarchy, State, and Utopia.|Nozick was born in Brooklyn to a Russian Jewish family and taught at Harvard for most of his career. "Anarchy, State, and Utopia" (1974), written in response to his colleague John Rawls, defended a minimal state limited to protecting rights, and became a classic of libertarian thought. It introduced the "experience machine" thought experiment. Unusually, he never wrote a sequel; he moved on to other questions about knowledge, meaning and life, as in "Philosophical Explanations" and "The Examined Life", and later softened his libertarian views.
John Searle|American philosopher of mind and language (1932–2025).|Searle was born in Denver and studied at the University of Wisconsin and, as a Rhodes Scholar, at Oxford with J. L. Austin. He taught at the University of California, Berkeley, for over sixty years. He developed the theory of "speech acts", how we do things with words. In 1980 he proposed the "Chinese room" thought experiment, arguing that a computer following rules to manipulate symbols could appear to understand Chinese without understanding anything. He also wrote on consciousness and on how social reality, like money, is created by collective agreement.
Frank Jackson|Australian philosopher (born 1943) of mind.|Frank Jackson is an Australian philosopher, born in Adelaide, the son of two philosophers. He taught at Monash University and the Australian National University. In 1982 he published "Epiphenomenal Qualia", which introduced the thought experiment of Mary, a scientist who knows every physical fact about colour but has lived in a black-and-white room. When she first sees red, does she learn something new? Jackson originally argued yes, against physicalism, but later changed his mind. The "knowledge argument" remains central in debates about consciousness.
Max Weber|German sociologist (1864–1920) of The Protestant Ethic and the Spirit of Capitalism.|Weber was born in Erfurt into a prosperous family; his father was a politician and his mother a devout Protestant. He studied law and economics and became a professor at Freiburg and Heidelberg. After a violent quarrel with his father, who died soon after, he suffered a severe breakdown and could not teach for years. "The Protestant Ethic and the Spirit of Capitalism" (1905) linked religious ideas to economic life. He wrote on bureaucracy, authority, charisma and the "disenchantment of the world". He died in the 1920 influenza pandemic at 56.
Émile Durkheim|French sociologist (1858–1917), a founder of sociology.|Durkheim was born in Épinal, Lorraine, into a line of rabbis, and was expected to become one himself, but chose secular study. He made sociology an academic discipline in France, founding its first journal. His book "Suicide" (1897) showed that suicide rates vary with social factors like integration and regulation, introducing "anomie". "The Elementary Forms of Religious Life" studied Australian Aboriginal religion and the "collective effervescence" of rituals. The death of his only son in the First World War broke him, and he died two years later.
Robert K. Merton|American sociologist (1910–2003).|Meyer Robert Schkolnick was born in Philadelphia to Russian Jewish immigrants and grew up near a Carnegie library, which he called his real school. As a teenage amateur magician he took the stage name Robert Merton, and kept it. He studied at Harvard and taught at Columbia for decades. He coined or popularised the terms "self-fulfilling prophecy", "unintended consequences", "role model", "focus group" and the "Matthew effect", and founded the sociology of science. His son Robert C. Merton won the Nobel Prize in Economics.
Arthur Pigou|British economist (1877–1959).|Pigou was born on the Isle of Wight, the son of an army officer, and studied at Cambridge, where he succeeded Alfred Marshall as professor of political economy at 30, a post he held for 35 years. "The Economics of Welfare" (1920) introduced the idea of externalities, costs or benefits that spill onto others, and proposed taxes to correct harmful ones, such as pollution, now called "Pigouvian taxes". A keen mountaineer, he was a conscientious objector in the First World War who drove an ambulance on the front.
Thomas Gresham|English merchant and financier (c. 1519–1579).|Gresham was born in London into a family of merchants and was apprenticed as a mercer. He became royal agent in Antwerp, managing the English crown's debts and arranging loans for Edward VI, Mary and Elizabeth I, and was knighted. He founded the Royal Exchange in London, modelled on the Antwerp bourse. In his will he founded Gresham College, which still offers free public lectures. "Gresham's law", that bad money drives out good, was named after him in the 19th century, though the observation is much older.
Dmitri Mendeleev|Russian chemist (1834–1907) who created the periodic table.|Mendeleev was born in Siberia, the youngest of many children. His father went blind, and his mother ran a glass factory, which burned down. She then took him thousands of kilometres to St Petersburg to find him an education, and died soon after. He became a chemistry professor. In 1869, writing a textbook and arranging cards of the elements, he devised the periodic table, leaving gaps and predicting the properties of elements yet to be discovered, such as gallium and germanium, which were found within twenty years. Element 101, mendelevium, is named after him.
Alexander Fleming|Scottish physician and microbiologist (1881–1955) who discovered penicillin. Nobel Prize 1945.|Fleming was born on a farm in Ayrshire, Scotland, and moved to London to work in a shipping office before an inheritance let him study medicine. He trained in bacteriology and served in the army medical corps in the First World War, seeing soldiers die of infected wounds. In 1928, returning from holiday to his untidy laboratory, he noticed a mould killing bacteria in a Petri dish: penicillin. Howard Florey and Ernst Chain later developed it into a drug. They shared the Nobel Prize in 1945. Fleming warned early that misuse could create resistant bacteria.
Edward Jenner|English physician (1749–1823), pioneer of vaccination.|Jenner was born in Berkeley, Gloucestershire, the son of a vicar, orphaned at five. He was apprenticed to a surgeon and studied in London with John Hunter, who told him "Don't think, try." As a country doctor, he heard that milkmaids who caught cowpox seemed protected from smallpox. In 1796 he inoculated eight-year-old James Phipps with cowpox, then with smallpox, and the boy did not fall ill. He called it vaccination, from the Latin for cow. He worked to spread it for free. Smallpox was declared eradicated in 1980. He also studied cuckoos.
Santiago Ramón y Cajal|Spanish neuroscientist and artist (1852–1934). Nobel Prize 1906.|Cajal was born in a small village in Navarre, Spain, a rebellious boy who wanted to be an artist and was jailed at eleven for blowing up a neighbour's gate with a homemade cannon. His father, an anatomy teacher, made him study medicine. He served as an army doctor in Cuba and caught malaria. Using a staining method of Camillo Golgi, he spent decades drawing nerve cells, showing that the nervous system is made of separate cells, neurons. He shared the Nobel Prize with Golgi in 1906. His drawings are still admired as art.
John Nash|American mathematician (1928–2015) of game theory. Nobel Prize in Economics 1994.|Nash was born in Bluefield, West Virginia, the son of an engineer. His recommendation letter to Princeton graduate school was one line: "This man is a genius." At 21 his 28-page thesis introduced the Nash equilibrium. He made deep contributions to geometry. In his early thirties he developed schizophrenia, was hospitalised several times and spent years wandering Princeton's halls. He gradually recovered and won the Nobel Prize in Economics in 1994. His life was told in the book and film "A Beautiful Mind". He and his wife Alicia died in a taxi crash in 2015.
Benoît Mandelbrot|Polish-born French-American mathematician (1924–2010), father of fractal geometry.|Mandelbrot was born in Warsaw to a Lithuanian Jewish family that moved to France in 1936. During the Nazi occupation he survived in hiding in central France, working as an apprentice toolmaker. He studied at the École Polytechnique and worked for decades at IBM in New York. He noticed the same irregular patterns, repeating at every scale, in cotton prices, coastlines and noise. He coined the word "fractal" in 1975, and the Mandelbrot set, revealed by computer graphics, became an icon. "The Fractal Geometry of Nature" (1982) changed how we see roughness.
Leonardo of Pisa (Fibonacci)|Italian mathematician (c. 1170–c. 1250) who brought Hindu-Arabic numerals to Europe in his Liber Abaci.|Leonardo of Pisa, later nicknamed Fibonacci ("son of Bonacci"), grew up in North Africa, in Béjaïa in modern Algeria, where his father was a Pisan customs official. There he learned the Hindu-Arabic numerals from Muslim teachers, and travelled around the Mediterranean studying mathematics. His "Liber Abaci" (1202) introduced these numerals, including zero, to Europe and showed their use in bookkeeping, conversion of currency and interest. A puzzle in it about breeding rabbits gives the Fibonacci sequence. Pisa granted him a salary for his service to the city.
Zeno of Elea|Greek philosopher (c. 490–430 BC), pupil of Parmenides, famous for his paradoxes of motion.|Zeno came from Elea in southern Italy and was the student and friend of Parmenides. Plato describes him visiting Athens with Parmenides when Socrates was young. He wrote a book of about forty paradoxes defending his teacher's claim that motion and plurality are illusions; the most famous are Achilles and the tortoise and the arrow that never moves. Aristotle called him the inventor of dialectic. Ancient sources say he plotted against a tyrant and, under torture, bit off his own tongue rather than betray his friends.
Friedrich Schiller|German poet, playwright and philosopher (1759–1805), author of the "Ode to Joy" and On the Aesthetic Education of Man.|Schiller was born in Marbach, Württemberg, and forced by the Duke to attend a military academy, where he studied medicine. His first play, "The Robbers" (1781), about a noble outlaw, caused a sensation; he deserted his post as a regimental doctor to see it performed and fled the duchy. He wrote history, philosophy and plays such as "Don Carlos", "Wallenstein", "Mary Stuart" and "William Tell". In Weimar he formed a great friendship with Goethe. His "Ode to Joy" was set by Beethoven in the Ninth Symphony. He died of tuberculosis at 45.
Nāgārjuna|Indian Buddhist philosopher (c. 150–250), founder of the Madhyamaka school.|Nāgārjuna was an Indian Buddhist philosopher, probably from the south of India, active around the 2nd century AD. Legends say he retrieved the Perfection of Wisdom scriptures from the realm of the serpent-beings (nāgas). His "Fundamental Verses on the Middle Way" argues that all things are "empty" of independent existence, arising only in dependence on other things, and that emptiness itself is empty. He founded the Madhyamaka school, deeply influencing Tibetan, Chinese and Japanese Buddhism. He is honoured as a "second Buddha".
Isaac Newton and Gottfried Leibniz|Newton (1643–1727) in England and Leibniz (1646–1716) in Germany, who independently invented calculus.|Newton developed his "method of fluxions" in 1665–66 but did not publish it for decades. The German polymath Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz, a diplomat, librarian and philosopher, developed calculus independently in the 1670s and published first, in 1684, with the elegant notation still used today, such as dx and the integral sign. A bitter priority dispute followed, with Newton secretly writing the Royal Society report that accused Leibniz of plagiarism. Historians now credit both. The quarrel split British and continental mathematics for a century.
Peter Dirichlet|German mathematician (1805–1859).|Johann Peter Gustav Lejeune Dirichlet was born in Düren, then under French rule, to a family originally from Belgium. He studied in Paris and taught at Breslau, Berlin and Göttingen, where he succeeded Gauss. He proved that there are infinitely many primes in many arithmetic sequences, founded analytic number theory and gave the modern definition of a function. The "pigeonhole principle" is often called "Dirichlet's box principle". He married Rebecka Mendelssohn, sister of the composer Felix Mendelssohn.
Daniel Kahneman|Israeli-American psychologist (1934–2024), founder of behavioural economics. Nobel Prize in Economics 2002.|Kahneman was born in Tel Aviv and grew up in Paris, where his family hid from the Nazis; his father died just before liberation. He moved to Palestine in 1948, served in the Israeli army as a psychologist and designed its interview system. With his close friend Amos Tversky he studied the systematic errors in human judgement, such as anchoring and loss aversion, and developed prospect theory. Though a psychologist, he won the Nobel Prize in Economics in 2002. "Thinking, Fast and Slow" (2011) explained his work to millions.
Great ideas|Concepts that changed how people think, from Plato's Cave to the uncertainty principle. Each is given in its originator's own words where the original is in the public domain, with a short explanation written for the app.
Mythology|Myths from around the world, told where possible in the words of old translations and retellings, each with a short note on what it means.
Philosophy|Ideas about how to live, what we can know and what is real, from the ancient Greeks to the existentialists.
Science|Discoveries and theories about the universe, life and matter.
Mind & psychology|Ideas about how the mind works: attention, habit, emotion, memory and the biases that steer us.
Society & economics|Ideas about power, money, cooperation and how people live together.
Mathematics & logic|Ideas about number, shape, chance and the limits of proof.
Eastern thought|Ideas from Indian, Chinese, Japanese and other Asian traditions, and from African philosophy.
Art & aesthetics|Ideas about beauty, art, stories and why they move us.
Greek & Roman|The myths of ancient Greece and Rome: gods, heroes and the human stories behind them.
Norse|The myths of the Vikings and Icelanders, from the creation of the world to Ragnarök.
Egypt & Mesopotamia|The oldest written myths, from the Nile and the land between the rivers.
Myths of the world|Creation stories and tales from Finland, Wales, China, Japan, India, Africa, Polynesia and Mexico.
Lady Gregory|Irish playwright and folklorist (1852–1932), co-founder of the Abbey Theatre, who retold the Irish myths in Gods and Fighting Men and Cuchulain of Muirthemne.|Isabella Augusta Persse was born into a Protestant landowning family in County Galway, Ireland. At 28 she married Sir William Gregory, a former governor of Ceylon 35 years older, and after his death she devoted herself to Irish culture. She learned Irish and collected folklore from local people. With W. B. Yeats she founded the Abbey Theatre, Ireland's national theatre, and wrote some forty plays. Her retellings of the Irish myths, "Cuchulain of Muirthemne" and "Gods and Fighting Men", were praised by Yeats. Her home at Coole Park welcomed writers.
Johannes Kepler|German astronomer (1571–1630) who discovered the laws of planetary motion.|Kepler was born in Weil der Stadt in Württemberg; smallpox weakened his eyes and hands as a child. He saw a comet at six and an eclipse at nine. A teacher of mathematics in Graz, he became assistant to the great astronomer Tycho Brahe in Prague, and after Tycho's death inherited his precise observations of Mars. From them, after years of calculation, he discovered that planets move in ellipses, the first of his three laws. He spent years defending his mother against charges of witchcraft and won her release. He also explained how the eye forms images and wrote early science fiction.
Antoine Lavoisier|French chemist (1743–1794), father of modern chemistry, executed in the Revolution.|Lavoisier was born into a wealthy Parisian family and trained as a lawyer, but loved science. He invested in the Ferme générale, a private tax-collecting company, which funded his laboratory. With careful weighing, he showed that mass is conserved in chemical reactions, explained combustion as combining with oxygen, which he named, and created the modern naming of chemicals. His wife, Marie-Anne Paulze, assisted him, translated English papers and drew his apparatus. During the Revolution he was guillotined in 1794 as a tax farmer. The mathematician Lagrange said it took a moment to cut off that head and a century may not produce another like it.
Michael Faraday|English scientist (1791–1867) who discovered electromagnetic induction.|Faraday was born in south London, the son of a blacksmith, with only basic schooling. Apprenticed to a bookbinder, he read the books he bound, especially on electricity. He attended lectures by Humphry Davy, bound his notes and sent them to Davy, who hired him as an assistant. He discovered electromagnetic induction, built the first electric motor and generator and found the laws of electrolysis, despite knowing little mathematics. A devout member of a small Christian sect, he refused a knighthood. He began the Royal Institution's Christmas lectures for children, which continue today.
Elinor Ostrom|American political economist (1933–2012), the first woman to win the Nobel Prize in Economics (2009).|Elinor Awan was born in Los Angeles to a Jewish father and Protestant mother, and grew up poor during the Depression. She was discouraged from studying mathematics as a girl. She overcame a stammer through debate and studied political science at UCLA. With her husband Vincent Ostrom, she taught at Indiana University. Studying fisheries, forests, irrigation systems and pastures around the world, she showed that communities often manage shared resources well without either private ownership or government control. In 2009 she became the first woman to win the Nobel Prize in Economics.
Antonio Gramsci|Italian Marxist thinker (1891–1937) who wrote his Prison Notebooks in Mussolini's jails.|Gramsci was born in Sardinia, the fourth of seven children; a childhood illness left him with a hunched back and he was very short. His father was jailed for embezzlement, and the family lived in poverty. He won a scholarship to the University of Turin, became a journalist and co-founded the Italian Communist Party. Elected to parliament, he was arrested by Mussolini's government in 1926; the prosecutor said "we must stop this brain from functioning for twenty years". In prison he filled 33 notebooks, including his theory of cultural hegemony. He died shortly after his release in 1937.
Marcel Mauss|French anthropologist (1872–1950) of The Gift.|Mauss was born in Épinal, France, the nephew of Émile Durkheim, who guided his education and work. He studied philosophy and the history of religions, and became a central figure in French sociology and anthropology, though he never did fieldwork himself, drawing on the reports of others. "The Gift" (1925) showed that in many societies giving creates obligations to receive and to repay, binding people together. He also studied magic, sacrifice and "techniques of the body". He served in the First World War and lost many students and colleagues in it.
David Hilbert|German mathematician (1862–1943).|Hilbert was born in Königsberg, Prussia, the city of Kant, and spent most of his career at Göttingen, which he made the world centre of mathematics. He worked on invariants, geometry, number theory and the foundations of physics. In 1900, at the International Congress of Mathematicians in Paris, he presented 23 unsolved problems that shaped 20th-century mathematics. He championed Emmy Noether against colleagues who opposed a woman lecturer. His motto, on his gravestone, was "We must know, we will know", from a 1930 speech in Königsberg given, by irony, the day after Gödel first announced his incompleteness theorem in the same city.
Emmy Noether|German mathematician (1882–1935), whose theorem links symmetry and conservation laws.|Noether was born in Erlangen, Bavaria, the daughter of a mathematics professor. Women could only audit university classes when she began; she earned a doctorate in 1907, then worked unpaid for years. David Hilbert invited her to Göttingen, where she lectured under his name until 1919. Her 1918 theorem linking symmetry and conservation laws became fundamental to physics, and she transformed abstract algebra. Dismissed by the Nazis in 1933 as a Jew, she moved to Bryn Mawr College in the US and lectured at Princeton. She died after surgery in 1935, aged 53.
Ernest Hemingway|American novelist (1899–1961) of The Old Man and the Sea. Nobel Prize 1954.|Hemingway was born in Oak Park, Illinois, and learned to hunt and fish in northern Michigan. A reporter at 18, he drove ambulances in Italy in the First World War and was badly wounded. In Paris in the 1920s he learned from Gertrude Stein and Ezra Pound and wrote "The Sun Also Rises". He reported on the Spanish Civil War, wrote "For Whom the Bell Tolls", and lived in Key West and Cuba. "The Old Man and the Sea" won the Pulitzer Prize, and he won the Nobel Prize in 1954. After years of illness and depression, he took his own life at 61.
Robert Zajonc|Polish-American social psychologist (1923–2008).|Zajonc was born in Łódź, Poland. When he was 16, a German bombing raid on Warsaw killed his parents and left him badly injured. He escaped two labour camps and joined the French Resistance. After the war he emigrated to the United States, studied at the University of Michigan and taught there for nearly forty years, later at Stanford. He discovered the "mere exposure effect", that we come to like things simply by seeing them often, and argued that feelings can come before thinking. He also studied "social facilitation".
Edward Thorndike|American psychologist (1874–1949).|Thorndike was born in Massachusetts, the son of a Methodist minister. As a student he kept chickens in his landlord's basement for experiments. Studying cats escaping puzzle boxes, he formulated the "law of effect": behaviours followed by satisfaction tend to be repeated. He taught at Teachers College, Columbia, for decades and was a founder of educational psychology, studying learning, intelligence and the transfer of skills. He also described the "halo effect" in 1920.
Ole Rømer|Danish astronomer (1644–1710) who first measured the speed of light.|Rømer was born in Aarhus, Denmark, the son of a merchant. In Paris at the Royal Observatory, studying the moon Io's eclipses behind Jupiter, he noticed they came earlier or later depending on Earth's distance from Jupiter, and concluded in 1676 that light travels at a finite speed. Back in Copenhagen as royal mathematician, he served as chief of police, introducing street lights, and as mayor. He reformed weights and measures and invented a temperature scale that influenced Fahrenheit. Most of his records were lost in the Copenhagen fire of 1728.
Ludwig Boltzmann|Austrian physicist (1844–1906) who explained entropy through the statistics of atoms.|Boltzmann was born in Vienna, the son of a tax official, and studied with Josef Stefan. As a professor in Graz, Vienna, Munich and Leipzig, he explained the laws of heat through the statistics of countless moving atoms, defining entropy in terms of probability; his formula S = k log W is carved on his tombstone. Many scientists of his time doubted atoms existed, and the attacks on his work weighed on him. He suffered from what was probably bipolar disorder, and took his own life in 1906, shortly before experiments confirmed the existence of atoms.
Barry Schwartz|American psychologist (born 1946) of The Paradox of Choice.|Schwartz is an American psychologist who taught for many years at Swarthmore College. Studying how people make decisions, he argued in "The Paradox of Choice" (2004) that while some choice is good, too many options can cause anxiety, regret and dissatisfaction. He distinguished "maximisers", who seek the best, from "satisficers", who settle for good enough and tend to be happier. His TED talk on the subject was widely watched. He has also written on practical wisdom and on why we work.
Paul Samuelson|American economist (1915–2009). Nobel Prize in Economics 1970.|Samuelson was born in Gary, Indiana. He attended a lecture at the University of Chicago at 8 a.m. on 2 January 1932, aged 16, and said he was "born again" as an economist. At MIT he built a world-class economics department and introduced mathematical rigour to almost every area of economics. His textbook "Economics" (1948) was the best-selling economics book for decades. He was the first American to win the Nobel Prize in Economics, in 1970, and advised Presidents Kennedy and Johnson. He wrote a newspaper column for years.
Frank Benford|American physicist (1883–1948).|Frank Benford was an American electrical engineer and physicist who worked for nearly forty years at the General Electric research laboratory in Schenectady, New York, mainly on optics. In 1938 he noticed, as the astronomer Simon Newcomb had in 1881, that the pages of logarithm tables were more worn at the start. He checked over 20,000 numbers from sources such as river areas, populations and street addresses, and found that numbers beginning with 1 are far more common than those beginning with 9. The pattern is now used to detect fraud.
Baldassare Castiglione|Italian courtier and diplomat (1478–1529) of The Book of the Courtier.|Castiglione was born near Mantua into a noble family and served as a courtier and diplomat at the courts of Milan, Mantua and especially Urbino. His friend Raphael painted his famous portrait, now in the Louvre. "The Book of the Courtier" (1528) presents conversations at the court of Urbino about the ideal courtier and court lady, introducing "sprezzatura", the art of making difficult things look effortless. It was a European bestseller. He served as papal nuncio to Spain and died in Toledo.
T. S. Eliot|American-British poet (1888–1965) of The Waste Land and Four Quartets. Nobel Prize 1948.|Thomas Stearns Eliot was born in St Louis, Missouri, and studied philosophy at Harvard, the Sorbonne and Oxford. He settled in England, worked for eight years at Lloyds Bank, and married Vivienne Haigh-Wood, an unhappy marriage marked by illness. "The Love Song of J. Alfred Prufrock" and "The Waste Land" (1922) changed modern poetry. He became a director at the publisher Faber and Faber, joined the Church of England and took British citizenship in 1927. "Four Quartets" is his great meditation on time and faith. He won the Nobel Prize in 1948.
Willa Cather|American novelist (1873–1947) of My Ántonia and O Pioneers!, who wrote of the prairie, its immigrant families and the pull of memory. Pulitzer Prize 1923.|Cather was born in Virginia and moved at nine to the prairies of Nebraska, among Czech, Scandinavian and German immigrant farmers, whose lives filled her novels. She worked as a journalist and teacher, then as editor of McClure's magazine in New York. "O Pioneers!" and "My Ántonia" celebrate the land and its settlers. "One of Ours" won the Pulitzer Prize, and "Death Comes for the Archbishop" is set in New Mexico. She lived for nearly forty years with her partner Edith Lewis and protected her privacy fiercely.
Lafcadio Hearn|Greek-Irish writer (1850–1904) who settled in Japan, took the name Koizumi Yakumo, and wrote some of the first and most loving accounts of its ghosts, beliefs and quiet beauty.|Patrick Lafcadio Hearn was born on the Greek island of Lefkada to an Irish army surgeon and a Greek mother, and was raised by a great-aunt in Dublin after both parents left. An accident blinded his left eye. He emigrated to the US at 19, working as a journalist in Cincinnati and New Orleans. In 1890 he went to Japan, married Koizumi Setsu, took Japanese citizenship and the name Koizumi Yakumo, and taught English. His books, especially "Kwaidan", a collection of ghost stories, introduced Japanese culture to the West.
George MacDonald|Scottish novelist, poet and minister (1824–1905), a pioneer of fantasy with Phantastes and The Princess and the Goblin, whose work inspired C. S. Lewis and Tolkien.|MacDonald was born in Aberdeenshire, Scotland. He became a Congregational minister but was forced out for his belief that God's love would eventually redeem everyone. He supported his large family by writing and lecturing, often in poverty and poor health. His fantasies, "Phantastes", "Lilith", "At the Back of the North Wind" and "The Princess and the Goblin", were loved by Lewis Carroll, a friend, and later deeply influenced C. S. Lewis, who said reading "Phantastes" "baptised" his imagination, and Tolkien.
Sara Teasdale|American poet (1884–1933) of clear, musical lyrics about love, beauty and stars; her Love Songs won the first Pulitzer Prize for poetry.|Teasdale was born in St Louis into a wealthy family and was a sheltered, often ill child, educated at home. She travelled to Europe and the Middle East and began publishing lyrical poems of love and beauty. "Love Songs" (1917) won the first prize that became the Pulitzer Prize for Poetry. She married a businessman, Ernst Filsinger, but later divorced. Her poem "There Will Come Soft Rains", about nature continuing after humanity, inspired a Ray Bradbury story. In fragile health and depressed, she died in 1933.
E. M. Forster|English novelist (1879–1970) of A Room with a View, Howards End and A Passage to India, who wrote of connection across class and culture.|Edward Morgan Forster was born in London; his father died before he was two and he was raised by his mother and great-aunt, whose legacy made him independent. At Cambridge he joined the Apostles, a discussion society. His novels "A Room with a View", "Howards End", with its motto "Only connect", and "A Passage to India" explore class, friendship and empire. He wrote no novel after 1924; "Maurice", about love between men, was published after his death. He lived for many years with his mother and spent his last decades at King's College, Cambridge.
George Santayana|Spanish-born philosopher, poet and novelist (1863–1952) who taught at Harvard; he wrote on beauty, reason and the life of the spirit.|Jorge Agustín Nicolás Ruiz de Santayana was born in Madrid and moved to Boston at eight, keeping his Spanish citizenship all his life. He studied and taught philosophy at Harvard, where his students included T. S. Eliot and Walter Lippmann. At 48, after his mother's death and an inheritance, he left Harvard and lived in Europe. He wrote "The Life of Reason", where he warned that "those who cannot remember the past are condemned to repeat it", and the novel "The Last Puritan". He spent his last years in a convent in Rome cared for by nuns.
Bertrand Russell|British philosopher, logician and Nobel laureate in literature (1872–1970), who also wrote warmly and plainly about happiness and how to live.|Russell was born into an aristocratic family; his grandfather had twice been prime minister. Orphaned at four, he was raised by his strict grandmother. With Alfred North Whitehead he wrote "Principia Mathematica", trying to ground mathematics in logic, and he discovered Russell's paradox. His opposition to the First World War cost him his Cambridge post and six months in prison. "A History of Western Philosophy" was a bestseller, and he won the Nobel Prize in Literature in 1950. At 89 he was jailed again for a protest against nuclear weapons. He married four times.
J. P. Jacobsen|Danish novelist and poet (1847–1885) whose Niels Lyhne, a young dreamer's search for truth, was beloved by Rilke and Freud.|Jens Peter Jacobsen was born in Thisted, Denmark, the son of a merchant, and trained as a botanist. He translated Darwin's "On the Origin of Species" into Danish and won a gold medal for a study of algae. Diagnosed with tuberculosis in his twenties, he turned to literature. His novels "Marie Grubbe" and "Niels Lyhne", about a young man who loses faith and seeks meaning without God, and his story "Mogens" were revered by Rilke, who recommended them to the young poet, and by Freud and Thomas Mann. He died at 38.
John O'Donohue|Irish poet, philosopher and former priest (1956–2008) of Anam Cara and To Bless the Space Between Us, rooted in Celtic spirituality.|O'Donohue was born in County Clare, Ireland, among the limestone landscape of the Burren, the son of a stonemason. He became a Catholic priest and earned a doctorate in philosophy and theology in Germany, writing on Hegel. "Anam Ċara" (1997), on Celtic spirituality and "soul friendship", became an international bestseller. He left active priesthood and wrote poems and blessings, collected in "To Bless the Space Between Us". He died suddenly in his sleep in France at 52.
Marilynne Robinson|American novelist and essayist (born 1943) of Housekeeping and Gilead, which won the Pulitzer Prize; she writes of grace, wonder and ordinary life.|Robinson grew up in Sandpoint, Idaho, and studied at Brown and the University of Washington. Her first novel, "Housekeeping" (1980), about two sisters raised by their eccentric aunt by a lake, was acclaimed. Then she published no novel for 24 years, writing essays on Calvinism, science and politics. "Gilead" (2004), letters from an elderly preacher in Iowa to his young son, won the Pulitzer Prize, followed by "Home", "Lila" and "Jack". She taught for many years at the Iowa Writers' Workshop.
W. E. B. Du Bois|American sociologist, historian and civil rights leader (1868–1963), author of The Souls of Black Folk and a founder of the NAACP.|William Edward Burghardt Du Bois was born in Great Barrington, Massachusetts. He was the first Black American to earn a doctorate from Harvard, in 1895. "The Philadelphia Negro" was a pioneering sociological study. "The Souls of Black Folk" (1903) described "double consciousness" and the "color line". He co-founded the NAACP in 1909 and edited its magazine, "The Crisis", for 24 years, and was a leader of Pan-Africanism. Disillusioned with the US, he moved to Ghana at 93 and died there on the eve of the 1963 March on Washington.
Gustave Le Bon|French social psychologist (1841–1931) whose book The Crowd shaped early ideas of mass psychology.|Le Bon was born in Nogent-le-Rotrou, France, and trained as a doctor, though he never practised. He travelled through Europe, Asia and North Africa, writing on anthropology and archaeology, and served as an ambulance officer in the Franco-Prussian War. "The Crowd: A Study of the Popular Mind" (1895) argued that individuals in a crowd lose their reason and become suggestible, impulsive and emotional. It influenced Freud, and was read by politicians, sadly including dictators. Much of his racial thinking is now discredited.
Charles Mackay|Scottish poet and journalist (1814–1889), best remembered for his history of financial manias and popular delusions.|Mackay was born in Perth, Scotland, and grew up partly in Belgium and France. He worked as a journalist and editor, including at the Illustrated London News, and wrote popular songs and poems. "Extraordinary Popular Delusions and the Madness of Crowds" (1841) describes financial bubbles like the South Sea Bubble and Dutch tulip mania, witch hunts, alchemy and the Crusades. Its line "men go mad in herds, while they only recover their senses slowly, one by one" is still quoted, especially about markets. He was a New York correspondent during the American Civil War.
Max Horkheimer|German philosopher and sociologist (1895–1973) who directed the Institute for Social Research in Frankfurt and set out the programme of critical theory.|Horkheimer was born in Stuttgart, the son of a Jewish textile manufacturer, and worked in the family business before studying philosophy. In 1930 he became director of Frankfurt's Institute for Social Research, the home of the "Frankfurt School", and defined "critical theory". When the Nazis came to power, he moved the institute to Geneva and then Columbia University in New York. With Adorno he wrote "Dialectic of Enlightenment" in California. After the war he returned to Frankfurt, rebuilt the institute and became rector of the university.
Theodor W. Adorno|German philosopher, sociologist and composer (1903–1969), a leading Frankfurt School thinker on culture, music and modern society.|Adorno was born in Frankfurt to a Jewish wine merchant and a Catholic singer. He studied philosophy and music, and in Vienna studied composition with Alban Berg. He fled the Nazis to Oxford, New York and Los Angeles, where he co-wrote "Dialectic of Enlightenment" with Horkheimer and advised Thomas Mann on music for "Doctor Faustus". "Minima Moralia", reflections from "damaged life", is his most personal book. Returning to Frankfurt, he became a leading public intellectual. He famously wrote that to write poetry after Auschwitz is barbaric, and later qualified it.
