Herbert Marcuse|German-American philosopher of the Frankfurt School (1898–1979), author of One-Dimensional Man and an inspiration to the 1960s New Left.|Marcuse was born in Berlin to a Jewish family and served in the German army in the First World War. He studied with Husserl and Heidegger, then joined the Frankfurt School. Fleeing the Nazis to the US, he worked for American intelligence analysing Nazi Germany during the war. He taught at Brandeis and the University of California, San Diego. "Eros and Civilization" and "One-Dimensional Man" (1964), critiquing consumer society, made him a hero of the 1960s New Left. His students included Angela Davis.
Jürgen Habermas|German philosopher and sociologist (born 1929), the Frankfurt School's best-known second-generation thinker, on democracy, reason and the public sphere.|Habermas was born in Düsseldorf and grew up in Gummersbach. He was born with a cleft palate and had surgery as a child, which he later said made him think about communication and dependence on others. As a teenager he was in the Hitler Youth; learning of Nazi crimes at the Nuremberg trials shaped his life's work on democracy. He became assistant to Adorno in Frankfurt. "The Structural Transformation of the Public Sphere" and "The Theory of Communicative Action" defend reasoned discussion as the basis of democratic life.
Axel Honneth|German philosopher (born 1949), a later director of Frankfurt's Institute for Social Research, known for his theory of recognition.|Honneth was born in Essen, Germany, and studied philosophy and sociology. He was an assistant to Jürgen Habermas and became director of the Institute for Social Research in Frankfurt, continuing the tradition of critical theory. "The Struggle for Recognition" (1992) argues that people need three kinds of recognition, love, rights and esteem, and that injustice is felt as their denial. He has also written on freedom, reification and socialism, and taught at Columbia University.
Hartmut Rosa|German sociologist (born 1965) who writes on social acceleration and on resonance as the heart of a good life.|Rosa was born in 1965 in Lörrach, Germany, in the Black Forest region, and studied political science and philosophy. He is a professor of sociology at Jena and directs the Max Weber Centre in Erfurt. In "Social Acceleration" he argues that modern life speeds up technology, social change and the pace of life, leaving us alienated. In "Resonance" (2016) he proposes that a good life consists of relationships of "resonance" with people, nature, art and work, in which the world speaks to us and we answer.
Ibn Sina|Persian physician and philosopher (c. 980–1037), known in Europe as Avicenna, whose Canon of Medicine was a standard text for centuries.|Abu Ali al-Husayn ibn Sina, known in Europe as Avicenna, was born near Bukhara in today's Uzbekistan. A prodigy, he had memorised the Qur'an by ten and was treating patients at 16. Curing the Samanid sultan won him access to the royal library. He lived an unsettled life serving various rulers, sometimes as vizier, sometimes in prison or hiding. He wrote over 200 works; his "Canon of Medicine" was a standard textbook in Europe until the 1600s, and "The Book of Healing" covers philosophy and science. His "floating man" thought experiment explores self-awareness.
Ibn Khaldun|North African historian and statesman (1332–1406) whose Muqaddimah is often called the first work of sociology.|Ibn Khaldun was born in Tunis to a family of Andalusian origin; the Black Death killed his parents when he was 17. He served rulers across North Africa and Spain as secretary, ambassador and judge, often caught up in intrigue and imprisoned. Withdrawing to a castle in Algeria for four years, he wrote the "Muqaddimah" (1377), the introduction to his world history, analysing why dynasties rise and fall through "asabiyya", group solidarity. He later served as a judge in Cairo and met the conqueror Timur outside Damascus. He is called a founder of sociology.
Ibn al-Haytham|Arab mathematician and physicist (c. 965–c. 1040), known as Alhazen, a pioneer of optics and experimental method.|Abu Ali al-Hasan ibn al-Haytham, known in Europe as Alhazen, was born in Basra in today's Iraq. The story goes that he offered to regulate the Nile floods for the Fatimid caliph in Cairo, realised it was impossible, and feigned madness to escape punishment, spending years under house arrest, where he wrote. His "Book of Optics" showed that we see because light enters the eye, not because rays leave it, using experiments with a camera obscura. His insistence on testing claims by experiment made him a pioneer of the scientific method.
Ibn Rushd|Andalusian philosopher, judge and physician (1126–1198), known as Averroes, the great medieval commentator on Aristotle.|Abu al-Walid Muhammad ibn Rushd, known in Europe as Averroes, was born in Córdoba in Muslim Spain to a family of judges. He served as a judge and court physician to the Almohad caliphs, who asked him to explain Aristotle. His detailed commentaries made him known in Europe simply as "The Commentator". He argued that philosophy and religion do not conflict when properly understood. Late in life his books were burned and he was briefly exiled, then restored shortly before his death in Marrakesh. Raphael placed him in "The School of Athens".
Al-Farabi|Philosopher of the Islamic Golden Age (c. 870–950), called the Second Teacher after Aristotle.|Abu Nasr al-Farabi was born in Central Asia, perhaps in today's Kazakhstan, and studied in Baghdad, where he became a renowned philosopher. Later he lived in Aleppo and Damascus, reportedly living simply, working as a garden watchman at one time. He was called "the Second Teacher" after Aristotle. He wrote commentaries on Aristotle's logic and, in "The Virtuous City", described an ideal state led by a philosopher-prophet, aimed at its citizens' happiness. He also wrote a major treatise on music, and was said to be a fine player.
Zera Yacob|Ethiopian philosopher (1599–1692) whose Hatata argues for reason and the equality of all people.|Zera Yacob was born near Aksum, Ethiopia, the son of a poor farmer, and studied in church schools. When a king's religious persecution reached his region, he fled with a little gold and his Psalms and lived for two years alone in a cave by the Tekezé River. There he reasoned about God and morality for himself, concluding that all religions contain human errors and that truth is found through the light of reason God gave everyone. He later became a tutor, married and wrote his "Hatata" (Inquiry) in 1667. Some scholars have questioned its authorship, but most now accept it.
Alexander von Humboldt|Prussian naturalist and explorer (1769–1859) who saw nature as one interconnected web of life.|Humboldt was born in Berlin to a Prussian aristocratic family and trained as a mining inspector. With his inheritance, he set off in 1799 with the botanist Aimé Bonpland on a five-year expedition through Latin America, climbing Chimborazo, then thought the highest mountain, and mapping the Orinoco. He saw nature as a web of life and noticed how deforestation changed climate. His five-volume "Cosmos" sought to describe the whole universe. He inspired Darwin, Thoreau, Muir and Simón Bolívar. More places, plants and animals are named after him than after anyone else.
Aldo Leopold|American forester and writer (1887–1948), author of A Sand County Almanac and its land ethic.|Leopold was born in Burlington, Iowa, and studied at Yale's new forestry school. As a young forest ranger in Arizona and New Mexico he shot wolves to protect deer, until he watched "a fierce green fire" die in a wolf's eyes and began to understand the land as a whole. He helped create the first official wilderness area in the US, in the Gila, and founded the science of wildlife management. With his family he restored a worn-out farm in Wisconsin. He died fighting a neighbour's grass fire in 1948. "A Sand County Almanac" was published the next year.
Rachel Carson|American marine biologist and writer (1907–1964) whose Silent Spring began the modern environmental movement.|Carson grew up in Springdale, Pennsylvania, loving nature and writing. She earned a master's in zoology and worked for the US Fish and Wildlife Service, writing pamphlets and books. Her trilogy about the sea, including "The Sea Around Us", made her famous. In "Silent Spring" (1962), written while she was fighting breast cancer, she documented the harm of pesticides like DDT to birds, wildlife and people. Chemical companies attacked her, but the book led to new laws and the founding of the modern environmental movement. She died in 1964.
Arne Næss|Norwegian philosopher and mountaineer (1912–2009) who founded deep ecology.|Næss was born near Oslo and became, at 27, the youngest professor in Norway's history. A passionate mountaineer, he led the first ascent of Tirich Mir in Pakistan and spent much of his life in a hut called Tvergastein high in the mountains. Influenced by Spinoza and Gandhi, he coined "deep ecology" in 1973, arguing that all living beings have value in themselves and that humans should seek "self-realisation" by identifying with the wider world. In 1970 he chained himself to rocks at a Norwegian waterfall to protest a dam.
E. O. Wilson|American biologist (1929–2021), an expert on ants and a champion of biodiversity, who wrote of biophilia.|Edward Osborne Wilson grew up in Alabama and Florida, roaming woods and swamps. A fishing accident blinded one eye, so he studied small creatures up close, especially ants, becoming the world's leading expert on them. A Harvard professor, he founded sociobiology, the study of the biological basis of social behaviour, which caused fierce controversy. He won two Pulitzer Prizes, for "On Human Nature" and "The Ants". He coined "biophilia" and proposed "Half-Earth", protecting half the planet for nature. He discovered hundreds of ant species.
Lev Vygotsky|Soviet psychologist (1896–1934) whose work on thought, language and learning shaped modern education.|Vygotsky was born in Orsha, Belarus, and grew up in Gomel in a Jewish family. He was admitted to Moscow University through a lottery for Jewish students and studied law, while also reading literature and philosophy. He taught in Gomel, then moved to Moscow and became a psychologist, working on how children learn through social interaction and language, and on education for disabled children. He described the "zone of proximal development". He suffered from tuberculosis and died at 37. His works were banned under Stalin and rediscovered decades later.
Noam Chomsky|American linguist (born 1928) who transformed the study of language with the idea of a universal grammar.|Chomsky was born in Philadelphia; his father was a Hebrew scholar. He studied linguistics at the University of Pennsylvania and taught for over fifty years at MIT. "Syntactic Structures" (1957) proposed that humans share an innate capacity for language, a "universal grammar", which transformed linguistics and helped start cognitive science. He has also been a leading critic of US foreign policy and mass media since the Vietnam War, writing "Manufacturing Consent" with Edward Herman. He is one of the most cited scholars in history.
J. L. Austin|British philosopher (1911–1960) of ordinary language, known for How to Do Things with Words.|John Langshaw Austin was born in Lancaster, England, and studied classics at Oxford. During the Second World War he worked in British army intelligence and was a key figure in preparing for D-Day. Back at Oxford, he led "ordinary language philosophy", examining how we actually use words. His lectures, published after his early death as "How to Do Things with Words", showed that some sentences don't describe but perform actions, like "I promise" or "I do" at a wedding. He called these "performatives". He died of cancer at 48.
Elizabeth Loftus|American psychologist (born 1944) whose research showed how malleable human memory is.|Loftus was born in Los Angeles. Her mother drowned in a swimming pool when she was fourteen. Studying memory at Stanford and then the University of Washington and UC Irvine, she showed through experiments that memories are easily distorted by suggestion, and that false memories, like being lost in a shopping mall as a child, can be implanted. She has testified as an expert in hundreds of trials. Her work during the "recovered memory" debates of the 1990s made her controversial but transformed how courts treat eyewitness evidence.
James Lovelock and Lynn Margulis|Lovelock (1919–2022), a British scientist and inventor, and Margulis (1938–2011), an American biologist, developed the Gaia hypothesis.|James Lovelock was a British chemist and inventor whose electron capture detector revealed the spread of pesticides and ozone-damaging chemicals worldwide. Working for NASA on how to detect life on Mars, he realised that Earth's atmosphere is kept far from chemical equilibrium by living things. He proposed the Gaia hypothesis, named on the suggestion of the novelist William Golding, that life regulates the planet's conditions. Lynn Margulis, the microbiologist who showed that cells arose by symbiosis, added the role of microbes. Lovelock worked independently and lived to 103.
Martin Luther King Jr.|American minister and civil rights leader (1929–1968) who led the movement against segregation through nonviolence; Nobel Peace Prize 1964.|King was born in Atlanta, the son and grandson of Baptist ministers. He entered Morehouse College at 15 and earned a doctorate in theology at Boston University. As a young pastor in Montgomery, Alabama, he led the bus boycott of 1955–56 after Rosa Parks's arrest. Inspired by Jesus and Gandhi, he led nonviolent campaigns against segregation, wrote "Letter from Birmingham Jail" and gave the "I Have a Dream" speech in 1963. He won the Nobel Peace Prize at 35. He turned to poverty and the Vietnam War, and was assassinated in Memphis in 1968, aged 39.
Nelson Mandela|South African anti-apartheid leader (1918–2013), imprisoned for 27 years, who became his country's first democratically elected president; Nobel Peace Prize 1993.|Rolihlahla Mandela was born in the village of Mvezo in South Africa's Eastern Cape, into the Thembu royal family; a teacher gave him the name Nelson. He studied law and opened South Africa's first Black law firm with Oliver Tambo. A leader of the African National Congress, he turned to armed resistance after the Sharpeville massacre. Sentenced to life in 1964, he spent 27 years in prison, eighteen of them on Robben Island breaking rocks. Released in 1990, he negotiated the end of apartheid, shared the Nobel Peace Prize and in 1994 became South Africa's first democratically elected president, preaching reconciliation.
Muhammad Ali|American boxer (1942–2016), three-time world heavyweight champion, who refused the Vietnam draft on conscience and became a voice for civil rights and peace.|Cassius Clay was born in Louisville, Kentucky. At twelve his bicycle was stolen and he told a policeman he wanted to beat up the thief; the officer, a boxing coach, suggested he learn to box first. He won Olympic gold in 1960 and the heavyweight title in 1964, then joined the Nation of Islam and changed his name. Refusing to be drafted for the Vietnam War on religious grounds, he was stripped of his title and banned for three years; the Supreme Court overturned his conviction. He regained the title twice. He lived with Parkinson's disease for over thirty years and lit the Olympic flame in 1996.
Marianne Williamson|American author and spiritual teacher (born 1952), whose A Return to Love, a reflection on A Course in Miracles, contains her best-known passage, often misattributed to Nelson Mandela.|Williamson was born in Houston, Texas. After a restless youth she began, in the 1980s, giving talks on "A Course in Miracles", a spiritual text, in Los Angeles. During the AIDS crisis she founded centres to support people living with HIV and a charity delivering meals to the sick. "A Return to Love" (1992) became a bestseller, containing the passage beginning "Our deepest fear is not that we are inadequate", often misattributed to Nelson Mandela. She later ran for Congress and twice for the US presidency.
Mahatma Gandhi|Indian lawyer and leader of India's independence movement (1869–1948), who made nonviolent resistance, satyagraha, a force for change across the world.|Mohandas Karamchand Gandhi was born in Porbandar, Gujarat, and married at 13. He studied law in London. In South Africa, thrown off a first-class train for being Indian, he began a 21-year struggle for Indian rights, developing "satyagraha", nonviolent resistance. Returning to India in 1915, he led the movement for independence, through campaigns such as the Salt March of 1930, and was imprisoned many times. He lived simply, spun his own cloth and worked against untouchability and for Hindu-Muslim unity. Months after independence, in January 1948, he was assassinated by a Hindu nationalist.
Abraham Lincoln|16th President of the United States (1809–1865), who led the Union through the Civil War and ended slavery.|Lincoln was born in a log cabin in Kentucky and grew up on the Indiana frontier with less than a year of formal schooling, reading every book he could borrow. His mother died when he was nine. He worked as a rail-splitter, boatman and shopkeeper, taught himself law and served in the Illinois legislature and Congress. Elected president in 1860, he led the Union through the Civil War, issued the Emancipation Proclamation and pushed the Thirteenth Amendment abolishing slavery. His Gettysburg Address is 272 words long. He was assassinated days after the war ended in 1865.
Mary Wollstonecraft|English writer and philosopher (1759–1797) whose A Vindication of the Rights of Woman founded modern feminism; mother of Mary Shelley.|Wollstonecraft was born in London; her father squandered the family money and was violent, and she slept outside her mother's door to protect her. She worked as a companion, a teacher who founded a school with her sister and friend, and a governess, then as a writer and translator for a radical publisher. She wrote "A Vindication of the Rights of Men" and "A Vindication of the Rights of Woman" (1792). She went to Paris during the Revolution, had a daughter with an American, and survived two attempts on her own life. She married William Godwin and died days after giving birth to Mary, the future Mary Shelley.
Desmond Tutu|South African Anglican archbishop (1931–2021), Nobel Peace laureate, who opposed apartheid and chaired the Truth and Reconciliation Commission.|Tutu was born in Klerksdorp, South Africa; his father was a teacher. He wanted to be a doctor but couldn't afford it, so he became a teacher, then left teaching in protest against inferior "Bantu education" and was ordained as an Anglican priest. As the first Black Anglican Archbishop of Cape Town, he was a fearless, joyful voice against apartheid, winning the Nobel Peace Prize in 1984. After apartheid he chaired the Truth and Reconciliation Commission. He coined the phrase "Rainbow Nation" and wrote "The Book of Joy" with the Dalai Lama.
The Dalai Lama|Tenzin Gyatso (born 1935), the 14th Dalai Lama, Tibetan Buddhist leader in exile and Nobel Peace laureate, who teaches compassion and inner peace.|Lhamo Thondup was born to a farming family in Taktser, north-eastern Tibet. At two he was recognised as the reincarnation of the 13th Dalai Lama and taken to Lhasa, where he studied Buddhist philosophy. He took full political power at 15 as China invaded Tibet. In 1959, after a failed uprising, he fled to India, where he established a government in exile at Dharamsala. He has travelled the world teaching compassion and interfaith dialogue, and won the Nobel Peace Prize in 1989. He has met scientists regularly to discuss the mind, and handed political authority to an elected leader in 2011.
Mirabai|Rajput princess and bhakti poet-saint (c. 1498–1547) of Rajasthan, whose devotional songs to Krishna are still sung across India.|Mirabai was a Rajput princess of Merta in Rajasthan, born around 1498. Legend says that as a child she was given a statue of Krishna and devoted herself to him as her husband. Married into the royal family of Mewar, she refused to follow the customs expected of a widow after her husband's death and spent her time singing and dancing in temples with holy men, scandalising her in-laws, who are said to have tried to poison her. She left to wander as a devotee and is said to have merged into Krishna's image at Dwarka. Her songs are still sung across India.
Gabriela Mistral|Chilean poet, teacher and diplomat (1889–1957), the first Latin American to win the Nobel Prize in Literature (1945).|Lucila Godoy Alcayaga was born in Vicuña, in Chile's Elqui Valley, and became a rural schoolteacher at 15. The suicide of a young man she had loved inspired her "Sonnets of Death", which won a national prize in 1914, and she took the pen name Gabriela Mistral. She helped reform Mexico's schools and served for decades as a Chilean consul in Europe and the Americas. Her poems are full of tenderness for children, mothers and the poor. She became the first Latin American to win the Nobel Prize in Literature, in 1945.
Lewis Carroll|English writer, mathematician and photographer (1832–1898), author of Alice's Adventures in Wonderland and Through the Looking-Glass.|Charles Lutwidge Dodgson was born in Cheshire, the eldest son of a clergyman, among eleven children. He lectured in mathematics at Christ Church, Oxford, for most of his life, wrote books on logic and was a pioneering photographer. He had a stammer. On a boat trip in July 1862 he told a story to Alice Liddell, daughter of the dean of Christ Church, and her sisters; it became "Alice's Adventures in Wonderland" (1865), followed by "Through the Looking-Glass". He also wrote "The Hunting of the Snark" and invented word games.
Kenneth Grahame|Scottish writer (1859–1932), a banker by day, who wrote The Wind in the Willows from bedtime stories for his son.|Grahame was born in Edinburgh; his mother died when he was five, and his father, an alcoholic, sent him to live with his grandmother in Berkshire, near the River Thames. He wanted to go to Oxford, but his family sent him to work at the Bank of England, where he rose to Secretary. His bedtime stories and letters to his young son Alastair, about Mole, Ratty, Badger and Toad, became "The Wind in the Willows" (1908). Alastair, who struggled with poor sight and other difficulties, died at nineteen, a grief Grahame never recovered from.
Neil deGrasse Tyson|American astrophysicist (born 1958), director of the Hayden Planetarium and host of Cosmos: A Spacetime Odyssey.|Tyson grew up in the Bronx, New York; a visit to the Hayden Planetarium at nine set him on his path. As a teenager he met Carl Sagan, who invited him to visit Cornell. He studied physics at Harvard and astrophysics at Columbia, and was a wrestler and dancer. Since 1996 he has directed the Hayden Planetarium. He hosted the series "Cosmos: A Spacetime Odyssey" (2014), continuing Sagan's work, and the podcast "StarTalk". His book "Astrophysics for People in a Hurry" was a bestseller.
Richard Feynman|American physicist (1918–1988), Nobel laureate for quantum electrodynamics, teacher and author of Surely You're Joking, Mr. Feynman!|Feynman grew up in Far Rockaway, New York, where his father taught him to notice and question everything. He worked on the atomic bomb at Los Alamos, where he cracked colleagues' safes for fun. His first wife Arline died of tuberculosis there. At Caltech he developed quantum electrodynamics and "Feynman diagrams", winning the Nobel Prize in 1965. His "Lectures on Physics" are classics. He played bongo drums and learned to draw. On the Challenger disaster commission he dropped a rubber O-ring into ice water to show why the shuttle failed. "Surely You're Joking, Mr. Feynman!" collects his stories.
F. Scott Fitzgerald|American novelist (1896–1940) of the Jazz Age, whose The Great Gatsby is a lament for the American dream.|Francis Scott Key Fitzgerald was born in St Paul, Minnesota, named after a distant relative who wrote "The Star-Spangled Banner". He left Princeton for the army in 1917. His first novel, "This Side of Paradise", made him famous at 23 and let him marry Zelda Sayre. They became symbols of the Jazz Age, a term he coined. "The Great Gatsby" (1925) sold poorly in his lifetime. Zelda's mental illness, his alcoholism and debt shadowed his later years. He worked as a Hollywood screenwriter and died of a heart attack at 44, believing himself a failure.
William Faulkner|American novelist (1897–1962) of the Mississippi South, Nobel laureate, author of The Sound and the Fury.|Faulkner was born in New Albany, Mississippi, and lived most of his life in Oxford, Mississippi. He left school, trained briefly with the Royal Air Force in Canada, and worked as a postmaster, reportedly reading while customers waited. He created Yoknapatawpha County, the setting of most of his novels, including "The Sound and the Fury", "As I Lay Dying" (written, he claimed, in six weeks while working night shifts at a power plant) and "Absalom, Absalom!" He wrote Hollywood screenplays for money. He won the Nobel Prize in 1949, saying in his speech that man "will not merely endure: he will prevail".
Langston Hughes|American poet (1901–1967) and leading voice of the Harlem Renaissance, who brought jazz and the blues into verse.|James Mercer Langston Hughes was born in Joplin, Missouri, and raised mostly by his grandmother in Lawrence, Kansas, after his parents separated. He wrote "The Negro Speaks of Rivers" at 17, on a train crossing the Mississippi. Working as a busboy in Washington, he left poems by the poet Vachel Lindsay's plate, and Lindsay praised them. He became a leading voice of the Harlem Renaissance, bringing jazz and blues rhythms into poetry. He wrote poems, novels, plays and the stories of "Simple", and lived in Harlem until his death.
Nathaniel Hawthorne|American novelist (1804–1864) of guilt and conscience in Puritan New England, author of The Scarlet Letter.|Hawthorne was born in Salem, Massachusetts; his ancestor John Hathorne was a judge at the Salem witch trials, and he added a "w" to his name to distance himself. His father, a sea captain, died when he was four. After college he spent twelve years writing in seclusion in his mother's house. He worked at the Boston and Salem custom houses, lived briefly at the utopian Brook Farm, and was a friend of Melville. "The Scarlet Letter" (1850) and "The House of the Seven Gables" explore guilt and sin. He served as US consul in Liverpool.
Geoffrey Chaucer|English poet (c. 1343–1400), the father of English literature, whose Canterbury Tales gathers a whole nation on pilgrimage.|Chaucer was the son of a London wine merchant and served as a page in a royal household. Captured in France during the Hundred Years' War, he was ransomed with help from the king. He served as a diplomat in France and Italy, where he may have met Petrarch, and as controller of customs for the port of London. Writing in English when French and Latin were the languages of prestige, he produced "Troilus and Criseyde" and "The Canterbury Tales", stories told by pilgrims of every class. He was the first poet buried in what became Poets' Corner in Westminster Abbey.
Heinrich Heine|German poet (1797–1856) of bittersweet love songs, many set to music by Schubert and Schumann, and a sharp-witted exile in Paris.|Harry Heine was born in Düsseldorf to a Jewish family. He failed as a businessman in his rich uncle's bank and studied law, converting to Protestantism in 1825 as an "entry ticket to European culture". His "Book of Songs" (1827) made him famous; Schubert and Schumann set many of its poems. In 1831 he moved to Paris, where he spent the rest of his life, writing sharp satire about Germany; his works were banned there. His last eight years were spent paralysed in bed, his "mattress-grave", where he still wrote wonderfully.
Henrik Ibsen|Norwegian playwright (1828–1906), father of modern drama, whose A Doll's House and Ghosts shook the conscience of Europe.|Ibsen was born in Skien, Norway; when he was seven his father's business failed. At 15 he was apprenticed to a pharmacist and fathered an illegitimate child. He worked in theatres in Bergen and Christiania, then spent 27 years abroad in Italy and Germany. His verse dramas "Brand" and "Peer Gynt" were followed by realistic plays that shocked audiences: "A Doll's House" (1879), whose heroine Nora slams the door on her marriage, "Ghosts", "An Enemy of the People" and "Hedda Gabler". He is called the father of modern drama.
George Bernard Shaw|Irish playwright and critic (1856–1950), Nobel laureate, author of Pygmalion and a master of the paradox.|Shaw was born in Dublin; his father was an alcoholic and his mother, a singer, moved to London when he was 16. He followed her at 20 and educated himself in the British Museum Reading Room, writing five unsuccessful novels. He became a music and theatre critic, a leading Fabian socialist and a vegetarian. His plays, including "Arms and the Man", "Major Barbara", "Man and Superman" and "Pygmalion", use comedy to attack social hypocrisy. He won the Nobel Prize in 1925 and an Oscar for the "Pygmalion" screenplay. He lived to 94.
Molière|French comic playwright and actor (1622–1673) who mocked hypocrisy and pretension in Tartuffe and The Misanthrope.|Jean-Baptiste Poquelin, the son of a royal upholsterer, gave up his inheritance to become an actor, taking the stage name Molière. His first company went bankrupt and he was briefly jailed for debt. He toured the provinces for thirteen years before winning the favour of Louis XIV. His comedies, "Tartuffe", about a religious hypocrite, which was banned for five years, "The Misanthrope", "The Miser" and "The Imaginary Invalid", mock pretension and folly. He collapsed on stage while playing the hypochondriac in "The Imaginary Invalid" and died hours later.
Jean Racine|French tragic poet (1639–1699) whose Phaedra is the summit of French classical tragedy.|Racine was orphaned young and raised by his grandparents and educated at the Jansenist convent school of Port-Royal, where he learned Greek. He broke with his teachers to write for the theatre and became the leading tragic playwright of France, with "Andromaque", "Britannicus", "Bérénice" and "Phèdre" (1677), in perfectly controlled alexandrine verse about overwhelming passion. After "Phèdre" he gave up the theatre, married, reconciled with Port-Royal and became royal historiographer to Louis XIV. He later wrote two biblical plays for a girls' school.
Ferdowsi|Persian poet (c. 940–1020) who spent some thirty years writing the Shahnameh, the Book of Kings, the national epic of Iran.|Abu'l-Qasim Ferdowsi was born to a family of landowners near Tus, in Khorasan. He spent about thirty years writing the "Shahnameh" (Book of Kings), some 50,000 couplets telling the history of Persia from the creation of the world to the Arab conquest, including the tragic story of the hero Rostam, who unknowingly kills his son Sohrab. He wrote it in Persian with very few Arabic words, helping preserve the language. Legend says that Sultan Mahmud paid him in silver instead of gold, and that the promised reward arrived as his coffin was carried out.
Nizami|Persian poet (c. 1141–1209) of Ganja, whose Layla and Majnun became the great love story of the Islamic world.|Nizami Ganjavi was born in Ganja, in today's Azerbaijan, and lived there all his life; his parents died when he was young. He was deeply learned in philosophy, astronomy and medicine. His "Khamsa" (Quintet) of five long narrative poems includes "Layla and Majnun", the story of a young man driven mad by love, which became the great love story of the Islamic world, "Khosrow and Shirin" and "Haft Paykar" (Seven Beauties), in which a king hears seven stories from seven princesses in seven pavilions. His first wife, a Kipchak slave given to him by a ruler, died young; he mourned her in his poems.
Kalidasa|Classical Sanskrit poet and dramatist (around the 5th century), author of Shakuntala and The Cloud-Messenger.|Almost nothing is known about Kalidasa's life. He probably lived in the Gupta Empire, around the 4th or 5th century, perhaps at the court of Chandragupta II in Ujjain. Legend says he was a dull young man married to a learned princess, who mocked him; he prayed to the goddess Kali and was granted brilliance, hence his name, "servant of Kali". He wrote the play "Shakuntala", which Goethe adored, the long poems "Kumarasambhava" and "Raghuvamsha", and "The Cloud Messenger", in which an exiled spirit sends a love message by a cloud. He is considered the greatest poet of classical Sanskrit.
Tulsidas|Indian poet-saint (c. 1532–1623) whose Ramcharitmanas retold the Ramayana in Hindi as a song of devotion.|Tulsidas was born in Rajapur in northern India; legend says he was abandoned soon after birth because of an unlucky star and raised by a servant woman. He was devoted to his wife, who rebuked him, saying that if he loved God as much as her body he would be free; he left to become an ascetic. In Varanasi he wrote the "Ramcharitmanas", retelling the Ramayana in Awadhi, a language ordinary people spoke, rather than Sanskrit. It became one of the most loved books in North India, recited daily in homes and staged in the yearly Ramlila.
Murasaki Shikibu|Japanese lady-in-waiting (c. 973–c. 1014) at the Heian court, author of The Tale of Genji, often called the first novel.|Murasaki Shikibu's real name is unknown; "Murasaki" comes from a character in her novel. Her father was a scholar and governor, and she learned Chinese classics by listening to her brother's lessons, which was unusual for a girl. She married an older man, who died after two years, and became a lady-in-waiting to Empress Shōshi. "The Tale of Genji", written around 1000–1012, follows the loves and losses of the "shining prince" Genji across 54 chapters, and is often called the world's first novel. She also left a diary of court life.
Natsume Sōseki|Japanese novelist (1867–1916) of the modern self and its loneliness, author of Kokoro and Kusamakura.|Natsume Kinnosuke was born in Tokyo, the unwanted youngest child of an ageing family, and was given away for adoption, then returned. He studied English and became a teacher, and in 1900 the government sent him to London for two years, where he was lonely and miserable. Back in Japan, his comic novel "I Am a Cat" made him famous. He gave up a professorship to write for the Asahi newspaper. "Botchan", "Kokoro" and "Grass on the Wayside" explore loneliness and the conflict between tradition and modernity. His face was on the 1,000-yen note.
Robert Burns|Scotland's national poet (1759–1796), a ploughman's son who wrote Auld Lang Syne and A Red, Red Rose.|Burns was born in Alloway, Ayrshire, the son of a tenant farmer, and worked the land from boyhood, which left him with lasting ill health. Planning to emigrate to Jamaica, he published "Poems, Chiefly in the Scottish Dialect" (1786) to pay his passage; its success kept him in Scotland. Edinburgh society lionised him as the "ploughman poet". He collected and rewrote hundreds of old Scottish songs, including "Auld Lang Syne" and "A Red, Red Rose", and wrote "To a Mouse". He worked as an excise officer and died at 37. Burns Night is celebrated worldwide.
Laurence Sterne|Irish-born English clergyman and novelist (1713–1768) whose playful Tristram Shandy broke every rule of the novel.|Sterne was born in Ireland, the son of an army officer, and spent his childhood moving between garrisons. He studied at Cambridge on a scholarship and became a country parson in Yorkshire. At 46, he published the first volumes of "The Life and Opinions of Tristram Shandy, Gentleman" (1759), a playful novel in which the narrator barely manages to get himself born, with digressions, a black page and a marbled page. It made him a celebrity. He travelled to France and Italy for his tuberculosis and wrote "A Sentimental Journey". He died in London at 54.
François Rabelais|French Renaissance writer, monk and physician (c. 1494–1553), whose giants Gargantua and Pantagruel laugh at everything.|Rabelais was born near Chinon in the Loire valley and became a Franciscan, then Benedictine monk, studying Greek and law. He left the monastery to study medicine in Montpellier and practised as a doctor in Lyon. His comic books about two giants, "Pantagruel" and "Gargantua", full of feasting, bawdy humour, learning and satire of monks and scholars, were condemned by the Sorbonne. They include the Abbey of Thélème, whose only rule is "Do what you will." His name gave us the word "Rabelaisian".
Giovanni Boccaccio|Italian writer (1313–1375) whose Decameron gathers a hundred tales told by ten young people fleeing the plague.|Boccaccio was born in Florence or Certaldo, the illegitimate son of a merchant, and spent his youth in Naples learning banking and law, preferring poetry. Back in Florence he survived the Black Death of 1348, which killed perhaps half the city. In the "Decameron" (completed around 1353), ten young people flee the plague to a villa outside Florence and tell a hundred stories of love, wit and fortune over ten days. He became a friend of Petrarch, wrote the first biography of Dante and lectured publicly on the "Divine Comedy".
Luís de Camões|Portugal's great poet (c. 1524–1580), soldier and wanderer, author of The Lusiads and some of the finest sonnets in Portuguese.|Camões was born probably in Lisbon to a family of minor nobility and studied at Coimbra. He lost his right eye fighting in Ceuta in North Africa. After a street brawl in which he wounded a court official, he was jailed, then sent to serve in India. He spent seventeen years in the East, in Goa and Macau. Shipwrecked off the Mekong delta, he is said to have swum ashore holding his manuscript above the waves. "The Lusiads" (1572), an epic of Vasco da Gama's voyage to India, is Portugal's national poem. He died poor in Lisbon.
Chinua Achebe|Nigerian novelist (1930–2013) whose Things Fall Apart told the story of Africa from the inside.|Albert Chinualumogu Achebe was born in Ogidi, eastern Nigeria; his parents were Christian converts, and he grew up between Igbo tradition and Christianity. He studied at University College Ibadan and worked for the Nigerian Broadcasting Service. "Things Fall Apart" (1958), about an Igbo leader's downfall under colonialism, answered European portrayals of Africa and became the most widely read African novel. During the Biafran war he served as a Biafran envoy. A car accident in 1990 left him paralysed from the waist down, and he taught for many years at Bard College in New York.
Wole Soyinka|Nigerian playwright and poet (born 1934), the first African Nobel laureate in literature, imprisoned for speaking against tyranny.|Akinwande Oluwole Soyinka was born in Abeokuta, Nigeria, and studied at Ibadan and the University of Leeds, working at London's Royal Court Theatre. His plays, such as "Death and the King's Horseman", draw on Yoruba myth and ritual. During the Nigerian Civil War he was imprisoned for nearly two years, mostly in solitary confinement, for trying to broker peace; "The Man Died" describes it. In 1986 he became the first African to win the Nobel Prize in Literature. He went into exile under the dictator Sani Abacha, who sentenced him to death in absentia.
Ben Okri|Nigerian-born poet and novelist (born 1959) whose The Famished Road weaves dream and reality.|Okri was born in Minna, Nigeria, and spent his early childhood in London before returning to Nigeria, where he witnessed the civil war. He moved back to England to study, and was at times homeless while writing. His first novels appeared when he was in his early twenties. "The Famished Road" (1991), narrated by Azaro, a spirit child who chooses to stay in the world of the living, won the Booker Prize when he was 32. He also writes poetry and essays, including "A Way of Being Free".
Sylvia Plath|American poet and novelist (1932–1963), author of Ariel and The Bell Jar, writer of fierce and luminous intensity.|Plath was born in Boston; her father, a professor of biology and expert on bees, died when she was eight. A brilliant student, she won a guest editorship at Mademoiselle magazine in 1953, then suffered a breakdown and attempted to take her life, experiences behind her novel "The Bell Jar". On a Fulbright at Cambridge she met and married the poet Ted Hughes. After their separation, alone with two small children in London during a bitter winter, she wrote most of the poems of "Ariel" in a burst of creativity before her death in 1963 at 30. Her "Collected Poems" won the Pulitzer Prize in 1982.
W. H. Auden|English-born poet (1907–1973) of love, conscience and the age of anxiety.|Wystan Hugh Auden was born in York, the son of a doctor, and grew up in Birmingham. At Oxford he became the leader of a generation of young left-wing poets. He went to Spain during the Civil War and to China during the Japanese invasion. In 1939 he moved to New York and became an American citizen; he returned to Christianity. His poems include "Funeral Blues" ("Stop all the clocks") and "September 1, 1939". He lived for decades with his partner Chester Kallman, and spent summers in Austria. He was Oxford Professor of Poetry.
Philip Larkin|English poet and librarian (1922–1985) whose plain, rueful poems find tenderness in ordinary life.|Larkin was born in Coventry, the son of a city treasurer. He studied English at Oxford with Kingsley Amis, a lifelong friend, and became a librarian, working for thirty years at the University of Hull. He published only four main books of poems, including "The Whitsun Weddings" and "High Windows", plain-spoken, witty and melancholy, about time, love and death. "An Arundel Tomb" ends "What will survive of us is love." He declined the post of Poet Laureate in 1984. He asked that his diaries be destroyed after his death.
Elizabeth Bishop|American poet (1911–1979) of exact, quietly astonishing observation, author of One Art.|Bishop was born in Worcester, Massachusetts. Her father died when she was eight months old, and her mother was committed to a mental hospital when she was five; Elizabeth never saw her again. She was raised by grandparents in Nova Scotia and then Massachusetts. She travelled widely and lived for fifteen years in Brazil with the architect Lota de Macedo Soares. She published only about a hundred poems, each perfectly crafted, such as "One Art" ("The art of losing isn't hard to master") and "The Fish". She won the Pulitzer Prize in 1956.
César Vallejo|Peruvian poet (1892–1938), one of the great innovators of 20th-century poetry in Spanish.|Vallejo was born in Santiago de Chuco in the Peruvian Andes, the youngest of eleven children, of mixed Spanish and indigenous heritage. He studied in Trujillo and Lima. "The Black Heralds" (1918) was followed by "Trilce" (1922), which broke the rules of Spanish poetry, written partly after he was wrongly imprisoned for months. He moved to Paris in 1923 and never returned to Peru, living in poverty. He supported the Spanish Republic and wrote "Spain, Take This Cup from Me". He died in Paris at 46; in one of his best-known poems he had imagined dying in Paris on a day of rain.
John Steinbeck|American novelist (1902–1968), Nobel laureate, author of The Grapes of Wrath and East of Eden.|Steinbeck was born in Salinas, California, and worked on ranches and as a labourer, getting to know migrant farm workers. He studied marine biology at Stanford without finishing a degree. "Of Mice and Men" (1937) and "The Grapes of Wrath" (1939), about the Joad family fleeing the Dust Bowl, made him famous and controversial; the latter won the Pulitzer Prize and was banned in some places. He was a war correspondent in the Second World War. "East of Eden" retells the Cain and Abel story. He won the Nobel Prize in 1962.
Vladimir Nabokov|Russian-American novelist (1899–1977), a dazzling stylist and lover of butterflies, author of Speak, Memory and Pale Fire.|Nabokov was born in St Petersburg into a wealthy, liberal aristocratic family and grew up speaking Russian, English and French. After the Revolution the family fled; his father was assassinated in Berlin in 1922 by monarchists. Nabokov lived in Berlin and Paris for twenty years, writing in Russian. In 1940 he moved to the US, taught at Wellesley and Cornell, and worked as curator of butterflies at Harvard's Museum of Comparative Zoology. "Lolita" (1955) made him rich. He also wrote "Pnin", "Pale Fire" and the memoir "Speak, Memory". He spent his last years in Switzerland.
Julio Cortázar|Argentine writer (1914–1984) of playful, labyrinthine fiction, author of Hopscotch.|Cortázar was born in Brussels to Argentine parents and grew up near Buenos Aires; his father left the family. He taught in provincial schools and translated, including all of Poe's stories. Opposing Perón, he moved to Paris in 1951 and worked as a translator for UNESCO. His short stories, such as "House Taken Over" and "Blow-Up", which inspired Antonioni's film, slip from ordinary life into the strange. His novel "Hopscotch" (1963) can be read in more than one order. He loved jazz and boxing.
Yasunari Kawabata|Japanese novelist (1899–1972), the first Japanese Nobel laureate in literature, author of Snow Country.|Kawabata was born in Osaka and orphaned young: by fifteen he had lost his parents, his only sister and his grandparents. He studied English and Japanese literature at Tokyo Imperial University and helped found a modernist movement. "The Dancing Girl of Izu" made his name. His novels "Snow Country", "Thousand Cranes" and "The Sound of the Mountain" are delicate, lyrical and sad. He supported other writers, including the young Mishima. In 1968 he became the first Japanese writer to win the Nobel Prize. He died by gas inhalation in 1972.
Yukio Mishima|Japanese novelist and playwright (1925–1970), author of The Temple of the Golden Pavilion.|Kimitake Hiraoka was born in Tokyo and raised largely by his possessive grandmother, kept indoors and away from boys' games. He took the pen name Yukio Mishima and published as a teenager. "Confessions of a Mask" (1949) made him famous. He wrote some forty novels, including "The Temple of the Golden Pavilion" and "The Sea of Fertility" tetralogy, as well as plays. He took up bodybuilding and martial arts, and formed a private militia. In 1970 he took his own life after a failed attempt to rouse the army at a military base in Tokyo.
J. J. Thomas|Trinidadian schoolmaster and scholar (c. 1840–1889), born to formerly enslaved parents, who wrote the first grammar of Trinidadian Creole and answered the racism of the historian J. A. Froude in Froudacity.|John Jacob Thomas was born in Trinidad of African descent, into a poor family, a few years after the end of slavery, and was largely self-taught. He became a village schoolteacher and rose in the colony's education service. He wrote "The Theory and Practice of Creole Grammar" (1869), the first serious study of Trinidad's French Creole. In 1889 he answered the English historian James Anthony Froude's dismissive book on the West Indies with "Froudacity", a brilliant defence of Black West Indians' capacity for self-government. He died in London in 1889, shortly after it was published.
Maxwell Philip|Trinidadian lawyer and Solicitor-General (1829–1888) whose Emmanuel Appadocca, a pirate tale against slavery, is the first Trinidadian novel.|Michel Maxwell Philip was born in Trinidad of mixed parentage, the son of a white planter, and was educated in Scotland. Returning to Trinidad, he became a barrister and served for many years as Solicitor General. He was also a celebrated orator and a defender of the rights of Black and coloured Trinidadians. His novel "Emmanuel Appadocca" (1854), about the son of a planter who becomes a pirate to take revenge on his father, is one of the first novels by a Caribbean writer, a protest against slavery and the injustices of colonial society.
Sam Selvon|Trinidadian novelist (1923–1994) whose The Lonely Londoners told the Windrush generation's story in the music of Caribbean speech.|Samuel Selvon was born in South Trinidad to an East Indian father and a mother of Indian and Scottish ancestry. He served on minesweepers in the Second World War and worked as a journalist in Port of Spain. In 1950 he sailed to England on the same ship as George Lamming. "The Lonely Londoners" (1956), about West Indian immigrants in London, written in a Trinidadian voice for both dialogue and narration, is a landmark of Caribbean and Black British writing. He also wrote "A Brighter Sun". He later moved to Canada.
Earl Lovelace|Trinidadian novelist and playwright (born 1935) of Carnival, community and dignity, author of The Dragon Can't Dance and Salt.|Lovelace was born in Toco, Trinidad, and grew up in Tobago and Port of Spain. He worked as a proofreader and in the forestry and agriculture departments in rural Trinidad, experiences that shaped his writing. His novels, including "The Dragon Can't Dance" (1979), about Carnival, the steelband and the people of a Port of Spain hillside, "The Wine of Astonishment" and "Salt", which won the Commonwealth Writers' Prize in 1997, celebrate the culture and resistance of ordinary Trinidadians. He has taught at the University of the West Indies.
C. L. R. James|Trinidadian historian, thinker and cricket writer (1901–1989), author of The Black Jacobins and Beyond a Boundary.|Cyril Lionel Robert James was born in Tunapuna, Trinidad, the son of a schoolteacher, and became a teacher himself and a cricket writer. In 1932 he moved to England, where he reported on cricket for the Manchester Guardian and became a leading Pan-Africanist and Marxist. "The Black Jacobins" (1938) told the story of the Haitian Revolution and Toussaint Louverture. Expelled from the US in 1953 after years there, he returned to Trinidad to work with Eric Williams. "Beyond a Boundary" (1963), about cricket and colonialism, is often called the greatest sports book ever written.
Dionne Brand|Trinidad-born poet and novelist (born 1953) living in Canada, author of A Map to the Door of No Return.|Brand was born in Guayaguayare, Trinidad, and moved to Toronto at 17 to attend university. She became a poet, novelist, essayist and filmmaker, and an activist working with Black and women's communities. Her poetry collections include "Land to Light On" and "Ossuaries", and her novels "In Another Place, Not Here" and "What We All Long For". "A Map to the Door of No Return" is a meditation on the African diaspora. She was Toronto's Poet Laureate and has taught at the University of Guelph.
Monique Roffey|Trinidadian-British novelist (born 1965), author of The Mermaid of Black Conch.|Roffey was born in Port of Spain, Trinidad, and grew up between Trinidad and England. She has written novels and a memoir. "The White Woman on the Green Bicycle" follows a British couple through decades of Trinidad's history. "The Mermaid of Black Conch" (2020), a love story between a fisherman and a cursed mermaid, set on a Caribbean island, won the Costa Book of the Year. She teaches creative writing in England.
Ingrid Persaud|Trinidadian novelist (born 1960s) whose Love After Love captures Trinidad's warmth and talk.|Persaud was born in Trinidad. She trained as a lawyer and taught law in the UK and US, then studied fine art at Goldsmiths and became a visual artist before turning to fiction. Her story "The Sweet Sop" won the Commonwealth Short Story Prize in 2017. Her first novel, "Love After Love" (2020), about an unconventional family in Trinidad, written with the rhythms of Trinidadian speech, won the Costa First Novel Award. Her second novel is "The Lost Love Songs of Boysie Singh".
Daniel Defoe|English novelist and journalist (c. 1660–1731), author of Robinson Crusoe, often called the first English novel.|Daniel Foe, who later added the "De", was the son of a London butcher and a Dissenter, barred from the universities. He became a merchant, went bankrupt, and turned to pamphleteering; for a satire on intolerance he was pilloried, but the crowd threw flowers instead of stones. He worked as a government spy and wrote hundreds of works. At nearly sixty he published "Robinson Crusoe" (1719), inspired partly by the castaway Alexander Selkirk, followed by "Moll Flanders" and "A Journal of the Plague Year". He died in hiding from creditors.
Henry Fielding|English novelist, playwright and magistrate (1707–1754), author of the comic masterpiece Tom Jones.|Fielding was born in Somerset to a gentry family and educated at Eton and Leiden. He wrote satirical plays until the Licensing Act of 1737, provoked partly by his attacks on the prime minister, shut him out of the theatre. He became a lawyer and then a London magistrate, founding the Bow Street Runners, an early police force. His novels "Joseph Andrews" and "Tom Jones" (1749), the story of a good-hearted foundling, are full of comedy, warmth and the narrator's genial essays. He died in Lisbon, seeking a cure for his failing health.
Oliver Goldsmith|Irish novelist, playwright and poet (1728–1774), author of The Vicar of Wakefield, She Stoops to Conquer and The Deserted Village.|Goldsmith was born in Ireland, the son of a poor clergyman, and was scarred by smallpox as a child. He drifted through Trinity College Dublin, studied medicine in Edinburgh and Leiden, and wandered Europe on foot, playing the flute for his supper. In London he worked as a hack writer and became a friend of Samuel Johnson, who sold "The Vicar of Wakefield" for him to save him from debtors' prison. His comedy "She Stoops to Conquer" and poem "The Deserted Village" are still loved. Generous to a fault, he died in debt.
Fanny Burney|English novelist and diarist (1752–1840) whose Evelina pioneered the novel of manners that Jane Austen perfected.|Frances Burney was the daughter of the music historian Charles Burney and grew up among famous visitors such as Johnson and Garrick. Shy and self-taught, she wrote secretly and published "Evelina" (1778) anonymously; its success astonished London. She served unhappily for five years at the court of Queen Charlotte, then married a French exile, General d'Arblay. In 1811 she underwent a mastectomy without anaesthetic and wrote a famous account of it. Her journals and letters are among the liveliest of her age.
William Makepeace Thackeray|English novelist (1811–1863) of Vanity Fair, a satire of ambition and snobbery with the unforgettable Becky Sharp.|Thackeray was born in Calcutta, where his father worked for the East India Company, and sent to England at five after his father's death. He lost most of his inheritance through gambling and bank failures, and supported himself as a journalist and illustrator. His wife Isabella fell into lasting mental illness after the birth of their third child, and he raised their daughters largely alone. "Vanity Fair" (1847–48), "a novel without a hero", made him Dickens's great rival. He also wrote "Pendennis" and "Henry Esmond".
Anthony Trollope|English novelist (1815–1882) of the Barsetshire and Palliser novels, who wrote 47 novels while working for the Post Office.|Trollope had a miserable, poor childhood; his mother, Frances Trollope, became a bestselling writer to keep the family afloat. He joined the Post Office as a junior clerk and found himself in Ireland, where he flourished, and he introduced the red pillar box to Britain. He wrote every morning before work, a set number of words an hour, producing "The Warden", "Barchester Towers", "The Way We Live Now" and many more. His frank "Autobiography", revealing his methods and earnings, damaged his reputation for a time.
Elizabeth Gaskell|English novelist (1810–1865) of Cranford and North and South, who wrote with compassion about industrial Manchester and village life.|Elizabeth Stevenson's mother died when she was a baby and she was raised by an aunt in Knutsford, Cheshire, the model for "Cranford". She married William Gaskell, a Unitarian minister in Manchester, and saw the poverty of the cotton workers. After the death of her baby son she began writing; "Mary Barton" (1848) described the lives of the poor. Dickens published her work in his magazines. She also wrote "North and South", "Wives and Daughters" and a famous biography of her friend Charlotte Brontë.
Wilkie Collins|English novelist (1824–1889), author of The Woman in White and The Moonstone, a founder of the detective and sensation novel.|Collins was the son of a landscape painter and named after his godfather, the painter David Wilkie. He worked in the tea trade and studied law before becoming a close friend and collaborator of Dickens. "The Woman in White" (1860) caused a sensation, and "The Moonstone" (1868), with its Sergeant Cuff, is often called the first full-length detective novel in English. He suffered from gout and became dependent on laudanum. He lived unconventionally with two women and supported both their households.
George Meredith|English novelist and poet (1828–1909) of The Egoist and Modern Love, admired for his wit and psychological insight.|Meredith was the son of a Portsmouth tailor and was educated partly at a Moravian school in Germany. His first wife, a daughter of the novelist Thomas Love Peacock, left him for the painter Henry Wallis, and her death inspired his sonnet sequence "Modern Love". His novels, including "The Ordeal of Richard Feverel" and "The Egoist", are brilliant, difficult comedies of character. He also wrote "An Essay on Comedy" and supported women's equality. In old age at Box Hill he was revered as the grand old man of English letters.
George Gissing|English novelist (1857–1903) of New Grub Street and The Private Papers of Henry Ryecroft, a quiet book about reading, nature and peace.|Gissing was a brilliant scholarship student at Owens College, Manchester, until he was caught stealing to help a young prostitute, whom he later married; he was jailed and expelled. He lived for years in poverty in London, writing novels about struggling writers and the poor, such as "New Grub Street" (1891). Two unhappy marriages followed. Late in life he found companionship with Gabrielle Fleury in France. "The Private Papers of Henry Ryecroft" (1903), the imagined notebooks of a writer who at last finds peace in the countryside, became his most loved book.
H. G. Wells|English writer (1866–1946) of The Time Machine and The War of the Worlds, a founder of science fiction and a campaigner for a better world.|Herbert George Wells was the son of a shopkeeper and a lady's maid in Bromley, Kent. A broken leg at seven confined him to bed, where he discovered books. He worked as a draper's apprentice before winning a scholarship to study science under T. H. Huxley. "The Time Machine" (1895), "The Invisible Man" and "The War of the Worlds" invented much of modern science fiction. He also wrote comic novels, a popular "Outline of History", and argued for world government and human rights.
Edmund Spenser|English poet (c. 1552–1599) of The Faerie Queene, an allegorical epic of knights and virtues, and the love sonnets Amoretti.|Spenser was born in London and studied at Cambridge as a "sizar", a poor student who worked for his keep. He entered the service of powerful men and spent much of his life in Ireland as a colonial official, where he received an estate, Kilcolman. There he wrote "The Faerie Queene", dedicated to Elizabeth I, in which each knight represents a virtue, in the stanza now named after him. His "Amoretti" and "Epithalamion" celebrate his courtship and marriage to Elizabeth Boyle. He is buried in Westminster Abbey near Chaucer.
Christopher Marlowe|English playwright and poet (1564–1593) of Doctor Faustus and Tamburlaine, whose "mighty line" shaped Shakespeare's verse.|Marlowe was the son of a Canterbury shoemaker and won a scholarship to Cambridge, where he may have worked as a government spy. His plays "Tamburlaine", "The Jew of Malta", "Edward II" and "Doctor Faustus", about a scholar who sells his soul for knowledge and power, brought blank verse to new heights. He was accused of atheism. At 29, in a house in Deptford, he was stabbed to death in a quarrel, officially over the bill; the circumstances remain mysterious.
Ben Jonson|English playwright and poet (1572–1637) of Volpone and The Alchemist, Shakespeare's friend and rival.|Jonson's father, a minister, died before he was born, and his stepfather was a bricklayer; Jonson worked at the trade. He fought as a soldier in Flanders, then became an actor and playwright. He killed a fellow actor in a duel and escaped hanging by pleading benefit of clergy. His comedies "Volpone" and "The Alchemist" satirise greed. He wrote court masques, lyric poems such as "To Celia" ("Drink to me only with thine eyes"), and the tribute calling Shakespeare "not of an age, but for all time". He became, in effect, the first Poet Laureate.
Robert Herrick|English poet (1591–1674) of Hesperides, songs of flowers, festivals and love such as "To the Virgins, to Make Much of Time".|Herrick was the son of a London goldsmith who died when Robert was a year old. He was apprenticed as a goldsmith, then studied at Cambridge and moved among Ben Jonson's circle of poets. He became vicar of Dean Prior in rural Devon, which he found dull, though its customs and flowers filled his poems. Expelled by the Puritans in 1647, he published "Hesperides", some 1,400 poems, the next year. After the Restoration he returned to his parish and lived there until his death at 83.
Alexander Pope|English poet (1688–1744) of An Essay on Man and The Rape of the Lock, master of the rhyming couplet.|Pope was born in London to Catholic parents, which barred him from university and public office. Tuberculosis of the spine left him four feet six inches tall and in pain all his life. Largely self-educated, he published "An Essay on Criticism" at 23. His translations of Homer made him financially independent. He wrote satires such as "The Dunciad" and the philosophical "An Essay on Man". At his villa in Twickenham he created a famous garden and grotto. He is the third most quoted writer in English after Shakespeare and Tennyson.
William Cowper|English poet (1731–1800) of The Task and the hymn "God moves in a mysterious way", who wrote of quiet country life.|Cowper's mother died when he was six, and he was bullied at school. Training as a lawyer, he was overwhelmed by terror before an examination for a parliamentary post and suffered the first of several severe depressions. He found refuge with the Unwin family and the evangelical minister John Newton, with whom he wrote the "Olney Hymns". Gardening, keeping tame hares and writing gave him peace. "The Task" (1785), a long poem of everyday rural life, was hugely popular and anticipated Wordsworth.
John Clare|English poet (1793–1864), a farm labourer from Northamptonshire who wrote with unique closeness about birds, fields and village life.|Clare was the son of a farm labourer in Helpston, Northamptonshire, and worked as a ploughboy, gardener and lime-burner. "Poems Descriptive of Rural Life and Scenery" (1820) made him briefly famous as the "Northamptonshire Peasant Poet". He watched the enclosure of the common fields destroy the landscape he loved. Poverty and fading fame led to mental illness, and he spent his last 23 years in asylums, still writing, including "I Am". His precise poems about nests, flowers and seasons are now greatly admired.
Thomas Hood|English poet and humorist (1799–1845) of "I Remember, I Remember" and "The Song of the Shirt", a protest against sweated labour.|Hood was the son of a London bookseller and trained as an engraver. He became a magazine editor and friend of Lamb and De Quincey, famous for his puns and comic verse. Ill health and debt followed him; he lived for years in Germany and Belgium. His serious poems, including "The Song of the Shirt" (1843), about a poor seamstress, which tripled the sales of Punch and moved readers across the country, made him a voice for the poor. He died of tuberculosis at 45.
John Bunyan|English preacher and writer (1628–1688) whose allegory The Pilgrim's Progress was for centuries the most read book in English after the Bible.|Bunyan was a tinker's son from Bedfordshire, with little schooling, who served in the Parliamentary army in the Civil War. After a long spiritual struggle, described in "Grace Abounding", he became a Nonconformist preacher. After the Restoration he refused to stop preaching without a licence and spent twelve years in Bedford jail, making lace to support his family and writing. "The Pilgrim's Progress" (1678) tells of Christian's journey from the City of Destruction through the Slough of Despond and Vanity Fair to the Celestial City.
Izaak Walton|English writer (1593–1683) of The Compleat Angler, a gentle celebration of fishing, friendship and the contemplative life.|Walton was born in Stafford and apprenticed in London as an ironmonger and draper. He was a friend of the poet John Donne, whose parishioner he was, and wrote short biographies of Donne, George Herbert and others. A Royalist, he retired from business during the Civil War. "The Compleat Angler" (1653), a dialogue between a fisherman, a hunter and a falconer walking along the River Lea, mixes practical advice with songs, recipes and praise of a quiet life. It is one of the most reprinted books in English.
Joseph Addison and Richard Steele|The Spectator (1711–1712), the daily essay paper of Joseph Addison (1672–1719) and Richard Steele (1672–1729).|Joseph Addison and Richard Steele met as schoolboys at Charterhouse and remained friends for life. Steele, an Irish soldier and playwright, founded "The Tatler" in 1709; with Addison, a scholar and politician, he launched "The Spectator" in 1711. Its 555 daily numbers, narrated by the silent Mr Spectator and his club, including the country squire Sir Roger de Coverley, brought "philosophy out of closets and libraries" into coffee-houses, with gentle essays on manners, taste, religion and happiness. It shaped the English essay and polite society.
Samuel Pepys|English naval administrator (1633–1703) whose diary records the Restoration, the Great Plague and the Great Fire of London.|Pepys was the son of a London tailor and studied at Cambridge. Through his patron, the Earl of Sandwich, he rose to run the Navy Board and became one of the founders of a professional navy, and later President of the Royal Society. From 1660 to 1669 he kept a diary in shorthand, recording the coronation of Charles II, the plague of 1665, the Great Fire of 1666, music, theatre, food, his marriage and his own failings with total frankness. Failing eyesight made him stop. It was deciphered and published in the 19th century.
Charles Lamb|English essayist (1775–1834) of the Essays of Elia, gentle, witty and melancholy pieces about London, friendship and memory.|Lamb grew up in London's Inner Temple, where his father was a servant, and went to Christ's Hospital, where he met Coleridge. He worked for 33 years as a clerk at the East India Company. In 1796 his sister Mary, in a fit of madness, killed their mother; Charles took responsibility for her care for the rest of his life, and never married. Together they wrote "Tales from Shakespeare" for children. His "Essays of Elia", such as "Dream-Children" and "Old China", are among the most loved in English.
Thomas De Quincey|English essayist (1785–1859) whose Confessions of an English Opium-Eater explored addiction, dreams and the mind.|De Quincey was the son of a Manchester merchant. At 17 he ran away from school and wandered Wales and London in poverty, befriended by a young girl, Ann, whom he lost and never found. At Oxford he began taking opium for pain. He became a disciple of Wordsworth and Coleridge and lived in Dove Cottage after Wordsworth. His "Confessions" (1821) made him famous. He wrote brilliant essays, including "On Murder Considered as One of the Fine Arts", and spent his last decades in Edinburgh.
Thomas Carlyle|Scottish essayist and historian (1795–1881) of Sartor Resartus, The French Revolution and On Heroes, who preached work, duty and wonder.|Carlyle was the son of a stonemason in Ecclefechan and walked eighty miles to Edinburgh University at 13. After losing his faith and suffering years of dyspepsia and doubt, he found new purpose through German literature, especially Goethe. "Sartor Resartus" describes a spiritual crisis moving from the "Everlasting No" to the "Everlasting Yea". When the manuscript of "The French Revolution" was accidentally burned at John Stuart Mill's house, he rewrote it. With his witty wife Jane, he lived in Chelsea, the "Sage of Chelsea".
John Henry Newman|English theologian and writer (1801–1890), leader of the Oxford Movement, author of the Apologia and The Idea of a University.|Newman was the son of a London banker and became a fellow of Oriel College, Oxford, and vicar of the university church, where his sermons drew crowds. He led the Oxford Movement to renew the Church of England, but his studies led him to become a Catholic in 1845, costing him many friendships. His "Apologia Pro Vita Sua" (1864), answering Charles Kingsley's attack, told his spiritual journey with great honesty. He founded a Catholic university in Dublin, wrote "The Idea of a University" and the hymn "Lead, Kindly Light", and was made a cardinal. He was canonised in 2019.
J. M. Synge|Irish playwright (1871–1909) of Riders to the Sea and The Playboy of the Western World, who found his voice on the Aran Islands.|John Millington Synge was born near Dublin to a Protestant family and studied music and languages. In Paris, W. B. Yeats advised him to go to the Aran Islands and "express a life that has never found expression". He spent several summers there, learning Irish and listening to the islanders, recorded in "The Aran Islands". His plays, including the one-act tragedy "Riders to the Sea" and the comedy "The Playboy of the Western World", which provoked riots at the Abbey Theatre in 1907, use the rich speech of rural Ireland. He died of Hodgkin's disease at 37.
Washington Irving|American writer (1783–1859) of "Rip Van Winkle" and "The Legend of Sleepy Hollow", the first American author famous in Europe.|Irving was the youngest of eleven children of a New York merchant and was named after George Washington. He trained as a lawyer but preferred writing; his comic "History of New York" by "Diedrich Knickerbocker" gave New Yorkers a nickname. When the family business failed, he lived in England and wrote "The Sketch-Book" (1819–20), containing "Rip Van Winkle" and "The Legend of Sleepy Hollow". He lived in Spain, writing "Tales of the Alhambra", served as US minister to Spain, and spent his last years at Sunnyside on the Hudson.
James Fenimore Cooper|American novelist (1789–1851) of The Last of the Mohicans and the Leatherstocking Tales of the frontier.|Cooper grew up in Cooperstown, New York, a frontier village founded by his father. Expelled from Yale, he served in the US Navy. He began writing on a bet with his wife that he could write a better novel than the one he was reading. His five Leatherstocking Tales, including "The Last of the Mohicans" (1826) and "The Deerslayer", follow the frontiersman Natty Bumppo and show the vanishing wilderness and the injustice done to Native Americans. He also wrote sea novels and social criticism.
Harriet Beecher Stowe|American writer (1811–1896) whose Uncle Tom's Cabin turned millions against slavery.|Harriet Beecher was one of the many children of the preacher Lyman Beecher; several of her siblings became famous reformers. In Cincinnati, across the river from slave-holding Kentucky, she met fugitives and saw slavery's cruelty. After the death of her baby son Samuel, she said she understood the grief of enslaved mothers whose children were sold. Angered by the Fugitive Slave Act of 1850, she wrote "Uncle Tom's Cabin" (1852), which sold hundreds of thousands of copies in its first year. Lincoln is said to have greeted her as "the little woman who wrote the book that started this great war".
Louisa May Alcott|American novelist (1832–1888) of Little Women, based on her own life with her three sisters.|Alcott was the daughter of the visionary teacher Bronson Alcott, whose idealistic schemes, including a failed utopian farm, kept the family poor. She grew up in Concord among Emerson and Thoreau, worked as a seamstress, governess and servant, and wrote thrillers under a pen name. As a Civil War nurse she caught typhoid, which damaged her health. "Little Women" (1868), the story of the March sisters and the writer Jo, made her rich and famous. She supported women's suffrage and was the first woman to register to vote in Concord.
Ambrose Bierce|American writer (1842–c. 1914) of The Devil's Dictionary and "An Occurrence at Owl Creek Bridge", famous for his biting wit.|Bierce was the tenth of thirteen children of a poor Ohio farming family. He fought for the Union in the Civil War, including at Shiloh, and was seriously wounded; the war shaped his dark stories. He became a feared journalist in San Francisco, nicknamed "Bitter Bierce". His "Devil's Dictionary" defines words with savage irony, and his story "An Occurrence at Owl Creek Bridge" is a classic. Both of his sons died young. In 1913, at 71, he went to Mexico to observe its revolution and vanished without trace.
Stephen Crane|American writer (1871–1900) of The Red Badge of Courage and the spare, startling poems of The Black Riders.|Crane was the fourteenth child of a Methodist minister in New Jersey. He worked as a journalist in the slums of New York, which he portrayed in "Maggie: A Girl of the Streets". "The Red Badge of Courage" (1895), written by a man who had never been to war, was praised by veterans for its truth. His free-verse poems in "The Black Riders" anticipated modern poetry. Shipwrecked off Florida, he survived thirty hours in a dinghy, which became "The Open Boat". He died of tuberculosis at 28.
O. Henry|American short-story writer (1862–1910) of "The Gift of the Magi", famous for his warmth and twist endings.|William Sydney Porter was born in North Carolina; his mother died when he was three. He worked as a pharmacist, ranch hand, draftsman and bank teller in Texas. Accused of embezzling from the bank, he fled to Honduras, but returned when his wife was dying, and served three years in prison, where he began publishing stories as O. Henry. In New York he wrote hundreds of stories about clerks, shopgirls and tramps, including "The Gift of the Magi" and "The Last Leaf". He died at 47.
Phillis Wheatley|West African-born American poet (c. 1753–1784), the first African American to publish a book of poetry.|Kidnapped from West Africa at about seven, she was sold in Boston to the Wheatley family and named after the ship that carried her, the Phillis. The family taught her to read, and she mastered English, Latin and the Bible within a few years. Her "Poems on Various Subjects, Religious and Moral" (1773) was published in London after she had to prove to a panel of Boston worthies that she had written them. She was freed soon after, met George Washington, married, and died in poverty at about 31.
Olaudah Equiano|African writer and abolitionist (c. 1745–1797) whose autobiography helped end the British slave trade.|By his own account, Equiano was born in an Igbo village in what is now Nigeria, kidnapped at about eleven and taken across the Atlantic. He was sold to a British naval officer, renamed Gustavus Vassa, and served at sea, then was sold to a Quaker merchant in Montserrat, from whom he bought his freedom in 1766. He travelled to the Arctic and Central America and settled in London, where he joined the abolition movement. His "Interesting Narrative" (1789) was a bestseller. He married an Englishwoman, Susannah Cullen.
Paul Laurence Dunbar|American poet (1872–1906) of "We Wear the Mask" and "Sympathy", one of the first Black writers to win national fame.|Dunbar was born in Dayton, Ohio, to parents who had been enslaved; his mother loved poetry and his father had escaped to fight in the Civil War. He was the only Black student in his class, where his friends included Orville Wright, who printed his newspaper. Working as an elevator operator, he self-published his first poems. William Dean Howells's praise made him famous. He wrote in both standard English and dialect, novels and lyrics for musicals. He died of tuberculosis at 33.
James Weldon Johnson|American writer and activist (1871–1938) who wrote "Lift Every Voice and Sing" and The Autobiography of an Ex-Colored Man.|Johnson was born in Jacksonville, Florida, and became the first Black lawyer admitted to the Florida bar after Reconstruction. With his brother Rosamond he wrote "Lift Every Voice and Sing" (1900), now called the Black national anthem. He wrote Broadway songs, served as US consul in Venezuela and Nicaragua, and published "The Autobiography of an Ex-Colored Man" (1912) anonymously. As leader of the NAACP he campaigned against lynching. "God's Trombones" turns Black sermons into poetry. He died in a car accident.
Claude McKay|Jamaican-American poet and novelist (1889–1948) of Harlem Shadows and "If We Must Die", a founder of the Harlem Renaissance.|Festus Claudius McKay grew up in the hills of Clarendon, Jamaica, and published two books of poems in Jamaican patois before moving to the United States in 1912. Facing racism, he worked as a waiter and porter while writing. His sonnet "If We Must Die" (1919), written after a summer of racial violence, became a rallying cry. "Harlem Shadows" (1922) helped launch the Harlem Renaissance. He travelled to Russia, France and Morocco, and wrote the novels "Home to Harlem" and "Banjo". Late in life he became a Catholic.
Herodotus|Greek historian (c. 484–425 BC), "the father of history", whose Histories tell of the Persian Wars and the peoples of the ancient world.|Herodotus was born in Halicarnassus, a Greek city on the coast of today's Turkey, then under Persian rule. Exiled after a political struggle, he travelled widely, to Egypt, Babylon, the Black Sea and Italy, gathering stories, customs and legends. His "Histories" explain how the Greeks and Persians came to war, culminating in Marathon, Thermopylae and Salamis, while digressing into Egyptian religion, Scythian nomads and much else. Cicero called him the father of history; critics called him the father of lies. He was the first to make inquiry, historia, into the past.
Xenophon|Athenian soldier, historian and student of Socrates (c. 430–354 BC), author of the Anabasis and memoirs of Socrates.|Xenophon came from a wealthy Athenian family and as a young man followed Socrates. In 401 BC he joined Greek mercenaries serving the Persian prince Cyrus the Younger; when Cyrus was killed and their generals murdered, Xenophon helped lead ten thousand Greeks on a long retreat through hostile lands to the Black Sea, where they cried "Thalassa! Thalassa!" — the sea! — as told in his "Anabasis". Exiled from Athens, he lived on an estate near Olympia, hunting and writing. His "Memorabilia" records Socrates' practical conversations.
Tacitus|Roman historian (c. 56–120), author of the Annals, Histories, Germania and Agricola, a life of his father-in-law.|Publius Cornelius Tacitus rose through the Roman senate to become consul and governor of Asia. He married the daughter of Agricola, the general who conquered much of Britain, and wrote his biography.
