Having lived through the terror of the emperor Domitian, he wrote history to record "without anger or partiality" the abuses of power, in a compressed, ironic style. His "Annals" and "Histories" cover the emperors from Tiberius to Domitian. He gave the British chieftain Calgacus the famous words about Rome making a desert and calling it peace.
Livy|Roman historian (c. 59 BC–AD 17) whose History of Rome told the story of the city from its founding.|Titus Livius was born in Patavium (Padua) and spent most of his life in Rome during the reign of Augustus, who was his friend despite Livy's admiration for the Republic. He devoted some forty years to his "Ab Urbe Condita", a history of Rome in 142 books, of which 35 survive. It tells of Romulus and Remus, Lucretia, Horatius at the bridge and Hannibal crossing the Alps, presenting history as a store of examples of virtue and vice. Machiavelli wrote his "Discourses" on it.
Pindar|Greek lyric poet (c. 518–438 BC) whose odes celebrate victors at the Olympic and other games.|Pindar was born near Thebes into an aristocratic family and trained in Athens. He was commissioned by rulers and noble families across the Greek world to compose choral odes for victors at the Olympian, Pythian, Isthmian and Nemean games. Rather than describe the races, his odes connect the victory to myths, the gods and the fleeting nature of human glory. Their language is dense and dazzling. When Alexander the Great destroyed Thebes, he is said to have spared Pindar's house.
Catullus|Roman poet (c. 84–54 BC) whose love poems to "Lesbia" are among the most personal in Latin.|Gaius Valerius Catullus came from a wealthy family in Verona and moved in Rome's fashionable society. He loved a married noblewoman he called Lesbia, probably Clodia Metelli, and his poems trace the affair from rapture to bitterness. His brother's death in Asia Minor inspired the moving "hail and farewell". He also wrote witty, often obscene epigrams mocking politicians, including Julius Caesar. He died around thirty. His 116 poems survived in a single manuscript found in Verona in the 14th century.
Juvenal|Roman satirist (c. 55–130) whose sixteen Satires attack the vices of imperial Rome and gave us "a sound mind in a sound body".|Little is known of Decimus Junius Juvenalis. He may have come from Aquinum, and his satires suggest years of poverty and dependence on rich patrons in Rome. He wrote with savage indignation about greed, corruption, hypocrisy and the absurdities of city life. His phrases have lasted two thousand years: "bread and circuses", "who will guard the guards?" and "a sound mind in a sound body". Samuel Johnson's "London" and "The Vanity of Human Wishes" are imitations of his satires.
Martial|Roman poet (c. 40–104) who perfected the short, witty epigram in fifteen books.|Marcus Valerius Martialis was born in Bilbilis in Roman Spain and came to Rome around 64, where he lived for over thirty years as a client of wealthy patrons, often short of money. His some 1,500 epigrams sketch Roman life: dinner parties, legacy hunters, bad poets, friendship and the simple pleasures he longed for. Many are satirical or bawdy, but some are tender, such as his epitaph for a little slave girl, Erotion. In old age he returned to Spain.
Alexandre Dumas|French novelist (1802–1870) of The Three Musketeers and The Count of Monte Cristo.|Dumas's grandfather was a French marquis and his grandmother an enslaved woman in Saint-Domingue (Haiti); his father, General Thomas-Alexandre Dumas, was the highest-ranking man of colour in the French army. Raised in poverty after his father's death, Dumas went to Paris and found success with historical plays. With collaborators, especially Auguste Maquet, he produced hundreds of works, including "The Three Musketeers" (1844) and "The Count of Monte Cristo" (1844–46), a tale of wrongful imprisonment and revenge. He spent fortunes as fast as he earned them.
Émile Zola|French novelist (1840–1902) of Germinal and the Rougon-Macquart cycle, and author of "J'accuse".|Zola grew up in Aix-en-Provence, where he was a close friend of Paul Cézanne; his father, an engineer, died when he was six. Poor in Paris, he worked as a clerk at a publishing house. His twenty-novel cycle "Les Rougon-Macquart" studies one family under the Second Empire with almost scientific realism. "Germinal" (1885), about a miners' strike, remains his most powerful book. In 1898 his open letter "J'accuse...!" defended Captain Dreyfus, falsely convicted of treason, and Zola was forced to flee to England. He died of carbon monoxide poisoning from a blocked chimney.
Guy de Maupassant|French writer (1850–1893) of some 300 short stories, including "Boule de Suif" and "The Necklace".|Maupassant grew up in Normandy and fought in the Franco-Prussian War. He worked as a clerk in Paris ministries while being trained in writing by Flaubert, a family friend, who taught him to look at things long enough to see what no one else had seen. "Boule de Suif" (1880) made him famous. In ten years he wrote around 300 stories, six novels and travel books, with clear, ironic realism. He loved rowing on the Seine. Syphilis caused increasing mental illness, and he died in an asylum at 42.
Alessandro Manzoni|Italian novelist and poet (1785–1873) whose The Betrothed is the great novel of Italian literature.|Manzoni was born in Milan, the grandson of the philosopher Cesare Beccaria. After a youth among Parisian freethinkers, he returned to Catholicism. "I promessi sposi" (The Betrothed), set in 17th-century Lombardy, follows two young villagers whose marriage is blocked by a local tyrant, through famine, riots and plague, with deep compassion for ordinary people. He rewrote it in the Florentine language of his day, helping to create modern Italian. Verdi composed his Requiem in Manzoni's memory.
Maxim Gorky|Russian writer (1868–1936) of The Lower Depths and Mother, who rose from poverty to become a voice for the downtrodden.|Alexei Peshkov was orphaned young and grew up with his grandparents in Nizhny Novgorod, working from the age of eight as a shoemaker's errand boy, dishwasher on a steamer and baker's labourer. He tramped across Russia and took the pen name Gorky, "bitter". His stories of tramps and workers made him famous, and his play "The Lower Depths" (1902) was a sensation. He supported the revolution but criticised Lenin's terror, and lived abroad for years. Under Stalin he returned and was honoured, while his independence was curtailed.
Pedro Calderón de la Barca|Spanish playwright (1600–1681) of Life Is a Dream, a master of the Golden Age theatre.|Calderón was born in Madrid and educated by Jesuits and at Salamanca. He wrote plays for the court of Philip IV and fought as a soldier in Catalonia. After his brothers' deaths and a personal crisis, he became a priest in 1651 and afterwards wrote mainly religious dramas, the "autos sacramentales". He wrote some 120 plays. "Life Is a Dream" (1635), about Prince Segismundo, imprisoned since birth because of a prophecy, asks how we should act if life itself may be a dream.
Gotthold Ephraim Lessing|German dramatist and critic (1729–1781) of Nathan the Wise, a plea for religious tolerance.|Lessing was the son of a Lutheran pastor in Saxony. He studied theology and medicine but chose the theatre, working as a critic and dramatist in Berlin, Hamburg and finally as librarian at Wolfenbüttel. His friendship with the Jewish philosopher Moses Mendelssohn inspired "Nathan the Wise" (1779), in which a wise Jewish merchant tells the parable of three rings to show that Judaism, Christianity and Islam should compete only in goodness. His wife and newborn son died in 1778. He is a central figure of the German Enlightenment.
Henryk Sienkiewicz|Polish novelist (1846–1916) of Quo Vadis, who won the Nobel Prize in 1905.|Sienkiewicz was born into an impoverished noble family in Russian-ruled Poland. He worked as a journalist and travelled in America, where he lived for a time in a Polish colony in California. His trilogy of historical novels about 17th-century Poland, "With Fire and Sword", "The Deluge" and "Fire in the Steppe", aimed to "lift up the hearts" of a nation without a state. "Quo Vadis" (1896), about early Christians in Nero's Rome, became a worldwide bestseller. He won the Nobel Prize in 1905.
Karel Čapek|Czech writer (1890–1938) whose play R.U.R. gave the world the word "robot".|Čapek was the son of a country doctor in Bohemia. With his brother Josef, a painter, he wrote plays and stories; it was Josef who suggested the word "robot", from the Czech for forced labour. "R.U.R." (1920), about artificial workers who rebel, was performed worldwide. He also wrote "The War with the Newts", warning against fascism, gentle books on gardening and dogs, and conversations with his friend President Masaryk. He died of pneumonia in 1938, months before the Nazis occupied Prague; Josef died in a concentration camp.
Hans Christian Andersen|Danish writer (1805–1875) of fairy tales such as "The Ugly Duckling", "The Little Mermaid" and "The Snow Queen".|Andersen was the son of a poor shoemaker and a washerwoman in Odense. At 14 he went alone to Copenhagen hoping to become an actor or singer; he failed, but patrons paid for his schooling, which he endured as an awkward, older pupil. He travelled widely and wrote novels, plays and travel books, but his 156 fairy tales made him world-famous. Many draw on his own life, such as the ugly duckling who becomes a swan. He never married, and was often unhappily in love.
Adam Mickiewicz|Polish poet (1798–1855) of Pan Tadeusz, the national epic of Poland.|Mickiewicz was born near Navahrudak in today's Belarus, then part of the Russian Empire, and studied in Vilnius, where he joined a secret patriotic society. Arrested and exiled to Russia, he befriended Pushkin. After the failed uprising of 1830 he lived in exile in Dresden and Paris, where he wrote "Pan Tadeusz" (1834), a nostalgic epic of Lithuanian gentry life on the eve of Napoleon's invasion. He taught Slavic literature at the Collège de France. He died in Constantinople trying to raise a Polish legion.
Selma Lagerlöf|Swedish novelist (1858–1940) of Gösta Berling's Saga and Nils Holgersson, the first woman to win the Nobel Prize in Literature.|Lagerlöf was born at Mårbacka, her family's estate in Värmland, and a hip problem kept her lame from childhood; she grew up on her grandmother's stories. She trained as a teacher. Her first novel, "Gösta Berling's Saga" (1891), about a defrocked priest and the cavaliers of a Värmland manor, drew on local legends. Asked to write a geography reader for schools, she produced "The Wonderful Adventures of Nils", about a boy who flies across Sweden on a goose. In 1909 she won the Nobel Prize, and used the money to buy back Mårbacka.
Alexander Herzen|Russian writer and thinker (1812–1870), "the father of Russian socialism", whose memoirs My Past and Thoughts are a classic.|Herzen was the illegitimate son of a wealthy Moscow nobleman and a German woman. As a teenager he and his friend Ogaryov swore on the Sparrow Hills to devote their lives to freedom. He was exiled for his ideas and left Russia for good in 1847. In London he founded the Free Russian Press and the journal "The Bell", which was read secretly in Russia, even by the Tsar. His wife's affair, the drowning of his mother and son, and the failure of the 1848 revolutions are told in his memoirs.
Arthur Schnitzler|Austrian dramatist and novelist (1862–1931) of fin-de-siècle Vienna, known for his psychological insight.|Schnitzler was the son of a prominent Jewish laryngologist in Vienna and practised as a doctor himself. Freud regarded him as a kind of double, who reached by intuition what he found through research. His plays and stories, such as "La Ronde" (Reigen), "Lieutenant Gustl", one of the first interior monologues, and "Dream Story", explore desire, deceit and death among the Viennese middle class. He was attacked by antisemites and censors. "Casanova's Homecoming" (1918) shows the aging seducer facing his decline.
Torquato Tasso|Italian poet (1544–1595) of Jerusalem Delivered, an epic of the First Crusade.|Tasso was born in Sorrento, the son of the poet Bernardo Tasso, and served at the court of the Este dukes in Ferrara. His epic "Jerusalem Delivered" (1581) mingles crusading battles with love stories, such as Rinaldo and Armida. Anxious about its orthodoxy and suffering from paranoia, he was confined by Duke Alfonso II in the hospital of Sant'Anna for seven years. His sufferings made him a Romantic legend, celebrated by Goethe and Byron. He died in Rome shortly before he was to be crowned with laurel.
Heinrich von Kleist|German dramatist and storyteller (1777–1811) of The Prince of Homburg and Michael Kohlhaas.|Kleist was born into an old Prussian military family and served in the army before leaving to study. Reading Kant plunged him into a crisis about whether truth can be known. He wandered through Europe, wrote plays and novellas of startling intensity, and edited journals, but found little success or recognition in his lifetime. "The Broken Jug" is a classic comedy, "Michael Kohlhaas" the story of a horse dealer whose sense of justice becomes destructive, and "The Prince of Homburg" a drama of duty and the fear of death. He died at 34.
E. T. A. Hoffmann|German writer, composer and judge (1776–1822) of fantastic tales such as "The Sandman" and "The Nutcracker".|Ernst Theodor Wilhelm Hoffmann, who changed "Wilhelm" to "Amadeus" in honour of Mozart, was a Prussian lawyer from Königsberg. After Napoleon's armies cost him his post, he worked as a conductor, composer and music critic, then returned to the law as an upright judge in Berlin while writing at night. His tales, mixing the everyday and the uncanny, include "The Sandman", which Freud analysed, and "The Nutcracker and the Mouse King", basis of Tchaikovsky's ballet. Offenbach's "Tales of Hoffmann" is about him.
